#5949. Trump, Xi, Putin Hold Historic Trilateral Talks

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

World Brief: Trump, Xi, Putin Hold Historic Trilateral Talks

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at the headliners of this year’s Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit, a ruling of state-sponsored genocide in Myanmar, and Anthropic’s record-setting public debut.

A week of high-level diplomacy capped off on Thursday with a trilateral summit featuring U.S. President Donald Trump, Chinese President Xi Jinping, and Russian President Vladimir Putin. But although the meeting, which took place at a sprawling lakeside villa in the Chinese city of Shenzhen, was initially framed as an opportunity to pursue global peacemaking, analysts said it proved to be little more than a photo op.

Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at the headliners of this year’s Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit, a ruling of state-sponsored genocide in Myanmar, and Anthropic’s record-setting public debut.

A week of high-level diplomacy capped off on Thursday with a trilateral summit featuring U.S. President Donald Trump, Chinese President Xi Jinping, and Russian President Vladimir Putin. But although the meeting, which took place at a sprawling lakeside villa in the Chinese city of Shenzhen, was initially framed as an opportunity to pursue global peacemaking, analysts said it proved to be little more than a photo op.

Heading into the dialogue, Trump was clear about what he hoped to accomplish. “The United States, Russia, and China are uniquely positioned to discuss how to reduce the number of nuclear weapons on Earth and also to lessen the risk of those weapons being used,” the U.S. president said at the start of the summit. The three countries hold more than 90 percent of the world’s total nuclear weapons.

Yet the roughly 70-minute meeting concluded with no mention of future nuclear negotiations or a potential arms reduction treaty. Instead, the three leaders released a peace declaration that “reaffirmed that a nuclear war cannot be won and must never be fought.” It was one of just four items listed in the joint statement—and the last one mentioned.

No. 1 on the list was a commitment to reform the United Nations. The United States, China, and Russia are three of the five permanent members on the U.N. Security Council, granting them significant sway over the international body’s actions. Yet all three nations have repeatedly accused the U.N. of bias.

The second item said the three leaders “plan to increase the production and supply of refined petroleum products for the benefit of all nations” and “reaffirmed the importance of stable energy flows.” It did not specify how much additional crude would be provided to help stabilize the market, though the United States has already made efforts to suspend sanctions on Russian oil—to the condemnation of Washington’s Western allies—to ease economic pressures.

Item three concerned the International Criminal Court (ICC) and similar bodies, with the three leaders arguing that these courts “must not abuse their jurisdiction,” particularly against state officials who they claim have immunity.

The Trump administration has sought to dismantle the ICC by questioning its status and imposing sanctions on its members; the court is a “rogue globalist organization,” Trump said on Thursday. Putin has similarly denounced the ICC’s authority, particularly after it issued an arrest warrant for him in 2023 citing alleged war crimes. Moscow denies the accusations.

The trio walked away from Thursday’s meeting with optimistic outlooks. Trump characterized the summit as a “Great and Highly Consequential Trip”; Xi described the talks as “positive, constructive, and fruitful”; and Putin said they were “open and candid.”

However, not everyone shared those sentiments. “It’s a historic embarrassment that people will remember long into the future,” U.S. Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer said on Thursday. “It’s a betrayal of the United States by a president who loves nothing more than to bow down to our adversaries for a photo op.”

Guilty of genocide. The International Court of Justice (ICJ) ruled on Thursday that Myanmar had committed genocide against the country’s minority Rohingya Muslim community beginning in August 2017. This is the first time that the highest U.N. court has found a nation directly responsible for genocide.

“The court finds that Myanmar’s security forces killed thousands of Rohingya civilians, perpetrated widespread acts of sexual violence, as well as acts of extreme violence against children, and attacked fleeing Rohingya as they sought to flee in large numbers,” ICJ President Yuji Iwasawa said in the 9-6 ruling. The 15-judge panel, which included 2026 Nobel Peace Prize winner Navi Pillay, rejected the country’s defense that its so-called “clearance operations” were a legitimate campaign against insurgents.

Although the ICJ does not have an enforcement mechanism, the landmark decision could help determine future reparations for Rohingya survivors as well as prevent the current junta-led state from casting off its diplomatic pariah status; Myanmar’s military regime has recently tried to rebrand itself as a nominally civilian government.

The case, filed by Gambia in 2019, could also impact how the world’s top court handles similar genocide allegations linked to the wars in Gaza and Ukraine. Thursday’s judgment is “a demonstration of international justice & a reaffirmation that impunity for such horrific acts must not stand,” U.N. chief António Guterres wrote on X.

Record IPO. Anthropic made its Wall Street debut on Thursday, and already, interest has exceeded expectations. In its initial public offering on Wednesday, the U.S.-based company behind the artificial intelligence chatbot Claude sold 980 million shares for $83 a pop, raising $83.1 billion. Such a blockbuster showing put Anthropic above SpaceX, the previous debut record-holder, to become the largest stock market opener in history.

On Thursday, the AI company began trading shares for the first time. It opened at $111.50 per share, pushing Anthropic’s market value to roughly $2 trillion.

Since Anthropic was founded in 2021, global interest in AI has skyrocketed, with the United States and China leading the charge. Trump has characterized its advancement as akin to the Industrial Revolution, and Xi has similarly invested tens of billions of dollars in its development. AI has become a growing component of modern warfare, driven global markets, and raised security and environmental concerns.

But as AI has become more powerful, some have begun to warn that the world should pull back. Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei led calls in September to place guardrails on the tech’s advancement after former Anthropic employees warned of security risks. “We must slow the pace at which we improve the capabilities of AI models,” Amodei wrote at the time. “Left unchecked, it could outrun our ability to understand and control these systems.”

Trump’s Greenland win. Danish lawmakers unanimously approved a security deal on Thursday that will allow the United States to strengthen its presence in Greenland and the wider Arctic region. The deal, which expands on a 1951 defense agreement between the United States and Denmark, aims to defuse months of tensions over the Trump administration’s repeated threats to seize the semiautonomous, mineral-rich territory of Greenland.

“We have been through a difficult time, during which many people here at home have been concerned about our country and our future,” Greenlandic Prime Minister Jens-Frederik Nielsen wrote on Facebook on Thursday. “I hope that today’s decision will help bring greater calm and a sense of security.”

First announced in September, the new deal will allow the U.S. military to construct more bases on Greenland, “modernize and expand” its activities at Pituffik Space Base, and set up “defense areas” to bolster its Golden Dome ambitions. In exchange, the territory will remain under Danish control, and Greenland will respect its commitments to the United States if it chooses to seek independence.

One of the most anticipated video game releases is finally here. Grand Theft Auto VI rolled out at midnight on Thursday, starting in New Zealand and moving across time zones from Tokyo to London before finally reaching Miami—the inspiration for the game’s Vice City. More than 19 million copies of the open-world action-adventure game have already been sold, amounting to roughly $1.84 billion. Experts anticipate the game’s nearly 100 missions—in which players inhabit the world of a Bonnie and Clyde-like crime duo—to take around 50 hours to complete, so don’t be concerned if you don’t hear from your gamer friends over the next few days.

Alexandra Sharp is the World Brief writer at Foreign Policy. Bluesky: @alexandrassharp.bsky.social X: @AlexandraSSharp

#5948. China Doesn’t Want a Nuclear Arms Race

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

LYLE J. GOLDSTEIN is Director of the Asia Program at Defense Priorities and Director of the China Initiative at Brown University’s Watson School of International and Public Affairs.

NINA TANNENWALD is Associate Teaching Professor of Political Science at Brown University.

How America Can Avert an Unnecessary Rivalry

Today, pessimism—almost fatalism—reigns in Washington about the United States’ ability to avoid a costly and dangerous arms race with China. Policymakers point to China’s increasingly aggressive policies toward Taiwan, its massive military buildup, and its resistance to engaging in some arms control measures as evidence that Beijing has accepted the inevitability of such a race or is even pursuing one. Worried by intellectual frameworks that emphasize the likelihood of war between rival great powers, many U.S. leaders and analysts see little choice but for the United States to arm itself, too. But this thinking risks becoming a self-fulling prophecy. The major danger is that an unrestrained arms race increases the likelihood of war over Taiwan or even of a catastrophic nuclear exchange.

But the pessimism about averting an arms race is misplaced. A nuclear arms race, in particular, is not a foregone conclusion—and the United States has real opportunities to curb one. Avoiding a nuclear arms race will require a broader approach than the two-pronged one Washington has recently favored: building up U.S. nuclear deterrence and seeking to force concessions from Beijing by imposing restrictions on trade and technology transfers. American leaders should instead look beyond traditional deterrence, which falls short because its threat-based approach tends to provoke competition over arms, and strive to establish a more fundamental strategic stability through a process of reciprocal de-escalation and compromise. China is likely to be amenable to reciprocal de-escalation: it has a long tradition of moderation in nuclear matters and, ultimately, Chinese leaders do not want a war over Taiwan.

There are recent signs that even U.S. President Donald Trump, who has often boasted about his distaste for compromise, sees the value of engagement. After two U.S.-Chinese bilateral summits this year, in October he suggested that his November trilateral meeting in Shenzhen with Chinese leader Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin should focus on “denuclearization.” But Washington will have to become much more consistent in its rejection of an arms race, including by modifying its support for Taiwan and incorporating China into global arms control leadership. A nuclear arms race with China becomes inevitable only if Washington continues to believe it is.

China is undoubtedly pursuing a military buildup, including investments in nuclear weapons. Between 2020 and 2025, China doubled the number of its nuclear warheads, from 300 to 600—a startling increase, although its arsenal remains small compared with the United States’ approximately 3,700 warheads. In 2021, researchers at the Federation of American Scientists announced that China was constructing 350 new nuclear missile silos in three vast sites in Xinjiang and Gansu provinces. Beijing is also pursuing substantial qualitative upgrades to its nuclear weapons, a push it showcased at an October 2025 military parade that unveiled a new sea-launched ballistic missile, two new kinds of intercontinental ballistic missiles, a new air-launched hypersonic missile, and a new long-range hypersonic cruise missile.

Then, this July, the Chinese navy tested a submarine-launched long-range ballistic missile that could carry nuclear weapons. This significant new capability will allow China to launch long-range nuclear strikes that can reach the United States from protected waters near its own coastline rather than having to sail out into the Pacific. A recent editorial in a leading Chinese foreign affairs newspaper took the candid yet disturbing step of explicitly linking China’s rapid nuclear buildup to its desire to exert sovereignty over Taiwan. “The great cause of national reunification,” it asserted, “must rest on a solid, realistic foundation. That foundation is, first and foremost, hard power.” Although such overt nuclear saber rattling remains rare, the link between Beijing’s nuclear forces and the Taiwan issue stretches back over three decades and is also supported by the scholar Tong Zhao’s detailed analysis of China’s nuclear buildup in Foreign Affairs in May.

As China builds up its military, the perception that it is on a collision course with the United States over Taiwan—one that inevitably includes the risk of nuclear escalation—has become widespread in Washington. Taiwan, of course, appears to be an obvious flash point. The United States has long committed itself informally to defending the island while China seeks to unify with it, by force if necessary. In October, Army General Ronald Clark, the head of the U.S. Pacific Command, affirmed that his forces are actively readying for a Chinese invasion of Taiwan that could come as early as 2027.

The Biden administration and both Trump administrations made some attempts to engage China over arms control and nuclear risk reduction. Trump proposed trilateral arms control talks that would include Russia, whereas the Biden administration pursued bilateral talks with China on strategic stability and nuclear risk reduction. But both presidents gave up after China consistently rejected engaging in sustained talks on nuclear matters. Beijing’s stance is that it has a small nuclear arsenal compared with the United States and Russia and that it is important to first establish trust and mutual reassurance about core interests.

Beyond China’s resistance to arms control, theories about power that are trendy in Washington reinforce the collision course mentality. Numerous realist scholars argue that a U.S.-Chinese rivalry and even a great-power war may be structurally difficult to avoid. The political scientist Graham Allison captured this pessimism in his influential 2017 book, Destined for War, which argued that a so-called Thucydides trap—when a rapidly rising power challenges the position of the dominant power, yielding war—would doom the United States and China to conflict.

China hawks, such as Undersecretary of Defense for Policy Elbridge Colby, increasingly advocate an aggressive focus on “preparing for World War III” with China as a way to avoid it. In a July New York Times interview, Colby argued that the United States must “deny” China “the ability to seize and hold the key territory” of Taiwan, by enhancing U.S. deterrence with a massive military buildup. The next month, speaking at a military conference, Colby specified that such a buildup ought to put a greater emphasis on tactical nuclear weapons and the ability to fight a so-called limited nuclear war. The enemy, Colby said, has “got to believe there’s some sense in which we would use” nuclear weapons. Toward this end, the United States is building a new intercontinental ballistic missile, a new air-launched cruise missile, and new nuclear-armed submarines and bombers, and accelerating the ambitious Golden Dome homeland missile defense project. Unlike earlier U.S. missile defenses, which were designed to intercept limited strikes by so called rogue states such as Iran and North Korea, the Golden Dome is intended to defend against massive Chinese or Russian attacks. But the prospect of losing its ability to retaliate against a U.S. first strike only gives Beijing more incentive to expand its nuclear arsenal.

Such a nuclear-heavy approach only prevents a war with China if nuclear deterrence succeeds. That puts a lot of eggs in the deterrence basket. And recent wars suggest that nuclear deterrence is becoming less effective. Russia’s nuclear threats have not coerced Ukraine into halting its strikes deep into Russian territory. Iran has proved willing to launch strikes against nuclear-armed Israel as well as U.S. bases and allies in the Middle East. What is most ominous, in May 2025, India and Pakistan crossed previous redlines and attacked each other’s homelands, something that mutual nuclear deterrence was supposed to prevent. Even if neither the United States nor China want nuclear war, a China determined to unify with Taiwan may not be intimidated by U.S. nuclear threats.

A recent study by Brown University’s Costs of War program found that the United States has already spent more than $3.4 trillion on its militarized rivalry with China over the last decade. Sustaining or ramping up this spending—which encompasses new warships, aircraft, long-range missiles, drones, base upgrades, forward deployments, large military exercises, research and development, and new nuclear weapons—is infeasible in an era of ballooning national debt and expanding domestic needs.

One of the tragedies of an arms race is that neither party may actually want to pursue one. Despite its current arms buildup, Beijing has a long record of moderation in military and nuclear matters. For many decades, it has maintained a minimal nuclear deterrent and a no-first-use policy, and it did not use major military force to achieve strategic goals. Although it has long had access to the relevant technologies, China appears not to have produced or deployed tactical nuclear weapons. Chinese diplomats played a substantial role in coaxing Iran to accept the Obama administration’s 2015 nuclear deal and supported the UN’s relatively stringent sanctions on North Korea. And in the fall of 2022, according to then Secretary of State Antony Blinken and reporting by the Financial Times, China likely intervened diplomatically with the Kremlin to help stop Russia from using nuclear weapons in Ukraine. More broadly, China has embraced international law when it comes to war, playing a constructive role, for example, in removing chemical weapons from Syria in 2013.

Beijing’s inclination toward restraint has not disappeared completely, even as it upgrades its military. In November 2023, U.S. President Joe Biden and Xi agreed to renew lines of communication between their countries’ militaries that had been severed after U.S. House Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taiwan the previous year. A year later, the two leaders affirmed that humans, not AI, must control the decision to use nuclear weapons. And in late 2025, Beijing released a white paper emphatically reiterating its support for nuclear arms control and has moved toward more transparency by publishing more information, including in English, about its military upgrades.

Chinese leaders clearly do not want a war over Taiwan. Publicly, they repeatedly emphasize that their goal is “peaceful unification” and “winning without fighting,” invoking the Chinese strategist Sun-tzu. Beijing’s longtime “one country, two systems” policy toward Taiwan—which would bring the island under Chinese sovereignty while allowing it substantial autonomy—leaves room for a negotiated solution. Last year, in an important analysis of China’s strategic ambitions, the scholars David Kang, Jackie Wong, and Zenobia Chan examined 12,000 articles and hundreds of speeches by Xi to argue that Beijing prioritizes regime stability and internal development over displacing the United States as a global military hegemon. Although China’s major, unannounced exercises around Taiwan earlier this month may constitute an exception, overall, Chinese military exercises near Taiwan over the last five years are consistent with this interpretation: many have reacted to perceived provocations, illustrate only medium-scale capabilities, and have generally had a performative quality, accompanied by extensive press coverage.

There is still an opening for the United States and China to de-escalate. To achieve strategic stability, the two countries will need to make a concerted effort to defuse their security dilemma—the dynamic in which each side perceives the other’s defensive measures as aggressive. First, U.S. and Chinese leaders need to revive superpower summitry. Biden held only three face-to-face meetings with Xi on the sidelines of regional economic meetings. Although the May 2026 Beijing summit between Trump and Xi yielded no major breakthroughs, the meeting did improve the two leaders’ personal relationship; today, U.S.-Chinese tensions are lower than they were between 2021 and 2025. This progress suggests the great value of holding a regular, annual, multiday bilateral summit with a significant focus on nuclear arms control. Such meetings would give expert working groups time to methodically develop measures to reduce the risk of conflict. Just as important, the summits need to be long enough to leave room for unscripted conversations. At their first summit, in Geneva in November 1985, U.S. President Ronald Reagan and Soviet leader Mikhail Gorbachev famously broke away from anxious bureaucrats and advisers for an impromptu walk—an encounter that helped establish a more constructive relationship.

The United States must also take concrete steps to reduce the chance of escalation in the Taiwan Strait. Above all, it should adhere more consistently to its “one China” policy—its position that Beijing is the sole legal government of China. More consistency would include limiting U.S. legislators’ trips to the island and downgrading U.S.-Taiwanese defense relations by, for example, holding meetings less frequently and at lower diplomatic levels. Washington should remind key allies such as Japan to proceed cautiously in terms of their rhetoric and military deployments. Such a restraint-oriented strategy would make it clear to Taiwan that independence is off the table, encouraging it to take a more accommodating approach that would lower the temperature with Beijing. If Washington were to agree to common-sense limitations on arms sales to Taiwan—such as refusing to sell Taiwan the surface-to-surface ballistic Army Tactical Missile System (ATACMS), which can reach across the strait to the Chinese mainland—Beijing would likely reciprocate by scaling back its military exercises and related military deployments around the island. There are real incentives for Beijing to de-escalate. Doing so could improve relations with neighbors such as Japan, free up focus for domestic priorities, lower military spending, and limit the chance of war.

The Pentagon needs to demonstrate Washington’s intent to avoid war over Taiwan by gradually decreasing U.S. warship transits through the Taiwan Strait. Even more important, Washington should also substantially reduce U.S. military deployments in the Luzon Strait between Taiwan and the Philippines and around Japan’s Ryukyu Islands. Such deployments only exacerbate the security dilemma.

Exhibiting restraint does not mean the United States would be giving Taiwan away. The United States can continue to provide Taiwan with defensive arms (such as missile interceptors, artillery, drones, and mines) and harden U.S. bases in the region to provide stabilizing deterrence. But even if U.S. leaders publicly abjure direct military intervention to defend Taiwan—a rational step that would lower the risk of nuclear war—China will prefer negotiating with Taipei on the island’s autonomy over a bloody military showdown with Taiwanese forces. For their part, Taiwan’s leaders will likely accommodate themselves to limited self-governance under Chinese overall sovereignty. In the end, China’s main goal is to get Taiwan out of the U.S. sphere of influence. Chinese leaders’ major fear is not so much Taiwanese autonomy as the prospect of the island serving as a U.S. forward base—in Chinese parlance, “using Taiwan to contain China.”

Another important step would be to seek a multilateral pledge by all nuclear-armed states to employ nuclear weapons only in retaliation against a nuclear attack. Such a policy would reduce the risk of miscalculation during a crisis. China has long embraced such a no-first-use policy, but U.S. leaders have traditionally opposed it, believing that it might expose U.S. allies to conventional attack. The U.S. nuclear deterrent for allies, however, increasingly lacks credibility because the costs of starting a nuclear war would vastly outweigh the benefits. And after the collapse of traditional nuclear arms control, a mutual no-first-use policy offers a promising area of common ground between the United States and China. It would reinforce the existing no-first-use policies of China and India and implicitly acknowledge their leadership in the area of nuclear restraint, a virtue when newer nuclear states are chafing under the old Cold War superpowers’ dominance of arms control. This approach could even entice Beijing into a wider, more comprehensive process of bilateral or multilateral nuclear arms control.

Beyond these three major initiatives, the United States and China should sign an agreement to notify each other about their ballistic missile tests. Washington could also consider giving Beijing a larger role in nonproliferation efforts, for instance by affording China leadership roles in negotiations and inspection regimes in future nonproliferation arrangements. The U.S. arms control agenda with China should focus not only on offensive nuclear systems but also on missile defenses and tactical nuclear weapons, as well as AI technologies. And it would help if the United States evolved its own nuclear posture in the direction of restraint by reducing its warheads, warfighting capabilities, and alert posture.

Ultimately, the United States needs to get serious about reining in an arms race with China before its perception that one is apace becomes a self-fulfilling prophecy. The two countries have a mutual interest in restraint. If Washington continues to overlook Beijing’s incentives to compromise—and the real signs that it is willing to do so—an arms race that, as of now, is mostly putative will become a reality. And a catastrophic war that could have been avoided could indeed become inevitable.

#5946. Lawmakers warn Americans of vulnerable systems amid streak of outages: 'Something is going on'

Members of Congress from both parties warn that America's interconnected banking, aviation and communications systems face serious vulnerabilities. (Credit: Nicholas Ballasy for Fox News Digital)

Lawmakers are sounding the alarm over security threats in America's critical infrastructure following a 10-day wave of tech outages across major industries, warning about cybersecurity and the resilience of the nation's increasingly interconnected, vulnerable systems.

"I will tell you from my dealing back in the intelligence world in the Pentagon, this is not normal," Rep. Keith Self, R-Texas, said. "Ask the questions."

"Cybersecurity is a serious issue, and threats are becoming more serious," Sen. Jon Ossoff, D-Ga., said.

TIMELINE: AMERICA HITS A TECH WALL AS OUTAGES CRIPPLE EMAIL, GROUND FLIGHTS AND DISRUPT MAJOR BANKS

Sen. Jon Ossoff, D-Ga., during a Senate Intelligence Committee hearing on  worldwide threats on Capitol Hill in Washington, D.C., March 25, 2025. (Maansi Srivastava/Washington Post via Getty Images)

"These systems are complex," Rep. Pete Ricketts, R-Neb., said. "I used to run a company that had technology. It's not unusual."

The lawmakers' concerns come as the nation continues to experience tech outages, including at the National Security Agency, and grapples with whether the recent disruptions were isolated technical failures or signs of a broader cybersecurity threat.

President Donald Trump's administration says the incidents were unrelated and were not caused by malicious actors. But speculation continues to increase, particularly after Trump fired National Cyber Director Sean Cairncross Thursday over concerns he lacked the proper credentials in cybersecurity for the job.

"The question has got to be asked, ‘Is there an offensive cyberattack going on that we're not going to hear about?’" Self said. "Because I don't believe any of the public statements that are made, because something is going on behind the scenes."

RECENT SERVICE OUTAGES NOT STATE-SPONSORED ATTACKS, DNI JAY CLAYTON TESTIFIES

Officials say that as artificial intelligence gets better at spotting holes in a system's security, those systems are coming under more strain as companies try to patch those holes.

Regardless of what caused the disruptions, lawmakers warned that the incidents underscore a broader concern: America's reliance on interconnected technology has created potential vulnerabilities across critical infrastructure, from local water systems and power grids to banking and aviation.

"The localized municipal-based infrastructure in America is a soft target," Sen. Tim Sheehy, R-Mont., told Fox News Digital. "And that's been flagged for decades. We haven't addressed it fully. And I think over the past week, we've seen it's time we get serious about it."

Jon Ossoff, left, and John Fetterman expressed concerns about the outages. (Megan Varner/Getty Images; Anna Moneymaker/Getty Images)

$1M-A-DAY CHINA TECH CRACKDOWN PROPOSED AMID CASCADE OF US CYBER DISRUPTIONS

"Everything's interconnected now, so we've got to be working on security all the time," Sen. John Hoeven, R-N.D., said.

"Cybersecurity is a big deal, and it's not just in government, but in business as well, and that's what we're seeing. There's been a number of these incidents around the country now and, obviously, people are concerned about it and we should be."

Sen. John Fetterman, D-Pa., encouraged keeping conversation around cyber threats and developing U.S. systems to reduce vulnerability as a bipartisan issue.

"If they're interconnected or random, no one really knows because they're putting out a lot of misinformation," Fetterman said. "So, we really need to strip out the politics and find out really what it is."

#5944. The U.S. and Iran Are Back to Trading Strikes

Situation Report: The U.S. and Iran Are Back to Trading Strikes

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Welcome back to Foreign Policy’s Situation Report. We sincerely thank all of you for reading this week’s edition when you could be playing Grand Theft Auto VI. As a quick note, SitRep will be off next week for the U.S. Thanksgiving holiday.

Alright, here’s what’s on tap for the day: Iran and the United States engage in a new tit-for-tat, Pete Hegseth makes an unexpected visit to Venezuela, and Donald Trump meets with Xi Jinping and Vladimir Putin in China.

Welcome back to Foreign Policy’s Situation Report. We sincerely thank all of you for reading this week’s edition when you could be playing Grand Theft Auto VI. As a quick note, SitRep will be off next week for the U.S. Thanksgiving holiday.

Alright, here’s what’s on tap for the day: Iran and the United States engage in a new tit-for-tat, Pete Hegseth makes an unexpected visit to Venezuela, and Donald Trump meets with Xi Jinping and Vladimir Putin in China.

The Iran war appears to be heating up (again), as diplomatic talks have hit a wall (again). The United States and Iran resumed strikes over the past week, ending a roughly two-month lull in the conflict.

The renewed fighting has come as Iran continues to attack shipping in the Strait of Hormuz and amid an ongoing U.S. blockade on Iranian oil exports.

Tit-for-tat. Over the past six days, Iran launched a series of strikes on U.S. assets in Bahrain, which hosts the U.S. Navy’s 5th Fleet, and an air base in Kuwait that hosts U.S. forces.

Iran targeted a U.S. early warning radar facility in Bahrain on Friday and took aim at a radar system at a helicopter base in the tiny island nation on Sunday. No damage was reported. Tehran said the attacks were in retaliation for U.S. strikes on Iranian communications towers as well as ongoing drone surveillance operations.

On Tuesday, Iran targeted another radar facility in Bahrain with drones, which were reportedly intercepted; the United States retaliated with a strike on an Iranian missile launch site on Qeshm Island. Iran responded on Wednesday with an attack on Ali Al Salem air base in Kuwait. There were no reports of damage. Bahrain also said it intercepted Iranian aerial attacks on Wednesday.

Under pressure. U.S. President Donald Trump has been vague about his plans in the war, and some reports suggest he is mulling over ordering expansive strikes. But Trump on Thursday indicated that he’s not in a hurry to escalate. “I am not rushing into any decision. We will see what happens after the beginning of the year,” Trump told Axios.

Trump’s comments came as Israel is reportedly pressing the White House to escalate with a fresh bombing campaign. Roman Gofman, the director of the Israeli intelligence agency Mossad, visited Washington this week, where he is said to have expressed concerns to U.S. officials over alleged Iranian chemical weapons development.

The United States also has intelligence that Iran has drawn up plans to potentially use a dirty bomb or chemical weapon in Washington, according to a CNN report. Trump on Tuesday said the United States was looking into the dirty bomb report and takes it “very seriously.” But he later downplayed the prospect of Iran actually using such a weapon in Washington, and he warned that “if it does happen, we’ll wipe them out.” Trump this week also told Fox News there’s “indication that Iran is looking at gasses, looking at bio, because we’ve shut them out with the nuclear.”

“And they’re looking at gas and biological weapons. Not nice. And we’ll see,” he said.

Expired. If Trump does decide to ramp up the war, experts have warned that it would be a huge gamble and would undoubtedly lead to further retaliatory attacks on U.S. forces and assets in the region. The U.S. intelligence community reportedly assesses that Iran has a stockpile of up to 2,500 missiles in its arsenal, despite prior claims from the Trump administration of such capabilities being destroyed. Iran also remains defiant in the face of continued U.S. economic pressure, and Tehran appears emboldened by major Republican losses in the midterm elections.

The United States still has significant military assets in the region, including two aircraft carriers (after yet another reshuffling that saw the USS George Washington leave the Middle East this week, following the arrival of the USS Theodore Roosevelt). But the United States is also facing significant constraints, and the war has depleted stockpiles of key munitions. Due to such shortages, acting U.S. Navy Secretary Hung Cao this week told senators that U.S. forces resorted to firing expired munitions in the war.

U.S. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth made a surprise trip to Venezuela, where he signed a defense cooperation agreement with the country that the United States invaded in January. The Defense Department did not share details of the trip with the U.S. press, with the main images and details coming from Venezuelan state media.

Meanwhile, Politico reported that Hegseth’s personal lawyer, Tim Parlatore, also works for the son of a Libyan warlord who has been accused of war crimes.

The Pentagon nominated Gen. Christopher LaNeve to be permanent chief of staff of the U.S. Army, a role that he has been carrying out in an acting capacity since the ouster of Gen. Randy George in April.

Democrats on Wednesday officially nominated Hakeem Jeffries to be speaker of the House, making him the first black person to serve in that role should he be confirmed.

What should be high on your radar, if it isn’t already.

Rohingya genocide verdict. The International Court of Justice ruled on Thursday that Myanmar committed a genocide against its Rohingya ethnic minority population, the first time that the court has found a state responsible for genocide. The judgment is expected to set a number of international legal precedents, including those concerning standards of evidence and reparations, as journalist Andrew Nachemson wrote in FP.

Trump’s big trilateral. Trump capped off his Asia visit this week by meeting with Chinese President Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin in Shenzhen, China. In a joint “peace declaration” following their meeting, the three leaders committed to “reform” the United Nations, vowed to increase the supply of petroleum products “for the benefit of all nations,” and warned that the International Criminal Court and other similar entities “must not abuse their jurisdiction.” They also agreed to “conduct a dialogue” on nuclear risk and nonproliferation, saying that “a nuclear war cannot be won and must never be fought.”

Huang’s defenders. It’s been one week since Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigned amid a scandal over multiple extramarital affairs, but some of the United States’ most powerful individuals—including Trump—are calling for his return to the helm of the $4 trillion semiconductor company.

A petition to “Bring Jensen Back” has received more than 200,000 signatures, including from the world’s richest man, Elon Musk. Trump said he “would not mind” if Huang came back, and Huang himself fueled the fire with a lengthy post on X in which he wrote: “If the Board of Directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO.”

The board, however, appears uninterested. Nvidia chairman Harvey Jones said during the company’s quarterly earnings call on Tuesday that the board “unanimously accepted his [Huang’s] resignation and does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO.”

The scrutiny of Huang and Nvidia will continue in the meantime. Huang is scheduled to testify before Congress on Dec. 1, and the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission is reportedly investigating the company.

In a photo provided by the North Korean government on Nov. 17, leader Kim Jong Un inspects a tourist zone under development during a visit to Samjiyon City, North Korea.Korean Central News Agency/Korea News Service via AP

Friday, Nov. 20: The European Union hosts the EU-United Kingdom summit in Brussels.

Wednesday, Nov. 25: The EU-Western Balkans summit takes place in Brussels.

Saturday, Nov. 28: Taiwan is set to hold local elections.

Sunday, Nov. 29: Spain will hold snap parliamentary elections

Monday, Nov. 30: The International Criminal Court trial of former Philippine President Rodrigo Duterte is set to begin.

“It’s life. When you’re big and powerful and have all the money, things like that happen. It’s fine.”

—Trump, when asked if he was worried about the series of major software outages this month. (Read John’s smart explainer on the tech outages here.)

Would you commit to your current job for two to six more years if it meant getting a four-day weekend to play the hottest new video game on the market? That’s exactly what 43 soldiers in the U.S. military’s 9th Brigade Engineer Battalion did, signing up for an incentive program that gave them a couple of extra days off to play the long-awaited Grand Theft Auto VI video game that was released on Thursday. In exchange, the soldiers will reenlist in the military for a few more years.

It’s not just about the video game—many of them had other reasons to reenlist anyway, and some also got hefty cash bonuses—but one of the soldiers told Task and Purpose that he now had “a good 24 hours to waste on it.”

#5943. Why Israel Is Losing America

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

ANDREW P. MILLER is a Senior Fellow in National Security and International Policy at the Center for American Progress. He was Deputy Assistant Secretary of State for Israeli-Palestinian Affairs in the Biden administration and Director for Egypt and Israel Military Issues on the National Security Council in the Obama administration.

Only a Radical Course Change Can Win Back Its Most Important Ally

American politicians have woken up to the fact that the public has soured on Israel. On November 3, as Americans headed to the polls, U.S. President Donald Trump made the surprising announcement that the United States would stop sending aid to Israel, valued at around $4 billion annually, by the end of the year. That night, Democrats, many of whom are increasingly outspoken in their criticism of Israeli actions, swept the House and Senate.

As I wrote in Foreign Affairs a year ago, the “exceptional relationship” between the United States and Israel, in which Israel is treated differently than any other U.S. ally or partner, is coming to an end. No other country receives the tremendous latitude and unconditional backing that the United States extends to Israel. In addition to directly hurting Palestinians, the United States’ support for Israel incentivizes reckless Israeli behavior and dubious strategic thinking. The consequences of the blank check are clear. Over the past three years, tens of thousands of Gazans have been killed, progress toward peace and reconstruction in the Gaza Strip has stalled, the annexation of the West Bank proceeds at an unprecedented rate, and the United States is stuck in an unpopular war in the Middle East with seemingly no end. Israel, for its part, is no safer than on October 6, 2023, and unquestionably more isolated.

Things are about to change. For better or worse, the United States will recalibrate its relationship with Israel over the coming years and a new paradigm for the bilateral relationship will emerge—one in which Israel is expected to comply with U.S. and international law, the two countries refrain from interfering in each other’s domestic politics, and the United States limits its support when Israeli goals conflict with its own.

This change is inevitable, and there is little that Israel and its supporters can do to stop it. But Israel can help keep the United States onside by making itself a reliable partner. As it begins what could be a drawn-out government formation process after last month’s elections, Israel can opt to change direction and plot a course that will not further alienate its closest ally.

Whoever leads the next Israeli government can begin rebuilding trust with the United States before a new U.S. president takes office, in 2029. It won’t be easy. Just as Americans have grown critical of Israel over the past three years, Israelis have shifted further to the right. Israel’s next leaders will need the courage to curb the country’s expansionist policies and resurrect the much-maligned two-state solution. But if they fail to overhaul their approach to Palestinians and the wider region, Israel risks losing its biggest champion and defender—perhaps for good.

Trump, who seems to delight in bullying U.S. allies and partners, has maintained the United States’ long-standing exceptional approach to Israel. He joined Israel in launching strikes on Iran in June 2025 and a larger war in February 2026, embarking for the first time on a truly joint military operation with the Jewish state. Like other presidents before him, Trump has provided Israel with bombs, missiles, and aircraft, largely with no strings attached. He and Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu have also interfered in each other’s political systems to an unprecedented degree by endorsing candidates.

Time and again, Trump has deferred to Israel even when such support has run against American interests. The 2026 war on Iran is the most blatant example. Netanyahu persuaded Trump to give up nuclear talks with Iran and join a war that has failed in its objectives and upended energy markets. Even where the Trump administration has staked out U.S. priorities, it has largely refrained from trying to compel Israel to adhere to them. Israel, for example, has obstructed Trump’s peace plan for Gaza, which is meant to restore governance to the Palestinians, disarm Hamas, and lead to a permanent Israeli withdrawal from the territory. Israel has undermined the plan by expanding its military footprint in Gaza beyond the agreed demarcation lines and denying entry to the National Committee for the Administration of Gaza (NCAG), an interim technocratic body that is intended to govern the territory under Trump’s plan and is currently marooned in Egypt. But the Trump administration has barely sounded a single note of disapproval, reinforcing Netanyahu’s long-standing view that he can defy the United States at little cost.

Moreover, it remains to be seen whether Trump will follow through on his pledge to cut military aid to Israel. The timing of his statement suggests it could have been an electoral stunt, and he said himself that the announcement would deprive the left of a talking point. One possibility is that the United States continues gifting Israel weapons to the tune of billions, just under a different guise. The National Defense Authorization Act for fiscal year 2027, which passed the House this summer and could pass the Senate before Democrats take control of both chambers of Congress this coming January, contains a provision that would expand the Pentagon’s financial and material assistance to Israel.

Trump has made no effort to bring U.S. military assistance or transfers to Israel in line with what it provides to other countries. For decades, the United States has been reluctant to subject arms sales to Israel to U.S. regulations or condition them on Israeli behavior. All recent presidents, for example, have effectively exempted Israel from so-called Leahy laws, which prohibit U.S. assistance to foreign military units credibly implicated in human-rights violations. Despite rampant settler terrorism in the West Bank, in many cases facilitated by the Israeli military, the Trump administration has not indicated that it would declare any Israeli units ineligible to receive U.S. weapons.

Trump has also intervened in Israeli politics more than any other U.S. leader. He has hectored Israeli President Isaac Herzog to pardon Netanyahu so that the prime minister can escape the consequences of an ongoing corruption trial. Ahead of October’s election in Israel, Trump offered Netanyahu a “complete and total endorsement” in his bid for another term. Such blatant interference in Israel’s domestic politics not only undermines the rule of law and integrity of Israeli elections but also serves to normalize Netanyahu’s repeated interventions in U.S. domestic politics. True to form, the Israeli prime minister has praised Trump as “by far the greatest friend Israel has ever had or will have in the White House” just two weeks before the U.S. midterm elections, effectively endorsing Republican candidates for Congress. As in other areas of the relationship, Trump has made what is already an unhealthy dynamic worse.

Unconditional support from the United States has hurt Israel in a crucial way: it indulges the worst instincts of Israeli leaders and does nothing to dissuade them from their embrace of a near-perpetual state of war. After Hamas’s attack three years ago, Israel has bombed Gaza, Iran, Lebanon, Syria, Yemen, and even Qatar. Although Israel has dealt blows to its adversaries, there is no evidence that these wars have made Israel any safer. Iran and its proxies have been weakened, but in the course of fighting, Tehran has discovered the extent of its leverage over the Strait of Hormuz and the global economy and appears even more interested in developing a nuclear weapon. In Lebanon, Hezbollah still poses a threat and is devising new ways of striking Israeli territory. Although confined to a smaller portion of Gaza, Hamas is reconstituting its capabilities and quashing any internal Palestinian challenge to its rule. And in the West Bank, runaway settler violence, the proliferation of illegal Israeli outposts, and the growing threat of terrorism or even a Palestinian uprising have pushed the territory to the breaking point.

Israel has also drifted ever closer to pariah status, isolated internationally thanks to its aggressive tactics. Even the country’s closest historical allies have imposed punitive measures that would have been unthinkable just five years ago. Canada, France, and the United Kingdom, for example, announced wide-ranging sanctions against Israeli settlements in the West Bank in September. Such policies are no longer only the purview of traditional critics of Israel, such as Indonesia and Ireland.

Israel’s reputation within the United States has also tanked. According to Pew polling, 60 percent of Americans have an unfavorable view of Israel, up from 42 percent in 2022. So far, Trump has stopped Congress from restricting support for Israel—both through threatening vetoes and applying pressure on congressional Republicans—but he cannot hold back the tide of American public opinion indefinitely. Once it breaks, Israelis will have to face more than criticism from its closest ally and security guarantor. With Democrats soon to be in control of both the House and the Senate, Congress will likely pass bills that sanction settlement building and condition military aid on adherence to U.S. and international law.

Without changes in U.S. and Israeli policy, the two countries could see their relationship rupture as continued support for Israel becomes politically untenable in the United States. To rehabilitate itself in the eyes of Americans—and therefore ensure long-term cooperation with the United States—Israel will need to change course. It must abandon Netanyahu’s bludgeoning approach to both the Palestinians and the wider region.

It is no exaggeration to say that the future of the U.S.-Israeli relationship rests on what type of government Israel forms in the next few months. Netanyahu’s exclusion from the next government represents the best hope for preserving bilateral relations with the United States. Netanyahu has proved unwilling to stay out of U.S. politics or modify draconian security policies that have run afoul of the American public. Unfortunately, most of Netanyahu’s rivals for prime minister share his hawkish position on the Palestinians and the Middle East. Were they in power, they seem likely to resist fundamental revisions to Israeli policy that could safeguard the bilateral relationship.

Israel’s next leaders need to journey outside their comfort zone. They will have to come to terms with new realities: that tensions between the United States and Israel are now deep-rooted and that they can’t rely on the adamantine support of the Republican party any longer. To win back Americans, the Israeli government will have to rigorously follow through on its commitments under Trump’s plan for Gaza, daring Hamas to follow suit. In addition to flooding Gaza with humanitarian aid, Israel should let the NCAG enter the territory to offer an alternative to Hamas’s rule and nihilistic agenda. Enabling the deployment of newly trained Palestinian security forces and the formation of the International Stabilization Force, a peacekeeping mission, would also help show that, whatever unfolds in Gaza, Israel is operating in good faith. Israeli forces should immediately return to the demarcation lines traced in Trump’s Gaza peace plan and pull back even further in concert with Hamas’s progress in fulfilling the terms of the plan until Israel has completely withdrawn from Gaza.

Israel must also rein in violent settlers in the West Bank and hold them accountable through the courts. The Israeli security services can identify the leaders and primary instigators behind the violence and should arrest them and bring them to justice. Given that settlers have killed and injured American citizens in these attacks, continued inaction by Israeli authorities is a recipe for more friction with the United States, especially if a Democratic government comes to power in Washington.

Israel must also halt its creeping annexation of the West Bank. Over the past three years, the Netanyahu government has increased its military presence in the West Bank, deprived the Palestinian Authority of funds, asserted Israeli government control over larger swaths of Palestinian land, approved a slew of new Israeli settlements, and retroactively legalized illicit outposts. In sum, these actions aim to render a Palestinian state impossible. Although reversing these measures would not arrest the expansion of existing settlements and Israeli infrastructure throughout the West Bank, they would be received positively in the United States.

Israeli leaders also need to break the taboo around Palestinian statehood. Only a couple of decades ago, the idea of a Palestinian state was conceivable to most Israelis. Today, Israeli politicians, with precious few exceptions, cannot bring themselves to endorse a two-state solution. It will take time and no small degree of political resolve, but Israeli leaders must convince the public to accept the necessity and the virtues of a Palestinian state existing side by side with Israel. An Israel that can view Palestinians as neighbors entitled to the same rights would provide a very different image to the world than a messianic government bent on expelling whole populations.

These steps will not satisfy all American critics of Israel. Anti-Israeli attitudes cannot be erased in a matter of months. Where anti-Israeli opinion intersects with anti-Semitism—evident in incidents such as a recent boycott of Jewish-owned restaurants in Philadelphia organized by the group Philly Bike on Your Left—the task is even more difficult and will necessitate a different set of policies that transcend Israel, including the formation of diverse domestic and international coalitions to identify and condemn anti-Semitic acts. Yet a far less belligerent Israel and one newly committed to the resolution of the unanswered question of Palestinian self-determination would start to shift U.S. public opinion back in the country’s favor.

If Israel fails to change course, it will find itself less secure and more isolated internationally. Without access to international markets and capital, its economy, which has withstood the turmoil of the last several years remarkably well, will suffer and living standards will fall. Under these circumstances, Jewish Israeli citizens with second passports may elect to leave, depriving the state of its raison d’être—providing a safe and prosperous homeland for the Jewish people. Put simply, an Israel without American support will struggle to survive.

A Failing Peace Process Leads to Only One Outcome

The Netanyahu Doctrine Could Outlast Netanyahu

Beijing’s Overreach Threatens the Détente With America

U.S. Dealmaking Is Strengthening the Chavista Regime

Only a Radical Course Change Can Win Back Its Most Important Ally

Arab Citizens of Israel Can Bring Change, but They Can’t Do It Alone

#5942. 2028 presidential contender under fire from state auditor over waste, fraud, abuse fears: 'Troubling response'

Fox News Digital spoke to Kentucky Republican State Auditor Allison Ball about the waste, fraud and abuse discoveries she has made and what she says is a lack of cooperation from the Democratic Gov. Andy Beshear.

Kentucky’s top auditor says her office has uncovered $1.5 billion in taxpayer waste across state government and accused Democratic Gov. Andy Beshear, widely believed to have presidential aspirations, of brushing off the findings instead of helping fix them.

"I wish I could say that I had more support and help from the governor's office in Kentucky, but I haven't really. And that's one of the reasons why I'm very public with our reports. I'm very public with our findings because I need the public's help," Ball said in an interview this week at a State Financial Officers Foundation (SFOF) conference in Philadelphia.

"I need the backlash that comes when you see money just being flushed down the toilet, when you see that you're losing money, especially in Medicaid. It's money that should go to people who are actually in need, and we're losing those dollars."

Over the past year, Ball’s office flagged $836 million in wasteful Medicaid spending that was primarily driven by multi-state concurrent enrollment, where Kentucky continued paying monthly managed care capitation fees for individuals simultaneously enrolled in Medicaid in up to eight other states.

TRUMP ADMIN PAUSING OVER $1B IN MEDICAID PAYMENTS TO MINNESOTA, CALIFORNIA OVER FRAUD, COMPLIANCE CONCERNS

Kentucky Gov. Andy Beshear speaks during an event at the Center for American Progress on Feb. 19, 2026, in Washington, D.C. (Getty Images)

Her audits also flagged ineligible noncitizens receiving benefits, single Social Security numbers being used by up to 10 people at once, duplicate individual payments and roughly $100 million in SNAP benefit errors, bringing the total waste she cited across state benefit programs to $1.5 billion.

"I have had conversations with the governor's staff," Ball explained. "We are always letting the governor know, 'Hey, this is what we found.' We also offer solutions all the time. And typically what I get back is, like for the $836 million of Medicaid dollars that we found, is that, 'Well, that happens everywhere. You know, that's not unique to us.'

"And that's really a troubling response. If your attitude is like, 'Well, it's not just us, you know, we're not really doing a horrible job because it happens other places too,' that explains why problems like this continue."

RNC RIPS BESHEAR'S 'WOKE TRANS RECORD' OVER VETO OF KENTUCKY GIRLS' SPORTS BILL

The Kentucky state Capitol in Frankfort, Ky., June 8, 2022. (Ryan C. Hermens/Lexington Herald-Leader/Tribune News Service via Getty Images)

Additionally, Ball’s office released a report earlier this year outlining spending by Beshear’s executive branch in fiscal year 2025 that, while not illegal, flagged what she called "massive" issues of extravagant spending of tax dollars that "needs to stop."

The "concerning expenditures" listed include $183,576 in out-of-state travel costs, including $7,632 for a limousine in Germany; a $17,013 dinner at a Kentucky distillery; and $360,000 for 75 people to attend a two-day conference within the Commonwealth.

"I think one thing that I have realized is that the governor doesn't like bad news. And, unfortunately, a lot of times when I come in, and I do an audit, the news that I have is bad," Ball said.

"But what I also try to remind people, it's good too, in the sense that I also have a recommendation for how to fix it. So, while it is important to identify the problems — and I do that — I'm also hyper-focused on the solution. And I would encourage the governor to jump in and help me be part of the solution to this.

"That's not been the experience that I've had. I've not had cooperation, and I would love to have that. I think that that's what Kentuckians deserve."

Democratic Gov. Andy Beshear of Kentucky speaks to a crowd of New Hampshire Democrats at an event in Manchester, N.H., Oct. 7, 2025. (Paul Steinhauser/Fox News)

Beshear’s office has repeatedly pushed back on Ball’s criticisms, dismissing her $836 million Medicaid waste estimate as "a wild extrapolation from a small subset" and maintaining that once Kentucky gained access to updated federal cross-check databases, actual multi-state overlap numbers were "nothing remotely close to that."

In a statement to Fox News Digital, his office said the governor has "created a fiscally responsible administration, securing six consecutive budget surpluses, the five best years on record for economic investment and upgrades from all three major bond rating agencies while also cutting income and property taxes. His management of the state is delivering positive results, so hardworking Kentuckians can live better lives."

Beshear's office added that Ball's report has "significant errors and inaccuracies" and suggested she focus on "real threats looming in Kentucky due to Trump's ‘Big, Ugly Bill,’ which risks the closure of 35 rural hospitals, the jobs of 20,000 healthcare workers and health coverage for thousands of Kentuckians."

When it comes to potential Medicaid waste, fraud and abuse, Ball told Fox News Digital it is unlikely that money can be clawed back, heightening the importance of stopping it before it begins.

The Trump administration has cracked down on fraud this year and set up the Task Force to Eliminate Fraud in March, which has reported that federal agencies have identified $260.7 billion in estimated fraud since January 2025.

Ball told Fox News Digital that, heading into 2027, she sees a "tremendous opportunity" to address waste, fraud and abuse in Kentucky because the "public cares right now, I think, at a level that they've not cared before."

"I've always cared about protecting taxpayer dollars. I've also cared about stopping waste, fraud and abuse. I care about complying with the law," Ball said.

"I think this is this beautiful moment right now where the public deeply cares about it too. So, I think I have an opportunity. I think other auditors around the country, other people who are in similar roles, have opportunities like it's never been before where we really can take the effort to stop these things from happening."

Andrew Mark Miller is a reporter from Pasadena, California, who has been with Fox News Digital since 2021. You can reach him at AndrewMark.Miller@fox.com.

#5940. The Other Summitry in Shenzhen

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Latin America Brief: The Other Summitry in Shenzhen

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Welcome back to Foreign Policy’s Latin America Brief.

The highlights this week: Three regional presidents travel to China, the United States and Venezuela strike a military deal, and the region weighs fossil fuel phaseout plans at COP31.

Welcome back to Foreign Policy’s Latin America Brief.

The highlights this week: Three regional presidents travel to China, the United States and Venezuela strike a military deal, and the region weighs fossil fuel phaseout plans at COP31.

This week, many observers focused on the trilateral meeting among the presidents of China, Russia, and the United States on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation (APEC) summit. Three Latin American presidents were also in Shenzhen, China, for the meetings: those of Chile, Mexico, and Peru.

All six countries are APEC members. Though the bloc’s Latin American leaders don’t always attend the annual summit in person, they did this year—and held separate bilateral meetings with Chinese President Xi Jinping. The 2028 APEC conference, hosted by Mexico, will see Asian leaders travel to Latin America.

In their trips to Shenzhen, the three presidents signaled that they value relations with China despite pressure from the United States to spurn the country.

The U.S. National Security Strategy issued by the Trump administration last year said that allowing “non-Hemispheric competitors” to grow their presence in the Americas was a “strategic mistake.” And the U.S. ambassadors to both Mexico and Peru disparaged China in social media posts this week.

Mexico has applied new tariffs on Chinese goods since U.S. President Donald Trump returned to office, with some as high as 50 percent. But China remains Mexico’s No. 2 trade partner, and Mexican President Claudia Sheinbaum said in Shenzhen that she hoped her trip would yield more Mexican avocado and tequila exports to China and new cooperation on smart hospitals.

For Mexico, “integration with the United States and market access to the United States is priority No. 1,” said Diego Marroquín, a fellow in the Americas program at the Center for Strategic and International Studies. But “they can achieve that and at the same time, they can engage more with China,” he said, especially on issues that aren’t central to U.S. interests.

In more sensitive areas, such as critical infrastructure and telecommunications, Mexico “will most likely accommodate U.S. demands,” Marroquín wrote this week.

Chile has not imposed tariffs on Chinese goods, though it has seen tensions with China flare in other ways. This month, Chile and Belgium jointly challenged a Chinese bid to permanently host the secretariat of the new United Nations High Seas Treaty.

The dispute over hosting rights is still unresolved, and Chilean President José Antonio Kast did not mention it in his bilateral meeting with Xi, according to multiple reports. The two countries announced a new economic cooperation road map without providing specifics. The partnership is potentially promising news for Chile as it experiences a recession.

Peruvian President Keiko Fujimori, meanwhile, walked away with a more concrete commitment from China: a $22.5 million pledge to help Peru deal with the fallout of El Niño. Both countries also celebrated an update to their free trade deal that took effect this month, which officials said could help trade and investment rise by streamlining import- and export-related bureaucracy.

“Our meeting allows us to strengthen relations between Peru and China,” Fujimori wrote on social media after meeting with Xi. The Chinese readout said that Fujimori praised the majority-Chinese-operated port in the Chancay, Peru—an investment that the Trump administration has criticized.

Fujimori, Kast, and Sheinbaum held additional bilateral meetings at APEC. Sheinbaum met with Trump, while all three Latin American presidents sat down with other Asian leaders, including those of Japan; Vietnam; the Philippines, with which Chile signed a free trade agreement; and Thailand, with which Peru did the same.

The meetings show that the three leaders hope to deepen ties not only with China but also more broadly with Asia. They are not alone in their neighborhood: Last weekend, Brazilian President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva addressed the opening session of the Association of Southeast Asian Nations summit in Manila, Philippines.

These countries’ trade flows are beginning to reflect the presidential trips. To give one example, Mexico’s total goods traded with Japan between January and September of this year was 9 percent higher than over the same period in 2025, according to Mexican government statistics. With Thailand, trade grew by 50 percent, and with Malaysia, by 78 percent.

Friday, Nov. 20: Fujimori meets Japanese Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi in Tokyo.

Monday, Nov. 23, to Friday, Nov. 27: Polish President Karol Nawrocki visits Argentina and Chile.

Thursday, Dec. 3, to Friday, Dec. 4: Uruguay hosts the Mercosur leaders’ summit.

U.S. military deals. Less than a year after removing Venezuela’s president by force, the United States signed a military partnership with the country’s acting leaders on Wednesday. In Caracas to ink the deal, U.S. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth called Venezuela a “compliant security partner” and said the arrangement would allow both countries to better combat drug gangs.

Former Venezuelan President Hugo Chávez ended Venezuelan military cooperation with the United States in 2005, and his successors in the chavismo movement have used their control over Venezuela’s military apparatus to cling to power and crush dissent.

Rocío San Miguel, a top Venezuelan human rights advocate, told Union Radio that the U.S. partnership could be an opportunity to rein in Venezuela’s military, relegating it to its intended constitutional role.

The Venezuela deal comes as U.S. military cooperation with neighboring Colombia appears to be falling short of Trump’s vision. The New York Times reported on Wednesday that Hegseth altered his travel plans to Colombia this week after the country’s government said it lacked the congressional votes to greenlight U.S. military operations on its soil.

Colombian President Abelardo de la Espriella wrote on social media afterward that he did not reject a specific plan but added that any U.S. military cooperation must follow Colombian law.

Shifting regional affinities. A gathering of Latin America scholars and policymakers in Rio de Janeiro last week highlighted the cross-border integration that is often excluded from headlines about the region. This newsletter writer attended the event, Conversatorio Latinoamericano.

One speaker flagged a recent University of Buenos Aires opinion survey about Argentines’ attitudes on geopolitics. Between 2025 and 2026, Brazil surpassed the United States to top respondents’ answers to two questions: “Which country do you think Argentina should have relations with?” and “Which country do you think Argentina should be like?”

The shift suggests that average Argentines don’t fully embrace President Javier Milei’s pro-Trump pivot—or his chilled relations with Brazil.

Natalia Saltalamacchia Ziccardi of the Mexico Autonomous Institute of Technology said that Brazil and Mexico are turning the page on years of distrust, experiencing a “springtime” of warming ties since around 2024. She cited increased high-level visits, coordination in response to Venezuela’s crisis, and shared support for a Chilean U.N. secretary-general candidate.

These sympathies show that dividing the region into pro- and anti-Trump camps is an oversimplification.

Chilean director Sebastián Lelio, winner of the best foreign language film award for A Fantastic Woman, poses in the press room during the 90th Academy Awards in Hollywood, California, on March 4, 2018.Alberto E. Rodriguez/Getty Images

Chilean cinema pioneer. This month, Chileans are remembering star film director Sebastián Lelio, who died on Nov. 6 at the age of 52. Lelio got his start as part of a wave of indie Chilean filmmaking in the 2000s that focused on small-scale, interpersonal plotlines and was dubbed Novísimo Cine Chileno by critics.

Lelio’s most beloved works include the 2013 film Gloria, which shows the ups and downs of dating as a 58-year-old divorcée in the capital of Santiago, and the 2017 film A Fantastic Woman, which follows a transgender singer dealing with the logistics triggered by the death of her partner—and the prejudice that she faces from his conservative family.

A Fantastic Woman won Chile its first-ever Oscar for best foreign language film and helped pave the way for the passage of a 2018 trans rights law. Though Lelio worked across genres, La Tercera’s Gonzalo Valdivia wrote that he “found his natural habitat in portraits of female characters who don’t give in or apologize.”

Which Latin American country was the first to strike a free trade deal with China?

The two countries signed the agreement in 2005.

Panamanian Environment Minister Juan Carlos Navarro speaks during a press conference discussing electrification at COP31 in Antalya, Turkey, on Nov. 18. Chris McGrath/Getty Images

The leaders of Antigua and Barbuda, the Bahamas, Barbados, Bolivia, Brazil, St. Lucia, and Venezuela all traveled to this year’s U.N. climate summit in Antalya, Turkey, known as COP31. The conference is due to wrap up on Friday.

COP31 has moved the ball forward on some initiatives from last year’s U.N. climate summit, which Brazil hosted. For example, a planned investment fund that would pay countries to keep tropical forests intact has now raised more than $8 billion, thanks to a new pledge from Canada.

Last week, Brazil published a framework for countries to create national plans for transitioning away from fossil fuels, a topic that generated buzz at last year’s summit but was cut from the final agreement. Its potential inclusion in the COP31 agreement is still being haggled over.

While Brazil has left a big mark on COP31, so too has a previous Latin American government: that of Colombian President Gustavo Petro, who departed in August. Unsatisfied with last year’s event, the Petro administration hosted a new global conference in April that was devoted entirely to quitting fossil fuels, which will see a second edition next year in Tuvalu.

A group of countries that was heavily involved in planning both conferences has also committed to creating a binding treaty on moving away from fossil fuels. Though Colombia has stayed quiet about the fossil fuel treaty initiative under de la Espriella, countries including Guatemala and Panama had joined it by the time that COP31 arrived.

“We are being held hostage by the petroleum mafia,” Panamanian Environment Minister Juan Carlos Navarro said at COP31.

Many Latin American countries are currently led by right-wing leaders who have not styled themselves as climate champions. But they are nevertheless moving forward on an issue that has been proposed as a main outcome of COP31: electrification.

This year, Brazil is on track to more than double the number of electric vehicles, or EVs, sold since last year, while Mexico’s EV sales are up by more than 25 percent. Countries across the region are planning large-scale battery storage build-outs to support larger electric grids.

The attractive price of green energy—and its insulation from global price shocks—is keeping the region’s green transition gradually moving forward.

Catherine Osborn is the writer of Foreign Policy’s weekly Latin America Brief. She is a print and radio journalist based in Rio de Janeiro. X: @cculbertosborn
