#5938. Is China Misreading the Moment?

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

RUSH DOSHI is an Assistant Professor at Georgetown University and Director of the China Strategy Initiative at the Council on Foreign Relations. He served as Director and Deputy Senior Director for China and Taiwan Affairs at the National Security Council from 2021 to 2024.

Beijing’s Overreach Threatens the Détente With America

When U.S. President Donald Trump lands in Shenzhen later this week for the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit, he will sit down with Chinese leader Xi Jinping for the fourth time amid an unexpected détente between Washington and Beijing. The trade war of 2025 is in abeyance, the two governments have announced a relationship of “constructive strategic stability,” and, to the alarm of American allies, Trump and Xi are planning to meet with Russian President Vladimir Putin. Beneath the surface, however, the détente is beginning to crack.

The relationship between Washington and Beijing looks remarkably different from the way it did a little over a year ago, when the two sides agreed to a truce in Busan, South Korea. In April 2025, Washington initiated the trade war by raising tariffs on China past 140 percent, and Beijing responded by restricting rare-earth exports later that month and threatening another cutoff that October. After Trump promptly backed down, the two leaders reached the deal in Busan: Washington would cap tariffs and suspend a specific export control regulation known as the Affiliates Rule in exchange for Beijing’s suspension of new export controls on minerals and magnets and the resumption of their normal flow to the United States. On that basis, both sides pursued a broad deescalation.

Since then, the Trump administration has been notably accommodating. Washington has kept new tariffs at 12.5 percent, well below the 20-percent ceiling Beijing previously agreed to. It has let advanced AI chips flow to China and to Chinese cutouts in Southeast Asia and has not added a Chinese firm to the Entity List in over the year, the longest pause since 2008. It has withheld arms sales from and downgraded defense talks with Taiwan, cut back naval transits of the Taiwan Strait, and raised doubts about U.S. support in the event of a crisis. And it has offered only muted support to such allies as Japan and the Philippines in the face of escalating Chinese coercion. All the while, Trump has praised Xi as a “great leader” and extended rare courtesies, including by welcoming Xi on the tarmac when he arrived in Washington for a state visit in September, the first such greeting of a foreign leader in 60 years.

Beijing has appreciated this accommodating approach—and seized the opportunity to act more assertively. It has viewed the competitive pause as temporary, a product of China’s growing strength and Trump’s admiration for Xi, and as a way to buy time to build advantage before the next eruption of tensions. With Washington occupied by its war in Iran and humbled by defeat in the last round of the trade war, Beijing has throttled the supply of minerals and magnets to the United States, taken steps to ensure that its industrial and technological gains remain in China, and increased pressure on its neighbors.

So far, Beijing has used the time well, advancing its objectives with little serious pushback from Washington. But China risks overreaching in its relationship with the United States. Not only is an incoming Democratic Congress likely to constrain Trump’s conciliatory China policy. There are also growing frustrations over Beijing’s unwillingness to fulfill the terms agreed to in Busan. Pressure on AI chips and export controls are emerging in the wake of revelations about the Nvidia co-founder Jensen Huang’s relationship with an alleged Chinese spy and his company’s influence over U.S. export control policy. And new U.S. policies in response to the scandal could provoke a response from Beijing.

Xi and Trump will meet in Shenzhen and again in December in Miami, but with the January expiration of the Busan agreement looming in the background (and with no summits planned for next year), time is running out for the two leaders to extend and strengthen the current truce. If the détente falls apart the next time Washington’s and Beijing’s interests clash, China will be better prepared. It may also, perhaps hubristically, be more willing to escalate.

Although China favors Washington’s current accommodating approach and hopes it will last as long as possible, Beijing is also confident that it is strengthening its hand for a more contentious future. For China’s leaders, the current détente has been less an end goal than a temporary intermission, a means to build leverage ahead of the next round of strategic competition and, ultimately, a decisive long-term advantage.

Prominent Chinese scholars see the calm that followed Busan as tactical rather than strategic. Chen Dongxiao, the president of the Shanghai Institutes for International Studies, told the Shanghai news outlet The Paper that the two countries remain in a “tactical truce.” In May, Wu Xinbo of Fudan University told Beijing News, “If we can achieve a relationship of constructive strategic stability over the next three years, it will extend China’s period of strategic stability and win us time and space for development.” Writing in the journal International Review in August, he declared that the current “tactical stability” was “neither sustainable nor solid.” Shen Weizhong, a former Chinese foreign ministry official, was more blunt: “The more time we have, the better prepared we might be towards a final showdown on Taiwan or maybe another trade war.”

Beijing attributes little of the stabilization to Washington. In his August essay, Wu wrote that it “is not the result of the U.S. side having a change of heart or showing goodwill, but was won by China through arduous striving and struggle.” The United States had no choice; it simply needed the “breathing room.” Wang Yong, the director of Peking University’s American Studies Center, described the trade war as “a tipping point in terms of strength.” The former editor of the Global Times, Hu Xijin, wrote in a Weibo post after the U.S. midterm elections that “the current equilibrium in U.S.-China relations is the result of a contest of strength.” As Wu told The Paper in September, Washington has come to understand that Beijing “needs to be treated equally and with respect” and that China is “bolder about ‘drawing its sword.’” Keeping the supply of rare-earth minerals and magnets tight, to Beijing’s mind, might reinforce the current competitive pause rather than undermine it by reminding American leaders of China’s leverage.

Another critical factor in the current de-escalation, Chinese analysts argue, is Trump’s personality. Trump’s “distinct and unique decision-making style,” as Chen put it, has single-handedly blunted the more forceful defense of U.S. interests that members of his own administration and legislators in both parties favor. “He is often not entirely swayed by bipartisan opposition and still continues to pursue policies based on his own judgment,” Chen explained in the same interview. Writing in the Singaporean paper Lianhe Zaobao in September, Zhao Minghao of Fudan University more pointedly explained that Trump “admires power and prefers to engage with the leaders of powerful countries,” a stabilizing trait that benefits Beijing’s diplomacy while leaving allies and partners on the sidelines. “Of all modern U.S. presidents, Trump has cared the least about Taiwan,” one Guizhou-based commentator wrote. “He would readily sacrifice the island for his own interests.” An article reposted on the website of Qiushi, the Chinese Communist Party’s flagship journal, dwelt on Trump’s decision to greet Xi at the airport when he arrived for his state visit in September. The gesture, it claimed, “reflects the new historical position of China-U.S. relations.”

But China also worries that Trump’s influence will decline over the next two years and perhaps cease altogether after 2028. Wang Hao, a professor at Fudan University’s Center for American Studies, told Phoenix News that the Democratic Party’s approach will not be “as pragmatic and flexible as the Trump administration’s,” a sentiment echoed across Chinese social media after the Democrats’ victory in the midterm elections.

The awareness that Trump’s approach may not survive beyond his presidency is precisely why Beijing has sought to lock it in through diplomacy. According to the Chinese readout, at the summit in May 2026, Xi declared that the relationship of “constructive strategic stability” he and Trump had agreed to build would provide guidance for U.S.-Chinese relations “over the next three years and beyond.” That duration was not accidental. As the scholar Zheng Yongnian put it at the time, the three-year window was “tailor-made for President Trump,” matching his current term. Beijing hopes it will bind his successor.

If diplomacy fails to secure U.S. deescalation, China hopes leverage will succeed. Over the last year, Beijing has used the time and space created by the détente to strengthen its position in industrial and technological competition with the United States. Beijing has long sought to make the world more dependent on China while reducing its own dependence on the world. But over the last year, that approach has evolved. Beijing has unveiled a raft of measures intended to ensure that its industrial, technological, and scientific know-how do not diffuse beyond China’s borders in order to lock in the country’s advantages.

To keep foreign firms in China and blunt Western countries’ leverage over the Chinese economy, the State Council issued Orders 834 and 835, which effectively threaten companies that cut ties with Chinese partners or seek to exit the country with the risk of investigation, exclusion from the Chinese market, exit bans on executives, and possible criminal prosecution. To keep talent in China, Beijing has authorized exit bans on citizens considered possible risks to industrial or technological security and, according to Bloomberg, now requires travel approval for top AI and chip specialists and their families. And to keep scientific discoveries in China, the Ministry of State Security has warned researchers that they must clear security reviews before submitting articles to foreign journals. China has also imposed or proposed new export controls on dozens of technologies, from mining and batteries to AI models and biotechnology in order to protect its industrial capabilities. Beijing has even worked to prevent the transfer of equipment and engineers to India and Southeast Asia, stalled battery ventures with Indian firms, undermined Apple’s effort to move manufacturing to India, and issued a sweeping regulation that subjects Chinese investment abroad to a national security review more expansive than the United States’ own outbound investment review.

Beijing’s rare-earth licensing regime exemplifies China’s leverage-building strategy. To obtain a license, foreign buyers must hand over customer lists, the identities of their end users, and images of their production lines, all of which gives China insight into Western dependencies and helps it uncover chokepoints it can squeeze. Beijing has threatened to use these powers against countries that seek to protect themselves from the flood of Chinese manufactured goods entering their markets. In July, Beijing showed how precise it can now be in applying pressure when it cut off 14 European firms, including the German arms maker Rheinmetall, from Chinese dual-use exports in retaliation for EU sanctions related to Chinese support of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

Beijing has paired these measures at home with escalating pressure campaigns in its near abroad, betting that a distracted and chastened Washington will not come to the aid of China’s neighbors. That campaign has been most brazen in the South China Sea, where China has engaged in escalations unseen before the détente. Since Busan, Beijing has essentially abrogated its 2024 understanding with the Philippines, which maintained stability on the question of Second Thomas Shoal. In July, a Chinese coast guard boat rammed a Philippine navy inflatable and its crew clubbed a sailor on the head with a wooden baton, the most violent assault on Philippine personnel in years.

The timing of these incidents has been provocative. In July, when Chinese Foreign Minister Wang Yi and U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio were in Manila for the Association of Southeast Asian Nations meetings, the coast guard launched water-cannon attacks on Philippine vessels near Scarborough Shoal. Then, in September, during Xi’s U.S. state visit, Chinese warships and cutters blocked a Philippine resupply mission outright for the first time in two years. In October, Chinese cutters interfered with a medical evacuation from Second Thomas Shoal, delaying a patient transfer by ten hours. Last month, Beijing’s ambassador in Manila mused that if China chose to use force against the Philippines, “we may easily win.”

China has also run its broadest coercion campaign against Japan in more than a decade, nominally in response to comments made by Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi on Taiwan. It has included a travel advisory, a seafood ban, a dual-use export ban, and curbs on rare-earth exports that have forced Japanese companies to contemplate shutting down operations. Chinese carrier-based fighters have locked their weapons-targeting radar onto Japanese jets. Beijing has also pursued new gas structures in the East China Sea and set a record 357-day coast guard presence in 2025 around the Japanese-administered Senkaku Islands (known in China as the Diaoyu Islands), with cutters now chasing Japanese fishing boats into territorial waters. Washington’s support for Tokyo has, so far, been limited.

And around Taiwan, China has made its pressure quieter but more routine. In December 2025, during the People’s Liberation Army’s Justice Mission exercises, China fired live rockets closer to Taiwan than ever before, landing ten of them inside the island’s 24-nautical-mile contiguous zone. As of this summer, Beijing has reduced air incursions, but coast guard activity around Taiwan’s outlying islands has become routinized. And since June, Beijing has run standing “law enforcement patrols” east of Taiwan, asserting jurisdiction over the waters Taipei would need in a blockade.

Outside of Asia, Beijing has sought to take advantage of anger, particularly in the developing world, at the Trump administration’s “Liberation Day” tariffs and the Iran war. Before traveling to Washington, Xi made a point of stopping in several capitals: he toured Bishkek and Cairo, and attended the BRICS summit in New Delhi, stressing that China, not the United States, is now the steadier partner.

Beijing has reason to think this approach is working. It rightly judged that Washington would prioritize keeping the détente alive over responding to its provocations. A year after Busan, China holds a tighter grip on critical supply chains, has set new precedents in Asia, and faces an adversary in Washington that has grown used to holding back. Beijing likely believes it is better off now than it was when the truce began.

What looks like success now, however, may still prove to be overreach. In addition to rare earths and magnets, several important questions went unresolved in past U.S.-Chinese summits and are likely to resurface in this week’s summit in Shenzhen, including China’s anticipated purchases of Boeing aircraft and agricultural products, AI safety, and the United States’ suspension of arms sales to Taiwan. But the most important question is what will become of the current trade truce.

The United States has complied with the terms of the Busan agreement, suspending the Affiliates Rule and lowering tariffs, but Beijing has throttled the supply of rare-earth minerals and magnets to the United States. U.S. officials say China has delivered only about two-thirds of what it promised, and trade data suggest the shortfall is larger still. The administration believes Beijing’s noncompliance is deliberate. Anecdotal evidence from American business leaders in regular touch with the Ministry of Commerce confirms the administration’s assessment that Beijing seeks to keep a tight leash on rare-earth minerals and will occasionally “yank the chain” on individual companies in response to any perceived slight by Washington.

From the U.S. perspective, the very point of the Busan negotiations was to avoid this situation. Indeed, the U.S. fact sheet at Busan indicated that China agreed to issue general licenses for a number of rare earths and other critical minerals to American end users and their suppliers worldwide; case-by-case licenses, which could be delayed by Beijing, would not be required. China has not denied making that commitment, and a few weeks after the summit, the Ministry of Commerce began issuing a new category of general licenses to magnet makers. But Beijing’s licenses have been narrow and firm specific, and American industry continues to face enormous challenges in securing certain rare earths, notably yttrium and scandium.

Beijing’s intention may not be to undermine the Busan agreement but, paradoxically, to strengthen it. In keeping with its belief that strength begets American conciliation, China’s strategy appears to be to occasionally impose shortages to remind Washington of the leverage that Beijing continues to enjoy. But the tactic is beginning to backfire. Shortages of yttrium, used for jet engines and gas turbines, have forced some suppliers to suspend production. Chipmakers are running short of scandium. All of this strengthens the arguments of those who believe Beijing never intended to honor Busan and that the United States should begin to walk away from the agreement. If Washington concludes that China will weaponize rare earths no matter what the United States does, the case for American restraint may fall apart, and with it the détente that has bought Beijing the time it prizes.

Tensions are also mounting in other areas. Each side is increasingly frustrated with the other’s unilateral economic measures and restrictions on technology, particularly those that were not addressed in Busan: for Beijing, U.S. import controls, outbound investment regimes, and licensing backlogs; for Washington, Chinese export controls and State Council directives. A Democratic Congress is less likely to countenance Trump’s China policy and more likely to pursue investigations into China’s espionage and influence efforts and potential malfeasance at the Department of Commerce. The explosive controversy around Huang and Nvidia has the potential to inject fresh tensions into the relationship, particularly on matters related to AI and export controls. The Department of Commerce has already issued a new interim final rule to stop Chinese firms from smuggling chips into China or remotely accessing American AI chips in Southeast Asian data centers. Administration officials insist that closing a loophole China itself claims not to use “should not be something that generates countermeasures.” But Beijing may well retaliate before the rule takes effect in February.

These headwinds make breakthroughs in Shenzhen on purchases, artificial intelligence, nuclear weapons, or Iran less likely. With the truce, extended last week by just two months, set to formally expire on January 10, the summit is one of the last opportunities for Trump and Xi to reach even a modest declaration that they are working to extend the terms set out in Busan and will aim to conclude negotiations in December, when Xi comes to Miami for the G-20 summit.

If China were to continue to throttle rare-earth minerals and magnets, and a January breakdown did come to pass, the two sides might find themselves in another round of escalation. Xi may arrive at the two upcoming summits holding a stronger hand than he did in Busan. But his actions are gradually producing a counterpart with less room—and perhaps less reason—to keep folding.

#5937. Mamdani’s plan to dismantle NYPD’s riot unit ripped as ‘smoke and mirrors’ by former force leaders

Former NYPD Chief John Chell and former NYPD inspector Paul Mauro are accusing New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani's new disbanding of the Strategic Response Group as a move to please his base, with little real change to protest response being made.

New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani's plan to gut the city’s tactical police unit is not what is seems, two former high-ranking NYPD officials tell Fox News Digital.

They argue the stunt is a political sleight of hand in which specialized officers responsible for handling violent protests will be rebranded under a different name despite the socialist mayor's promise to disband it completely.

"The problem is … day-to-day New Yorkers don't understand all this gas lighting," former NYPD Chief John Chell told Fox News Digital. "And they feel the angst of it.

"But at the end of all that, this is nothing more than smoke and mirrors."

EXCLUSIVE: RETIRED POLICE SERGEANT ISSUES WARNING OVER MAMDANI'S NYPD SHAKE-UP AHEAD OF THANKSGIVING PARADE

New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani attends a news conference at NYPD headquarters in New York City on Jan. 6, 2026. (Spencer Platt/Getty Images)

The move to dissolve the Strategic Response Group (SRG) and replace it with a "first-of-its-kind" civilian dialogue team fulfills one of Mamdani's campaign promises as noted in the first sentence of his news release on the move.

But Chell and retired NYPD Inspector Paul Mauro are questioning whether it's all a facade to please the mayor's left-wing base and is a restructuring rather than a disbandment. They say the unit will likely be rebuilt under a new name to continue dealing with mass protests, major crimes and counterterrorism.

"The con here is that the SRG is simply being rebranded into an unknown name right now," Chell said.

"First of all, Mamdani did add … that there will be uniformed police standing by to take more forceful enforcement if necessary," Mauro said. "So, what really changed? Well, he changed the name of that unit, and he inserted this civilian unit between those uniformed cops and the protesters."

The newly proposed civilian-led Community Dialogue and De-escalation Division (CDDD) is meant to work as a buffer between protesters and the on-duty police who will remain present at large demonstrations.

"Socialism and public safety do not go hand in hand," Chell insisted.

Mamdani’s office did not respond to Fox News Digital’s request for comment on the criticism of his plan.

NY COP CALLS FOR POLICE PROTECTIONS AFTER PROTESTER HE ARRESTED SHOWED UP TO HIS HOUSE, HARASSED HIS FAMILY

NYPD officers arrest a demonstrator during an anti-ICE protest outside 26 Federal Plaza Building in New York City June 10, 2025. (Mostafa Bassim/Anadolu via Getty Images)

Both former NYPD officials warned that relying on civilians to mediate rowdy crowds could delay police intervention if a peaceful protest turns into a violent riot. This, he said, could leave officers responding to an escalating situation rather than preventing it in the first place.

"Once a cop, wherever they're assigned, becomes reactive versus proactive, public safety suffers," Chell said. "And [when cops] become reactive, they tend to hold back, and that can get them hurt. And it can get civilians hurt."

"That is one of the risks inherent in this thing," Mauro added. "That you deploy the civilians, they're trying to talk … and by the time the civilians turn around and say, ‘Police, we need help,’ it may be too late."

SRG receives information from the NYPD’s Intelligence and Counterterrorism Bureau to be kept aware of known or potentially dangerous demonstrations that will require specialized training to de-escalate.

The SRG unit is trained to handle crowds that have turned violent, beginning with verbal de-escalation. The physical training teaches officers how to get a noncomplying individual into police vans. A common tactic used by the SRG is bicycle crowd management, whereby officers can line up their bikes to build a barricade in crowds, using those formations for crowd control.

"The purpose of the SRG was to make sure that you had highly trained officers who could diffuse, spin down, but then control if need be, protests that were getting out of hand," Mauro said. "That's why they were only deployed in about 6% of the instances."

MAMDANI CLASHES WITH DEMOCRATIC SOCIALISTS' PUSH TO ABOLISH POLICE AND PRISONS

During a budget hearing earlier this year, it was noted the SRG did not often deal with peaceful protests and was mostly only deployed for protests that turned violent. In 2025, NYPD reportedly facilitated 4,255 protests and the SRG was available for roughly 6% of them.

Critics of the SRG, including the ACLU's New York affiliate, say the unit is inherently violent due to its counterterrorism specialization that they claim "suppress protesters" and disregards "focus on First Amendment protections."

The training manual, reviewed by Fox News Digital, distinguishes between "peaceful/compliant" crowds and those deemed "violent/non-compliant." It also identifies three categories of protesters: "everyday citizen," "professional" and "anarchist."

The first identifier under the "everyday citizen" protester immediately acknowledges they are honoring their First Amendment right to organize.

FLORIDA EYES POACHING NYPD OFFICERS AFTER MAMDANI DISMANTLES ELITE UNIT

The NYPD's Special Response Group's cycling training manual identifies three categories of protesters: "everyday citizen," "professional" and "anarchist." (NYPD)

Despite the dismantling of the SRG, Mamdani's plan would still likely require police officers be present at larger demonstrations. The new civilian-led group, however, would be there to attempt to de-escalate the protesters if it should show signs of turning violent.

Mamdani has not yet said when the civilian-led team will be established or take the streets. He also announced the creation of the First Amendment Support Team (FAST), a 30-officer NYPD unit that will help de-escalate crowds while the CDDD is being developed.

Chell, who has announced he will be running for mayor of New York City in 2029 when Mamdani would likely be seeking a second term, said he doubts the new team will take form until at least 2028, if ever.

"What's scary about all this, the mentality of how the mayor and his staff and his socialist friends want to run this city," Chell said.

"If they get everything they want, we're gonna be a Third World city, and I don't know if we can ever get it back."

#5935. ICJ Finds Myanmar Committed Genocide. What Comes Next?

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Q&A: ICJ Finds Myanmar Committed Genocide. What Comes Next?

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

In August 2017, Myanmar’s military launched sweeping “clearance operations” against alleged insurgents in the country’s Rakhine state. Within just three months, security forces had killed thousands of people, razed hundreds of villages, committed widespread sexual violence, and forced some 700,000 Rohingya Muslims to flee into neighboring Bangladesh.

Nine years later, the International Court of Justice (ICJ) found Myanmar guilty of genocide. The landmark ruling, delivered Thursday, marked the first time that the United Nations’ top court has found a country directly responsible for violating the 1948 Genocide Convention.

In August 2017, Myanmar’s military launched sweeping “clearance operations” against alleged insurgents in the country’s Rakhine state. Within just three months, security forces had killed thousands of people, razed hundreds of villages, committed widespread sexual violence, and forced some 700,000 Rohingya Muslims to flee into neighboring Bangladesh.

Nine years later, the International Court of Justice (ICJ) found Myanmar guilty of genocide. The landmark ruling, delivered Thursday, marked the first time that the United Nations’ top court has found a country directly responsible for violating the 1948 Genocide Convention.

Foreign Policy spoke with Tom Andrews, the former U.N. special rapporteur on human rights in Myanmar, a few hours after the ruling came down. He discussed the decision’s implications, including where justice lies for the world’s largest stateless population and how the ruling could affect similar genocide cases linked to the wars in Gaza and Ukraine.

This interview has been edited for length and clarity.

Foreign Policy: Earlier today, the ICJ found that Myanmar’s security forces had committed genocide against the country’s minority Rohingya Muslim community. For those who aren’t aware of the case, could you give a brief primer on the court’s findings?

Tom Andrews: They were very explicit in their findings. They talked about the thousands and thousands of people who were killed, the thousands of women who were raped, the more than 2,000 children who were killed, the hundreds of thousands of homes that were burned.

I was in Bangladesh when people had arrived [in 2017], and I sat and talked to many people who had literally been running for their lives. And the stories they told me were just absolutely incredible. I mean the horror, the brutality, the systematic nature of the killing, of the rapes, of the torture, was just absolutely extraordinary. And, of course, more than 700,000 people escaped into Bangladesh; we know that 1.3 million are now in Bangladesh, in the largest refugee camp in the world.

But what [the Rohingya] did was they pushed for justice and they pushed for accountability. And there were people who were victimized by this brutality, who came to the court, the International Court of Justice, and provided their own personal testimony, told their own stories in a private session, which I believe was a very powerful, powerful part of this whole process, where it allowed the judges to focus on the actual consequences, the human element of this.

FP: This is the first time that the U.N.’s top court has found a nation directly responsible for genocide. What is the significance of that ruling?

TA: History was made today. The standard for genocide is very, very high. And that is a reason why this is so remarkable. This is the first time it’s ever been made, because the standard not only includes the acts but also the intention behind those acts—that [the state] designed to harm a population in whole or in part.

But the question that it begs is, well, OK, now what? Now that we’ve established genocide, are there consequences for genocide, and what are they? And the court has ruled that the regime in Myanmar is responsible for providing relief and compensation for the victims.

I believe that that needs to occur both within the United Nations and outside of the United Nations. Within the United Nations, I believe that the United Nations Security Council should take this up. I think they should pass a resolution. I think that these individuals who were responsible for this genocide should be criminally prosecuted, and so there should be a reference to the International Criminal Court for that criminal prosecution.

I think it’s also important for us to recognize that the killings that continue to go on are happening because the military junta—the generals and [junta leader] Min Aung Hlaing—has access to sophisticated weapons of war that it’s using to attack civilians. I think it’s really important that the international community do everything that it can to cut off that access to those weapons and those weapon materials, and the financing that’s being used to acquire those weapons and those weapon materials, so that we can stop that flow.

Another step that they can take is to reject this rehabilitation campaign that Min Aung Hlaing has been involved in now for the past few months. He’s been traveling to states in the region, to his neighbors, to ASEAN [Association of Southeast Asian Nations] states. He’s been invited into these capitals, welcomed into these capitals. He’s been seeking to gain greater acceptance within ASEAN and the structure of ASEAN. And I think what’s really important is for those member states who have been moving in that direction of accepting or softening their relationship to Min Aung Hlaing and this genocidal regime to reverse those steps and that the regime be as isolated diplomatically as it deserves to be.

FP: Do you think that the ruling will be enough, though, to actually push member states to take that next step and start to reverse the junta’s efforts to cast off its diplomatic pariah status?

TA: I certainly hope so. I mean, this is unprecedented. This is a powerful, powerful moment for the world. I think it’s incredibly important for the world to respond to that finding in a responsible way and that member states and their citizens need to stand up and make their voices very, very clear and very strong that their government needs to do everything that it can to isolate this regime, weaken this regime, take away this regime’s capacity to continue to commit murder against its own people.

I mean, this is a moral imperative, and it is a call for everyone to make a moral stand for not only the Rohingya, not only for the people of Myanmar, but for justice and humanity around the world. Because the thing is, if we establish that there is no consequence for genocide, then we can be guaranteed that genocide is going to happen again. And it’s only by imposing consequences that we can begin to see justice prevail, not only in this case but in future cases.

FP: What’s next for the Rohingya community themselves? The ICJ doesn’t have an enforcement mechanism, but could this case have a tangible impact on the future of that population?

TA: It can, and it should. First of all, the world has turned its back on the Rohingya. Support for the conditions in which they’re living, the refugee camps, has been declining. There have been increased difficulties in just being able to survive in these camps, in part, certainly, by the fact of the international community reversing commitments that it had made and support that it provided to people who are in these conditions for no fault of their own.

Now, I think, with respect to reparations, the idea that the government of Myanmar—the current illegitimate regime of Myanmar—would accept the order of the court to provide compensation is, at best, a very, very remote possibility. In fact, I think that the chances of this happening are exactly zero. So, until there is a new regime that actually is legitimate and believes in justice and accountability and human rights, until that day happens, I think the world has to step forward, and I think there are options the world can take.

For example, the United Nations can establish a reparations fund and a mechanism to manage that. The United States, for example, seized over a billion dollars in assets from this regime. And that billion dollars can be used as part of a compensation fund, and that can then be built upon with governments coming forward to provide additional resources so that there is some form of justice and compensation for those who were victimized by these attacks.

FP: What is the view coming out of Myanmar right now? Is there talk of this case possibly leading to something like regime change?

TA: I’ve heard reports of additional hate speech that’s beginning to be drummed up, some of the nationalists that have used hate speech against the Rohingya in the past, that they are beginning to once again generate that kind of hatred against the Rohingya. It’s a dangerous moment in the country.

FP: Obviously, this case has an outsized impact on Myanmar’s future, but could this ruling affect similar cases at the ICJ on the conflicts in Gaza and Ukraine?

TA: It remains to be seen. The U.S. ambassador to the United Nations [Mike Waltz] posted a tweet in which—I’m paraphrasing here, but [he] accused the court of being poised to lower the standard for genocide, which could have an impact on future cases. And what’s happened is that the court was very explicit in saying that they used the current standard for genocide, including the standard of genocidal intent, in making this judgment. So, in fact, there was no lowering of the standard for genocide that some have accused the court of preparing to do.

I think what’s important is to focus on this case, to focus on this community and on people who were wronged, and to focus on our responsibility to them and to accountability and justice for them and to make sure that there is a consequence for genocide. So I’m hoping that we’re not distracted by what might happen in the future, that we’re focused on what’s happening right now.

I saw a report that a child is killed in these attacks every 29 hours in Myanmar now. So, yes, we need to stop the atrocities from continuing, but we need to focus on responsibility and accountability for these acts and these acts of genocide.

Alexandra Sharp is the World Brief writer at Foreign Policy. Bluesky: @alexandrassharp.bsky.social X: @AlexandraSSharp

#5933. Chicago Mayor Brandon Johnson fights to stay on 2027 ballot amid City Hall exodus, credit warning

Johnson, Tobin reports, could be kicked off the ballot for re-election.

Democratic Chicago Mayor Brandon Johnson is fighting to keep his name on the 2027 ballot with his administration is engulfed in a fiscal and leadership meltdown at City Hall.

Election officials are reviewing challenges to more than 25,000 of Johnson's nominating signatures ahead of the Feb. 23, 2027, mayoral election, a threshold fight unfolding just days after his third chief financial officer in 10 months abruptly resigned, Standard & Poor's placed Chicago's credit rating on negative watch and the City Council voted 45-5 to investigate three years of city financial reporting for possible misstatements.

Despite the mounting pressure, Johnson brushed off questions about whether he will survive the ballot challenge.

"Yeah, feeling just fine. I'm going to be on the ballot," Johnson told ABC 7 Chicago about the challenge. "Looking forward to building the safest, most affordable big city in America, and we're going to continue to do that work."

CHICAGO'S 12-LANGUAGE BALLOTS MAKE ELECTIONS 'HARDER TO MONITOR,' ILLINOIS GOP CHAIR WARNS

Chicago Mayor Brandon Johnson speaks to demonstrators in Grant Park who were protesting the policies of President Donald Trump and showing their support for union labor on May 1, 2025, in Chicago. (Scott Olson/Getty Images)

Johnson needs 12,500 valid signatures to qualify for Chicago's Feb. 23, 2027, mayoral ballot. Hearing officers at the Chicago Board of Elections are reviewing more than 25,000 signatures challenged by supporters of perennial mayoral candidate Willie Wilson.

Johnson's campaign says 6,718 signatures were not challenged and projects that he would finish with 14,788 valid signatures if the first day's 32% validation rate holds. That would put him 2,288 signatures above the requirement, but the figure is the campaign's estimate, not a final board tally. The objectors say early rulings are favoring their challenge, and the review remains unresolved.

The turmoil inside City Hall accelerated after the sudden departure of Chief Financial Officer Ashlee Gabrysch, who said continuing in the role would "compromise my integrity and professional obligations." Johnson later said she "wasn't the right fit."

"It’s yet another high-level change in the mayor’s administration and is a sign of continuing turmoil and a lack of direction at a very key time for the city, especially for him heading into a mayoral election," David Greising, CEO of the Better Government Association, told CBS Chicago after Gabrysch's abrupt exit.

A survey of 1,108 adults by NORC at the University of Chicago, conducted Sept. 13-23, found that 22.2% approved of Johnson's job performance, while 52.9% disapproved and 24.8% had no opinion. The survey predates both the current petition review and Gabrysch's departure.

4 OF NATION'S LARGEST CITIES NOW LED BY SOCIALIST MAYORS AFTER TUESDAY'S ELECTION, AFFECTING TENS OF MILLIONS

Chicago residents are calling for more police as crime continues to rise in the Windy City. (Fox News Digital)

Johnson's proposed $17.6 billion 2027 budget is intended to close an $882.4 million gap, but a 28-member City Council bloc told him it did not see the votes needed to pass his tax proposals.

Standard & Poor's ultimately placed Chicago's BBB general-obligation rating on negative CreditWatch, citing the budget stalemate and management instability. The agency said there was at least a 50% chance of a downgrade within 90 days, which could raise the city's borrowing costs.

The City Council eventually voted 45-5 on Wednesday to authorize an independent review of three years of financial reporting for possible material or intentional misstatements.

Chicago Mayor Brandon Johnson speaks during a news conference March 25, 2026, in Chicago (AP Photo/Erin Hooley)

They did not respond by publication.

#5932. The Fantasy of a Turkish Middle East

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

MELIHA BENLI ALTUNISIK is Professor of International Relations at Middle East Technical University.

Ankara’s Risky Bid to Impose Regional Order

Since Hamas’s October 7, 2023, attack on Israel, the future of the Middle East has been fiercely contested. The previous Gulf-centered order had Saudi Arabia and the United Arab Emirates (UAE) trying to stabilize and shape the region using economic statecraft and the normalization of relations with former foes in Iran, Israel, Turkey, and Syria. Three years of war in Gaza, and more recently in Iran and the Gulf, have severely disrupted that order-building project and generated a deep sense of uncertainty about what comes next.

Turkey, like every major power in the Middle East, has tried to fill the vacuum. Before 2023, Ankara’s influence had been in decline; after years of diplomatic isolation following the Arab uprisings, it had just begun repairing its ties with major regional actors. But in the past few years, Turkey has earned American backing for its policies in Syria and its regional diplomacy, and it has made an effort to secure the home front by pursuing a peace process with the Kurdistan Workers’ Party (PKK), a militant group that has fought a decades-long insurgency against the Turkish state. Most recently, in August, Pakistan, Saudi Arabia, and Turkey signed the Mecca Defense Alliance, a collective security agreement that institutionalizes Turkey’s regional positioning and pulls the country directly into Saudi Arabia’s war with the Houthis.

Turkey’s post–October 7 strategy has restored the country’s position as a regional heavyweight. But it is not capable of meeting its larger objective: establishing order on its own terms. Turkey, like the region’s other aspiring leaders, lacks the power to impose a new security framework on its own. Its nascent efforts to build one, such as the new Mecca pact, merely make countries outside the bloc feel unsafe, feeding instability. Turkey’s most ambitious plays for regional leadership are therefore likely to prove ineffective or even counterproductive. As long as the Middle East’s highly militarized, zero-sum security dynamics remain, Ankara would be better off concentrating on interests closer to home that it has the power to protect.

This is not the first time that Turkey has tried to shape the broader Middle Eastern order. Ankara saw the Arab uprisings in the early 2010s as a historic window of opportunity. The ruling Justice and Development Party (AKP) described the political transformation sweeping the region as “the people’s coming to power,” and the Turkish government expected that eventual elections in the Arab countries rocked by protest would bring into office parties aligned with the Muslim Brotherhood, which was ideologically sympathetic and had practical links to the AKP. The emergence of friendly governments, the thinking went, would increase Turkey’s influence in the region.

Yet these hopes soon receded. Egypt’s Muslim Brotherhood–affiliated president, Mohamed Morsi, was overthrown in 2013, and Turkey’s bet on the opposition movement in Syria failed as Bashar al-Assad clung to power. Turkey was left isolated. A rival axis composed of Egypt, Saudi Arabia, and the UAE objected to Turkey’s Muslim Brotherhood ties and sought to limit Ankara’s influence. This group contested Turkey’s positions across the Middle East, North Africa, the eastern Mediterranean, and the Horn of Africa, effectively fighting proxy battles with Turkey in conflicts in Libya, Somalia, and Syria.

Then, the attacks on October 7, 2023, ushered in another period of upheaval. Some changes since have played in Turkey’s favor. Chief among them is the collapse of the Assad regime in Syria in December 2024 at the hands of opposition groups led by Hayat Tahrir al-Sham. Turkey had arguably invested more than any other country in pursuit of regime change in Damascus; it assisted opposition groups for years. Yet as the years went by, that objective seemed increasingly out of reach. The region’s other major powers made their peace with the regime’s survival, and even Ankara began considering rapprochement with Assad to avoid being left out in the cold. Assad’s fall changed Turkey’s fortunes overnight, handing it a level of influence in Syria it had not held in more than a decade.

Other developments proved more dangerous for Ankara. Israel’s foreign policy became more assertive and revisionist after October 7, and its expansive security objectives began to clash with Turkey’s own. Turkish President Recep Tayyip Erdogan’s sharp criticism of Israeli policies in Gaza strained the relationship, and it deteriorated further when Turkey expanded its foothold in post-Assad Syria and Israel came to view Ankara’s military presence there as a direct threat. As Israel continues to strike sites in Syria—attacks Ankara has condemned—the risk of escalation is only growing. A miscalculation on either side could lead to a direct clash between Israeli and Turkish forces.

The U.S.-Israeli war with Iran, meanwhile, has cut both ways for Turkey. While Tehran’s attention has been focused elsewhere, Ankara has welcomed Iran’s retreat from Syria and the chance to push back against its remaining influence in Iraq. Turkey also has no interest in seeing a nuclear-armed Iran upend the regional balance of power. Yet the prospect of chaos across Turkey’s eastern border, should the Iranian regime fall, has bred real anxiety in Ankara, as has the possibility that Iran could become emboldened if the regime emerges from the war intact.

In this complex environment, Ankara has pursued a three-pronged strategy to protect its security interests and, it hopes, capitalize on opportunities the upheaval has created. Since U.S. President Donald Trump’s return to power, Erdogan has drawn Turkey closer to the United States and tried to use his relationship with Washington to advance Ankara’s regional policies. Turkey has also pressed ahead with regional normalization, building on efforts to repair ties with Saudi Arabia, Egypt, and other key Arab countries in the early 2020s, with the aim to forge a broader regional bloc. And, domestically, Ankara launched the “Turkey Without Terror” process, an attempt to resolve the conflict with the PKK inside and outside Turkey, which Turkey’s adversaries have long exploited by arming Kurdish groups or backing them politically.

Some of Ankara’s policies have borne fruit. Consider, for instance, the outreach to Washington. Erdogan’s close relations with Trump helped bring the United States on board with Turkey’s expanded role in Syria. Turkey was one of the four countries Washington brought in to help negotiate the U.S.-proposed October 2025 cease-fire agreement between Israel and Hamas, and as a result, Trump offered and Erdogan accepted a seat on the U.S. president’s so-called Board of Peace for Gaza’s reconstruction. Turkey has played an active part mediating between the United States and Iran during the latest war. And Trump publicly endorsed the Mecca Defense Alliance, calling it “big, bold, and important.” All of this points to a recognition in Washington of Turkey’s regional importance, the U.S. administration’s willingness to support some of Ankara’s priorities, and Turkey’s determination to elevate its own position.

Yet there are undeniable limits to Turkey’s quest for influence. At a February 2026 Board of Peace summit, for instance, Turkey was excluded from participating in a planned international troop deployment and police training in Gaza—a sign that warming relations with Washington were not enough to override Israeli objections to having a Turkish military footprint in the Palestinian territory. In Syria, similarly, the support Turkey has secured from the Trump administration has not stopped Israel from launching military strikes at Syrian targets near Turkish positions. Simply put, Turkey has not been able to use its relationship with the Trump administration to overcome the limit Israeli opposition places on the scope of Turkey’s regional role.

Turkey’s regional normalization efforts have yielded mixed results, too. Restoring diplomatic ties with significant players such as Saudi Arabia is in Turkey’s interest, to be sure. But the alliances Ankara is entering with these countries risk making Turkey and the region less secure, not more. The Mecca pact with Pakistan and Saudi Arabia could end up hardening rival blocs and elevating regional tensions. Turkey insists that the alliance is directed against no country in particular and has suggested that Egypt, Syria, or even Iran could eventually join. But in the meantime, Iran, Israel, and the Houthis could see it as a threat and try to test or weaken its members’ commitment.

Furthermore, the pact could pull Turkey into other countries’ wars. That possibility is already on the table now that the Turkish parliament has ratified the agreement and the Houthis are escalating their attacks on Saudi Arabia. The Turkish government has insisted that any military support it provided would be confined to deterrence, intelligence sharing, and defense-industrial cooperation. Yet Saudi Arabia is now seeking Pakistan’s and Turkey’s help with air defense and territorial protection, and the three states have agreed to prepare forces for rapid deployment. Sending Turkish troops to Saudi Arabia would still require separate parliamentary approval. If parliament allowed it, even a limited deployment would risk drawing Turkey into a wider regional conflict.

Some kind of regional security framework is necessary. The problem is that no country in the region, including Turkey, has the power to impose a security order on the rest, so whenever someone tries, other countries feel threatened. An informal cooperation framework that is open to all countries in the region—and that asks its members to commit to nonaggression instead of making mutual-defense pledges—might work better. But the assembly and functioning of such a coalition require trust that governments in the region do not have. Breaking the impasse would likely require the United States to play a more constructive role than it has in recent years—not simply endorsing whatever regional arrangement emerges, as Trump did with the Mecca pact, or pressuring countries to normalize relations with Israel without expecting Israel to change its policies in return, as Trump has also done. Instead, Washington, perhaps with the help of Beijing, would need to compel regional rivals such as Iran and Saudi Arabia to take confidence-building measures that would make an inclusive security framework viable in the long run. Without this external push, the narrower security blocs that emerge in the region will only lead to further instability.

Ankara’s strongest cards lie closer to home. The Kurdish peace process has moved slowly since February 2025, when Abdullah Ocalan, the imprisoned leader of the PKK, called on the militant group to disarm. But it has yielded tangible progress. In May 2025, the PKK announced that it would dissolve. In Syria, Kurdish forces are being integrated into the Syrian army, and Mazloum Abdi, the commander of the People’s Defense Units, a Kurdish armed group affiliated with the PKK, was appointed as a presidential adviser in August. Syria’s postconflict transition remains fragile, and the reconciliation between Damascus and Kurdish militants in the northeast may owe as much to American pressure as it does to Turkey’s domestic efforts to make peace with the PKK. But for Turkey, the eventual integration of Syrian Kurdish forces into the Syrian state removes the risk Ankara saw in having an autonomous, armed Kurdish entity on its southern border. And, given the close security and economic ties Ankara has developed with Damascus, as well as the political and legal steps the government can take at home to complete the disbanding of the PKK and to deepen democratic reforms, Turkey has the ability to keep these encouraging developments on track.

Turkey’s regional policies are most successful when the country plays to its strengths—not when it is trying to throw its weight around the region. In the first decade of the twenty-first century, Ankara built real influence by mediating between Syria and Israel and between Palestinian factions, expanding its economic engagement in the Levant, and proposing projects to connect the region’s energy and transportation infrastructure. Military power, meanwhile, has proved most effective when applied for specific purposes, such as to secure the border with Syria. This combination is Turkey’s winning formula: diplomacy and economic outreach to extend its influence, and limited force to protect its core interests. Broad, open-ended commitments, by contrast, risk drawing Turkey into regional conflicts without making it safer.

Today, sticking to a more effective formula does not mean retreating from regional politics. It means setting priorities and planning long-term. Consolidating the Kurdish peace process, keeping Washington invested in Syria’s stabilization, managing the risk of confrontation with Israel in Syria, and maintaining constructive relations with the Kurdistan Regional Government in northern Iraq and the Iraqi government in Baghdad are key to keeping Turkey’s borders secure and are within Turkey’s ability to achieve. Ankara can continue to use its experience in mediation when opportunities to advance peace processes present themselves. It can also promote economic connectivity, both by expanding trade ties and by supporting regional infrastructure projects such as the Iraq-Europe Development Road and a proposed pipeline network connecting the Gulf to Jordan, Syria, and Turkey. It need not shy away from security cooperation with the Gulf, either, if it can engage without making formal commitments. Yet in all these endeavors, Ankara should remember that it has the most clout when it stays out of the region’s conflicts.

The Middle East’s escalating wars and rival blocs could make a narrower Turkish security policy more difficult to sustain today than it was in the past. Defending Ankara’s interests and playing a constructive role in a volatile neighborhood will require a careful balance. The best way Turkey can contribute to regional stability may be to avoid trying to do too much.

The Overlooked Costs of a Transactional Foreign Policy

Even on the Sidelines, Ankara Faces Blowback

How Syria Can Capitalize on the Post-Hormuz Economy

Beijing’s Overreach Threatens the Détente With America

U.S. Dealmaking Is Strengthening the Chavista Regime

Only a Radical Course Change Can Win Back Its Most Important Ally

Arab Citizens of Israel Can Bring Change, but They Can’t Do It Alone

How to Defuse Looming Threats to U.S. National Security

#5931. What the Yalta Comparison Really Reveals About Shenzhen

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Analysis: What the Yalta Comparison Really Reveals About Shenzhen

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

The moment that the trilateral summit in Shenzhen, China, was planned, commentators began comparing it to the 1945 Yalta Conference. Unsurprisingly, this narrative was particularly fueled by Moscow, where the British-Soviet-U.S. “Big Three” summit stands for the codification of Soviet control over half of Europe. Pyotr Tolstoy, the head of the foreign affairs committee in the Russian parliament, called the summit a “new Yalta.” Ultranationalist ideologue Alexander Dugin found the meeting “deeply symbolic,” while the Kremlin boasted that “the significance and symbolism of this meeting are hard to overestimate.”

The Kremlin was right about the symbolism, not least because this week’s summit produced little else. The joint “Declaration Toward Peace” adopted by U.S. President Donald Trump, Chinese President Xi Jinping, and Russian President Vladimir Putin says practically nothing. It pledges United Nations reform without specifics, future fuel shipments without volumes or deadlines, and a dialogue on nuclear risks without a clear path forward. Its most pointed passage is a warning to the International Criminal Court not to abuse its jurisdiction.

The moment that the trilateral summit in Shenzhen, China, was planned, commentators began comparing it to the 1945 Yalta Conference. Unsurprisingly, this narrative was particularly fueled by Moscow, where the British-Soviet-U.S. “Big Three” summit stands for the codification of Soviet control over half of Europe. Pyotr Tolstoy, the head of the foreign affairs committee in the Russian parliament, called the summit a “new Yalta.” Ultranationalist ideologue Alexander Dugin found the meeting “deeply symbolic,” while the Kremlin boasted that “the significance and symbolism of this meeting are hard to overestimate.”

The Kremlin was right about the symbolism, not least because this week’s summit produced little else. The joint “Declaration Toward Peace” adopted by U.S. President Donald Trump, Chinese President Xi Jinping, and Russian President Vladimir Putin says practically nothing. It pledges United Nations reform without specifics, future fuel shipments without volumes or deadlines, and a dialogue on nuclear risks without a clear path forward. Its most pointed passage is a warning to the International Criminal Court not to abuse its jurisdiction.

Nothing about this is surprising. The vagueness on arms control reflects China’s lack of interest in it amid a rapid build-out of its nuclear forces. Ukraine is not mentioned, because Russia has made it repeatedly clear that it is not ready for peace in Europe on anything but its maximalist demands. Iran, too, was absent from the agenda, given Russian and Chinese support for Iran and the embarrassing cul-de-sac that the war represents for Trump.

But substance was hardly the point. The mere image of the three leaders sitting together made the summit significant—and made capitals from Taipei to Kyiv to Tokyo nervous that the three might strike a private understanding over their heads.

To understand what yesterday’s summit really meant, Yalta is indeed a useful comparison—but mainly for the differences that it reveals.

The first is the diminished stature of today’s threesome. The Big Three met as the victors of a war that had left much of Europe and Asia in ruins. What they decided at Yalta—and at the Potsdam Conference a few months later—drew lines that would last for more than four decades. The three leaders who met in Shenzhen are weak, each in his own way, and each of them needed a photo-op to project a strength that he does not fully possess.

China was not invited to Yalta because it was on the menu: U.S. President Franklin D. Roosevelt and Soviet leader Joseph Stalin agreed on Soviet rights over the Manchurian railways and ports of Dairen and Port Arthur without consulting the Republic of China’s leader, Chiang Kai-shek.

Eighty-one years later, a different China was the host. The location was carefully chosen: In 1980, when it became China’s first special economic zone, Shenzhen was a small town across the border from Hong Kong, whereas today it is one of the world’s leading technology and manufacturing hubs. But the rise that Shenzhen embodies is losing pace. The International Monetary Fund (IMF) expects Chinese economic growth to slow to 4.5 percent this year and 4 percent in 2027 as weak domestic demand and a real estate crisis weigh on the economy. Demographic decline will shrink the country’s population by 786 million by 2100. The flashy summit was also a way to distract the world from the change in China’s trajectory.

For Putin, the gain of face was even greater, given that by any material measure, Russia plays in a much smaller league. The IMF projects the U.S. GDP at $32.6 trillion in 2026 and China’s at $21.5 trillion. Russia’s is only about $2.7 trillion, roughly the size of Italy’s. Military spending tells the same story. In 2025, the United States spent $954 billion, China an estimated $336 billion, and Russia $190 billion. European NATO members together spent $559 billion, almost three times as much as Russia.

Putin’s seat in Shenzhen was therefore the dividend of nearly two decades of growing, calculated aggressiveness. Georgia, Syria, the 2014 and 2022 invasions of Ukraine, and the current hybrid attacks across Europe are a sequence of moves resting on the same bet. Moscow has converted Europe’s passivity into status, compensating for what it lacks in economic and military weight with a willingness to use force that none of its rivals share.

The bet paid off because Europe preferred to ignore the implications. Germany shrugged off Russia’s 2014 invasion of Crimea and the Donbas and moved forward with Nord Stream 2, thereby entrenching the continent’s energy dependence on Moscow. Since 2022, Europe has financed Ukraine’s defense without ever resolving to push for Kyiv’s victory. And Washington bears its share in encouraging Putin, from former President Barack Obama’s impotent red line in Syria in 2013 to Trump granting Moscow sanctions relief and a seat among the great powers before any settlement of Russia’s war.

For Trump, the summit was a welcome respite from a string of setbacks. On Nov. 3, Democrats won control of both the House and the Senate, winning various Republican strongholds for the first time in generations. The botched attack on Iran contributed to this electoral punishment, not least through its impact on gas prices.

Add to this that Trump has never hidden his admiration for leaders who govern without democratic constraints. A seat at the table with two of them, with all the ceremony that came with it, was worth a great deal to a president facing a hostile Congress and having few foreign successes to show for the year. “It’s a big win for us,” he told reporters as the meeting opened.

The strategic environment could hardly be more different from 1945. The leaders who met then were looking to consolidate the status quo that had emerged from the war, which they all found acceptable. By contrast, the three powers that met in Shenzhen are, each in its own way, fundamentally revisionist.

China has its sights on Taiwan and wresting technological leadership from the United States. Russia has its war in Europe and a relationship with Beijing that becomes more lopsided every year. The United States is renegotiating its alliances and trade policy while stuck in a quagmire in Iran. The U.S.-China trade truce expires in January, and the Nvidia scandal shows how far the technology rivalry now reaches. All three are navigating a strategic environment in flux, which each sees as an intermediate stop to improve its own position.

The third and deepest difference is vividly depicted by Europe’s absence. At Yalta, Europe sat among the victors. France, following four years under German occupation and the collaborationist Vichy regime, was of course not invited. For Poland and much of Central Europe, the conference is a reminder of a settlement imposed at its expense. But British Prime Minister Winston Churchill spoke for a European power that had fought the war since 1939—and the order that he helped design, from the postwar occupation of Germany to the creation of the United Nations, was one with freedom at its very core.

In Shenzhen, no European leader was in the room, the visible outcome of decades of strategic decline. The meeting took place on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation, a forum of 21 Pacific Rim economies of which no European Union member state is a member.

In fact, each of the three leaders at the table is, in different ways, working against Europe. China does it through trade: In 2025, the EU ran a deficit of 359.8 billion euros with China; the largest category of imports was mechanical appliances, electrical machinery, and equipment, the industrial core that Europe is trying to protect.

Russia does it through force: Its war against Ukraine is in its fifth year, and its hybrid campaign of sabotage, drone incursions, disinformation, and intimidation now reaches deep into EU territory.

The United States does it through ideological disdain, made crystal clear by Vice President J.D. Vance’s speech at the 2025 Munich Security Conference and by a National Security Strategy that warned of Europe’s “civilizational erasure” and committed Washington to supporting “patriotic” (read: far-right) parties on the continent, on top of tariffs on European goods at levels unseen in the postwar trans-Atlantic relationship.

This genuinely anti-European stance was the only real common denominator in Shenzhen—a hostility fueled by Europe’s weakness, to be sure, but even more by a shared ideology of power. Yalta was a meeting of champions of different worldviews. Roosevelt and Churchill embodied the liberal democracies that were about to defeat Nazism. Stalin embodied a revolutionary state with a rival vision of political and economic order and ultimately of humanity itself. Within three years, this ideological divergence would harden into the Cold War. For all its ill effects on the nations caught on the Soviet side, Yalta is still an icon of pragmatism across an ideological divide.

Shenzhen was the exact opposite. The three leaders do not lack conflicting interests. Washington wants Beijing in a trilateral arms control framework that the Chinese Foreign Ministry calls “neither fair, reasonable nor realistic.” The United States is at war with Iran, and the disruption of shipping through the Strait of Hormuz hits China’s energy supplies. Russia and China are far from strategic allies, and diplomatic protocol in Shenzhen showed how unequal their relationship has become.

But on the question that divided the room at Yalta—namely, how power should be constrained and legitimized—the three leaders in Shenzhen are closer to one another than any of them is to almost every other leader across the world. All three think of sovereignty as the right of strong states to act without external constraint. All three read the world in terms of spheres of influence and privilege bilateral deals to multilateral rules.

Finally, all three regard checks and balances, independent courts, and a free press as nuisances and obstacles at home and abroad. It is telling that one of the four points of their declaration—and the only one that wasn’t lukewarm boilerplate—defends the immunity of state officials and warns the International Criminal Court against abusing its jurisdiction.

In this sense, all three are anti-Western in the deepest sense of the term. For Xi and Putin, rejecting the underlying idea of the West is long-standing doctrine, but the United States—the country that once defined and defended the West—now shares much of the same inclination.

This is how the world has changed since 1945. At Yalta, ideological opposites negotiated a balance of power. In Shenzhen, three leaders with a shared worldview but competing interests discussed the affairs of the world, producing symbolism rather than substance. The two superpowers and their Russian sidekick were in a bubble, with neither the West nor anyone else represented in the room.

For all the lack of substance, that may be the summit’s most important outcome: If what we saw this week portends a lasting realignment of the United States, this summit’s legacy could be far greater than the one to which it was so eagerly compared.

Beniamino Irdi is a nonresident senior fellow at the German Marshall Fund of the United States, the founder and CEO of HighGround, and a former Italian government official.

#5929. Millions could lose paid benefits due to Congress ignoring decades of Social Security warnings: experts

Andrew Biggs, a senior fellow at the American Enterprise Institute, and Romina Boccia, the Cato Institute’s director of budget and entitlement policy, warned that Congress’ 50 years of action have led Social Security to the cliff of insolvency.

A panel filled with top economists, including a Nobel Peace Prize winner, warned Congress decades ago that its reforms to Social Security could spell disaster.

Now, experts tell Fox News Digital that the group from a bygone era was right as the safety net program hurtles toward a crisis that could affect millions.

"Social Security benefits were put on autopilot back in 1977," Andrew Biggs, a senior fellow of the American Enterprise Institute, told Fox News Digital. "Since then, Congress has done as little as it possibly could."

Senate Budget Committee Chair Ron Johnson, R-Wis., unveiled a report on the decades-old program that blasted it as a "Ponzi scheme" and warned that Social Security’s slide into insolvency began in the 1970s, when, despite warnings, lawmakers opted to tie benefit growth to wages rather than inflation.

AMERICANS MISLED ON SOCIAL SECURITY FOR DECADES AS CLOCK COUNTS DOWN ON BENEFITS, EXPLOSIVE REPORT REVEALS

President Jimmy Carter shakes hands with Louisiana Sen. Russell Long after signing the Social Security bill at the White House in Washington on Dec. 20, 1977. (Getty Archive)

Johnson wrote in his report that, at the time, an expert panel was formed that warned lawmakers that if they were to tie benefits to inflation, "Social Security would likely be sustainable today."

Still, lawmakers at the time ran with their own amendments to the program, which were signed into law by former President Jimmy Carter in December 1977.

"What they said in 1977 was if you index benefit growth to wages, you're effectively building a solvency crisis into the program," Biggs said. "You are over-promising, you're going to build in benefits that the current system can't afford to pay and you're committing future taxpayers to pay rates of taxes that the taxpayers in 1977 weren't willing to pay."

In the decades since, Congress has done little to change the program.

SENATE REPUBLICANS BREAK THROUGH WASHINGTON GRIDLOCK WITH MASSIVE AMERICAN ENERGY, DATA CENTER OVERHAUL

Romina Boccia, the Cato Institute’s director of budget and entitlement policy, told Fox News Digital that’s largely because lawmakers have not had the political will to do it.

She agreed with Johnson that Social Security was effectively a "Ponzi scheme," and said "there's this misconception that a lot of Americans think that Social Security is a retirement savings program."

"But for that, you would actually need to have savings occur in the system, and there would need to be assets that would be invested in earning returns," Boccia said. "That's not the case. We're talking about a pay-as-you-go program."

One of the side effects of Congress’ lack of action to rein in the program is that the wage growth formula relies on workers in the economy contributing with their paychecks.

Boccia noted that as the decades have gone on, "we've seen a severe decline in the number of workers compared to the number of retirees collecting benefits as a result of a decline in fertility."

DEMOCRATS COULD ENTER CONGRESSIONAL MAJORITY IN A GOVERNMENT SHUTDOWN

Sen. Ron Johnson, R-Wis., makes his way to votes in the U.S. Capitol Sept. 30, 2026. (CQ-Roll Call, Inc. via Getty Images)

Congress’ last real successful effort to reform Social Security came in the early 1980s, Biggs noted, when lawmakers were forced to step in to deal with the "funding crisis" hitting the program.

"They raised taxes. They increased the retirement age. So, they did this with months to spare to fix Social Security," Biggs said. "It's not something they wanted to do, but they were forced to do.

"Now, in 2032, we're going to face the same situation, where Congress will be forced to take action sometime before the trust fund runs out in 2032, not because they want to, but because they're forced to do it," he continued.

If Social Security’s trust fund runs dry by 2032, Americans’ benefits would be slashed, and the onus of paying for the shortfall would add trillions more to the nation’s debt.

But Johnson, in his report, declared that the Social Security problem could not be fixed between now and then because of Congress’ inaction for the last several decades. And he suggested the solution would be to repeal a possible benefits reduction as the program’s trust fund heads toward insolvency in just under seven years.

Biggs, who was part of former President George W. Bush’s effort to reform Social Security over two decades ago, said that, had Congress taken action "25 years ago, you could fix Social Security in barely noticeable ways."

"There is no easy fix to Social Security today, and that stems entirely from the fact that, every year, when Congress has had the opportunity to fix Social Security, they chose to duck the issue for political reasons and kick the can down the road," Biggs said.

Boccia disagreed with Johnson’s solution and contended that the real answer was one that lawmakers of yesterday and today would be hesitant to approach — raising taxes.

"The real way to think about this is that there's almost no way to imagine Congress would let those benefit cuts take effect," Boccia said. "It will be so politically toxic. So, most likely they're going to try to find a different solution.

"The last time the trust fund was running out of money in 1982, Congress put together a commission and then voted on a bill that increased taxes and reduced benefits, including raising the social security retirement age," she continued.

"The problem is that the funding gap now is much larger, more than two times bigger than it was in the ‘80s."

Alex Miller is a writer for Fox News Digital covering the U.S. Senate.
