#5927. Why BAAJA Matters

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Q&A: Why BAAJA Matters

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Prefer to listen? To hear this entire conversation, and more episodes in the weeks ahead, follow Ones and Tooze wherever you get your podcasts.

The U.S. Senate just cleared the way for passing the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act, more commonly referred to as BAAJA. The law aims to shorten review timelines for infrastructure projects and protect permits from later revocation, among other provisions. But beyond the technical details, the law has been notable for the unusual coalitions that have arranged in favor and opposed to it: Oil and gas companies, manufacturers, and labor unions are broadly in support, while environmental groups such as the Sierra Club and the Wilderness Society and progressive groups such as the NAACP have lined up against.

What exactly would BAAJA do? Why has the law divided some trade unions against progressives? And can renewable energy outcompete fossil fuels on the free market?

The U.S. Senate just cleared the way for passing the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act, more commonly referred to as BAAJA. The law aims to shorten review timelines for infrastructure projects and protect permits from later revocation, among other provisions. But beyond the technical details, the law has been notable for the unusual coalitions that have arranged in favor and opposed to it: Oil and gas companies, manufacturers, and labor unions are broadly in support, while environmental groups such as the Sierra Club and the Wilderness Society and progressive groups such as the NAACP have lined up against.

What exactly would BAAJA do? Why has the law divided some trade unions against progressives? And can renewable energy outcompete fossil fuels on the free market?

Those are just a few of the questions that came up in my recent conversation with FP economics columnist Adam Tooze on the podcast we co-host, Ones and Tooze. What follows is an excerpt, edited for length and clarity. For the full conversation, look for Ones and Tooze wherever you get your podcasts. And check out Adam’s Substack newsletter.

Cameron Abadi: What exactly would BAAJA do? And why is this just such a big issue right now in the United States?

Adam Tooze: It’s basically an effort to streamline environmental review, restrict certain legal challenges, make permits—once they are issued—more secure. So, enabling investors to proceed robustly. And it’s aiming basically to enable a more rapid build-out of the electricity transmission system.

It’s such a big deal because this is a really big problem. Electricity interconnection is a fiendishly difficult technical and legal issue. The Berkeley Lab found that about 2,600 gigawatts of generation and storage projects were seeking grid connection at the end of 2023. And more will have come online since. That’s about twice the existing capacity of the United States’ system. And they spend about five years at least applying for interconnection and entering commercial operation, compared to two years only as recently as a few decades ago. You’ve got projects like a giant wind project in New Mexico, which is supposed to be a transmission line that connects New Mexico to Arizona. It took roughly 17 years from initial development to construction, and the vast majority of that was in the permitting. Pipelines are notorious for exactly the same reasons. So, Mountain Valley Pipeline, which is a gas pipeline 303 miles [long through West Virginia and Virginia], originally billed at $3.5 billion and now finally opened in 2024 at a cost of about $8 billion.

So there are a fundamental set of issues about not only how American procedures decide whether they are for or against big infrastructure projects but also how long it takes them and how expensive they are. If we had a streamlined process, you could process a bunch of obstructions, do it quickly, make a decision, move on. And instead, we have sort of the worst of both worlds, which is a lack of coherence, political chops, and changes by administrations, which are very pro-renewable energy, for instance, and then very opposed to it—which means that even when you do get the permits, they’re not secure. So it’s a mess.

And this is a mess also against the backdrop of the last effort to break this impasse, which after all was the Biden administration. It’s certainly true now that AI is driving the conversation about the build-out of power supply in the United States. But the conversation started earlier with the push around Bidenomics, which then was instantiated in the form of the Inflation Reduction Act in 2022. And people like Brian Deese, who was a key architect of green industrial policy under the Biden administration, have said that, you know, passing the Inflation Reduction Act without permitting reform produced modest results. Passing the Inflation Reduction Act with permitting reform would have made a huge difference. Passing the permitting reform two years before the Inflation Reduction Act had come into effect, setting the stage for the energy and the money that poured through the Inflation Reduction Act, would have been a complete game-changer. In other words, the pace of rollout would have been very dramatic. And I think that, in a sense, is what this conversation currently is about.

And it’s furthermore crucial because it does expose fault lines within the coalitions that you might think of as being supportive to this. So Bidenomics glued a coalition together around the green energy project, around American reindustrialization, but also through extraordinarily complex coalition deals—the “everything bagel” liberalism, patching in a whole variety of different environmental justice groups, activists on behalf of rural America, activists on the behalf of minorities in the United States and underrepresented and discriminated-against communities—and they were all part of the Biden package. [Donald] Trump has blown that up. And what we’re seeing here is also a debate, I think, about what are the terms going to be under which a new, energized Democratic majority actually attempts to restart the project of green modernization in the United States. That’s why this matters beyond the technicalities.

CA: We tend to think of the left and unions as a natural coalition. But have there always been tensions between them that run along the fault lines of environmentalism, and is that what’s getting revealed in the debate around this law?

AT: In support of what I think is called BAAJA, you do see an unusually broad coalition of organized labor. It isn’t just building trade unions—sort of, you know, construction-happy pipeline workers or whatever. It is in fact a very broad coalition, including the AFL-CIO. And so, from their point of view, this is clearly an industrial, productivist kind of strategy. And it makes sense, and they’re not wrong. And the estimates are that this would move significant volumes of investment. Not stupendous, not dramatic. If you’ve actually seen the econometrics on this, the numbers are not overwhelming. But they’re real, and every little bit helps. And the obstruction around these issues has been highly significant in a number of individual cases. So the coalition is real.

But you’re right, there are also blocking oppositionists. In fact, it’s telling that in the Senate vote on the legislation so far the purple state senators—so senators who are exposed to more competitive races—were notably more supportive of this legislation than those in absolutely solid blue Democratic constituencies, where the leverage of other interests really came to the fore. And there are at least two that are very well worth taking seriously that have been articulated.

One is Bill McKibben, one of the real warhorses of the climate movement in the United States, who said, look, it’s all very well to say that we need an energy transition, so we need more electrical generation capacity and transmission facilities. This is clear. It is true. On the other hand, ultimately what counts here is the level of emissions. And so, more construction, more activity in this realm, is not an unalloyed and unambiguous benefit. And if it’s possible that these changes might in fact open the door to large-scale fossil build-out, then, in fact, this is really an ambiguous a set of proposals. And it’s quite clear that in its bipartisan nature, a little bit like some of the Biden administration legislation as well, they are trying to provide pork and trying to buy support from energy interests across the board.

And then the other constituency that you might think of as left is represented by a spokesperson like Abigail Dillen of Earthjustice, who is representing what in the American conversation is called an environmental justice point of view, first and foremost. And so, just for clarification’s sake, environmental politics is a very broad church. Climate politics within that comes in different shades as well, but climate politics has this very macroscopic, ultimately global point of view. And one of the answers given by the establishment with regards to climate politics is simply electrify everything, build as much clean energy infrastructure as possible as quickly as possible. But the consequences of that locally can of course be very, very disruptive, not to say incredibly damaging. And this is where the environmental justice movement comes in in the United States, which has a genealogy which really originates in the 1980s and 1990s, centrally concerned with the interests of marginalized communities in the United States, notably Black communities in the American South, who find themselves systematically in harm’s way when it comes to otherwise perhaps interesting and important industrial expansion. And so her argument, and the opposition of environmental justice campaigners around this, is to say, well, it’s all very well that you open the door to this kind of development, but who walks through that door? Which interests are going to take advantage of this, and who is going to lose out?

And to go back to your big-picture historical question, what you’re seeing there is a confrontation between, if you like, three generations of American radical and progressive politics. One is the classic New Deal productivist coalition, which is about a bigger government and facilitating public-private partnerships in various ways that enable large-scale investment and development. And that had very considerable buy-in from trade unions.

Expanding the U.S. grid is vital to staying globally competitive.

But what we see from the 1960s onward—not necessarily in hostility to organized labor but recognizing the impact of the success of this kind of development and its lopsidedness and its egalitarian tendencies—are the other two constituencies of American progressivism, which are now speaking their voice. These things didn’t emerge at the same time in the 1930s, but you could say in the 1960s we see the development of the ecological movement in the United States, the broad-based opposition to environmental damage, which in many cases has a distinctly upper-middle-class, Sierra Club kind of feel to it.

And then from the late 1970s, 1980s, onward, the environmental justice movement, which is in a sense environmental politics crossed with the Civil Rights Movement in the United States and focusing on environmental issues. And those three things together come out of the same tradition of American progressivism, you might say, but they are not necessarily on the same page. And their targets are not necessarily the same thing either. And the work that Bidenomics did was to, in some senses, square that circle. And as we’ve said, that’s the coalition that needs to be rebuilt. And this is a conversation about how that gets done.

CA: One often hears that renewables now outcompete other sources of energy on economic terms. So why shouldn’t advocates for renewables prefer a law that would presumably create a freer market?

AT: Well, those kind of claims are based on what are so-called comparisons of unsubsidized levelized costs of new electricity generation. That’s the standard measure. And outfits like Lazard have produced numbers that show, for instance, that onshore wind can be had for a cost of between $37 and $99 per megawatt hour, utility-scale solar for between $40 and $98, combined-cycle natural gas for $51 to $129, and so on and so forth.

And when you make the comparisons on that basis, it’s generally true that solar and onshore wind come out as the optimum strategies. But they’re very, very partial measures of cost. Because what they don’t really account for is intermittency fundamentally. Right now, we really shouldn’t be making comparisons of just pure solar and wind versus gas because gas can be turned on and off once you’ve got the infrastructure. Whereas obviously solar and rain can’t. It depends on the availability of sunshine and wind. So they need to be backed up by batteries. Once you do the backup by batteries, they’re still competitive, but the argument is not as simple as saying that they’re the cheapest.

But broadly speaking, I, in fact, entirely agree with the general thrust of your message. I’m an optimist on this question. And I think this is also the position, for instance, of the abundance agenda types, [Ezra] Klein and [Derek] Thompson and so on, which is that it’s a totally reasonable argument to make—namely that in the end, these technologies are superior. They are cheaper. People are investing in them. If you look in the pipeline, the pipeline is overwhelmingly renewables. And that’s not for reasons of wokeness. There are issues of intermittency, but the harder we push at this, the more we invest, the more the urgency will be to find solutions to them. And markets that are relatively free, like that of Texas, for instance, have turned out to be huge pioneers of rapid adoption of solar and wind. So I think, broadly speaking, that argument is well merited.

The risk is—and this is where somebody like a McKibben will come in—would be to say, well, that’s all very well, but at the margin, we can’t be certain that this game will play out necessarily in the favor of renewables. And the argument here is not coal anymore. Once upon a time, you might have said coal could come back as a source of electricity generation. I think that argument is done everywhere outside China, probably. [There are] very few places in the world where coal makes any sense. Nuclear, for those who have environmental concerns about nuclear, [is] also prohibitively expensive. A free market option without subsidy probably kills it completely stone-dead given its costs. The real issue is gas.

And the lesson here is not abstract—it’s historical. Because in the 2010s, what happened is that the entire calculus of the electricity-generating investment game in the United States was changed by the sudden availability of huge quantities of dirt-cheap shale gas, so fracked gas. And that had the effect of considerably displacing the adoption of wind and solar, which actually progressed more rapidly in Europe because gas prices—despite all of the talk of cheap gas from Russia, it was always nonsense. The really cheap gas comes from Texas, it comes from Pennsylvania, it comes from shale in the United States. And that considerably slowed down the adoption of more sustainable, more renewable energy sources. But broadly speaking, of course, it’s still a win. And folks from within the Obama administration embraced this under the heading of “all of the above” because gas replacing coal is a net win in terms of emissions. You might quibble with this if you think about the methane admitted in the production of gas, but nevertheless, it is a step in the right direction.

So the general thrust of your question, I think, should be affirmed. You know, we should not be thinking of renewables as a sort of endangered technology that needs protecting against the big beasts of fossil industry. Are there fossil risks? Yes, but they come largely from innovation in the fossil sector and not the old enemies. Not coal, not nuclear—oil has never really been in the game here anyway for electricity generation, not since the ’70s. But that, to my mind, means that this should be weighed up in terms of exactly how much build-out of which types of energy we expect. It’s not a matter of principle that renewables should favor regulation, fossils should favor deregulation. It’s not as simple as that. It’s a conjunctural specific historical gamble that, in the current moment, the benefits outweigh the downsides.

What we’re seeing, unfortunately, under Trump is that the biggest build-out and the biggest acceleration of electricity demand in American history—in recent times at least—probably ever in American history actually in terms of sheer volume, is taking place under an administration that seems determined to block renewables and under a permitting system that doesn’t permit a rapid build-out of efficient capacity. And the disaster there is it’s channeling all of that AI demand into gas-fired generation. It’s not epic by global standards; this isn’t China’s coal build-out. But it’s taking America in the wrong direction. And that’s the backdrop also against which this conversation is taking place right now. We see that there is a once-in-a-lifetime opportunity or historic opportunity to have accelerated American electrification along green lines, and it’s being obstructed.

Cameron Abadi is a deputy editor at Foreign Policy. X: @CameronAbadi

Adam Tooze is a columnist at Foreign Policy and a history professor and the director of the European Institute at Columbia University. He is the author of Chartbook, a newsletter on economics, geopolitics, and history. X: @adam_tooze

#5926. Republicans face 'brutal' 2028 Senate map after Dems' 2026 blue wave

Senate judicial confirmations accelerate, and Sen. John Thune, R-S.D., breaks down the Republican strategy before Democrats take control of the chamber.

Incoming Senate Republican campaign Chairman Bernie Moreno says the party faces a "very tough" road ahead as the GOP aims to take back its majority in 2028 after taking significant losses in the midterms earlier this month.

Democrats convincingly grabbed control of the Senate and will hold a 56-44 majority when the new Congress is sworn in at the start of the new year. They also won control of the House, and multiple governors' seats flipped from red to blue.

In the Senate, the new split means Republicans will need to flip at least six seats in the 2028 elections to win back the majority. That number rises to seven seats if they lose the White House in the next election cycle.

SENATE REPUBLICANS FEAR 'BRUTAL' 2028 MAP COULD KEEP DEMOCRATS IN POWER FOR YEARS

One senator told Fox News Digital Republicans can "forget 2028."

"It is an absolutely brutal map," Moreno recently told Fox News Digital, pointing to the states up for grabs in 2028.

Sen. Bernie Moreno, R-Ohio, will take over in January as chair of the National Republican Senatorial Committee, which is the Senate GOP's campaign arm. (Kevin Dietsch/Getty Images)

But the first-term senator from Ohio added that, in "politics, things change pretty quickly."

At the start of the 2026 cycle, the Democrats faced a steep and narrow path to winning back the majority, but, by Election Day, the party was able to ride a massive blue wave formed by a favorable political climate to flip nine Senate seats.

Senate Majority Leader John Thune, looking ahead to the next election, said in an interview on Fox News' "America Reports, "We can come back. We will."

"I've been there before, and we just have to get out and win the hearts and minds of the American people," Thune added. "And that means coming up with a compelling economic message, talking about secure streets, secure border, safe streets and more money in your pocket. I mean, I think those are the things that the American people care the most about."

Looking ahead to the next two years, when he will be leading the GOP minority in the Senate, Thune said, "We now have an opportunity in the minority to draw those bold lines, those contrasts, and make sure that we are delivering a message that connects with the middle of the electorate."

In 2028, Republicans will need to hold onto seats in battlegrounds Wisconsin and North Carolina, where GOP Sens. Ron Johnson and Ted Budd are up for re-election.

Meanwhile, Republicans will work to flip seats held by Democratic Sens. Mark Kelly of Arizona, John Fetterman of Pennsylvania, Raphael Warnock of Georgia, Catherine Cortez Masto of Nevada and Maggie Hassan of New Hampshire.

TRUMP GRIP ON GOP FACES MAJOR POST-ELECTION TEST

Sen. John Fetterman, D-Pa., makes his way to votes in the U.S. Capitol Sept. 30, 2026. (CQ-Roll Call, Inc via Getty Images)

Fetterman, who has repeatedly broken with his party and seen his poll numbers among Democrats crater, faces all-but-certain primary challenges if he seeks re-election. Kelly and Warnock are mulling White House runs in 2028.

Senate showdowns will also be held in 2028 in six states that President Donald Trump carried by double-digits two years ago that become key battlegrounds in this year's midterms.

Among them are Ohio, where former Democratic Sen. Sherrod Brown won a ticket back to the Senate with a five-point victory over appointed GOP Sen. Jon Husted. Brown will fill out the final two years of Vice President JD Vance's old Senate term, so he'll face re-election in 2028.

Republicans will be defending seats in four states where Democrats scored Senate flips in this year's midterms. The four seats are held by GOP Sens. Chuck Grassley of Iowa, Jerry Moran of Kansas, Lisa Murkowski of Alaska, and Tim Scott of South Carolina,

In Florida, appointed Republican Sen. Ashley Moody narrowly defeated progressive Democratic state Rep. Angie Nixon in a special election. Moody, who is filling out Secretary of State Marco Rubio's old Senate term, will face re-election in two years.

Republican Sen. Ashley Moody of Florida, right, who narrowly defeated far-left Democratic nominee state Rep. Angie Nixon, left, will face re-election in 2028. (Joe Raedle; Bill Clark/CQ-Roll Call, Inc)

Kyle Kondik, managing editor of Sabato’s Crystal Ball at the University of Virginia Center for Politics, which is a leading nonpartisan political handicapper, told Fox News Digital Republicans could win the Senate back by flipping six states in 2028: Arizona, Georgia, Ohio, Nevada, New Hampshire and Pennsylvania.

"Those are five states Trump won in 2024," as well as New Hampshire, which then-Vice President Kamala Harris carried by single digits.

"The Democrats just showed that they could win all these double-digit Trump states in the midterms," Kondik noted. "The Republicans don't have to flip any double-digit Harris states to win the Senate back" in 2028.

Kondik also highlighted that there will be a Senate special election next November, a year ahead of the 2028 showdowns.

That ballot box contest will be held in Minnesota, where Democratic Sen. Amy Klobuchar is about to step down after winning the state's gubernatorial election. Klobuchar will pick a replacement senator when she steps down, and the state will hold a special contest on Election Day 2027 to choose a successor to fill out the final three years of Klobuchar's current term.

#5925. The Model Middle Power

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

ANDREW YEO is a Senior Fellow and SK-Korea Foundation Chair at the Brookings Institution and Professor of Politics at the Catholic University of America. This essay is adapted from his forthcoming book South Korea’s Grand Strategy: Narratives and the Rise of a Global Nation.

What South Korea Gets Right About Geopolitics

In the ten months since Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney gave his widely discussed speech at the World Economic Forum in Davos, the question of whether, and how, the world’s middle powers can help rebuild a broken international order has become increasingly urgent. In his remarks, Carney stressed that although the old U.S.-led alliance system was collapsing, middle powers—states that do not have the ability to reshape global politics but that still possess substantial might—were well positioned to pick up the slack. But to do so, Carney argued, they would need to build up their “strength at home” and “act together” to harness their collective power, even in the absence of backing from the world’s hegemon.

Although the Canadian prime minister’s bold vision was widely applauded, some experts thought his agenda was overly ambitious. They noted, correctly, that it would be very difficult for middle powers to build a grand alliance composed of countries as diverse as Brazil, Canada, South Africa, and Turkey. “The path to unity among middle powers is fraught because this group includes rich and poor countries, as well as small and large ones,” wrote the economist Eswar Prasad in February. Carney, however, is right that middle powers have an opportunity to thrive going forward. Even when aligning with great powers, these countries can still exercise greater autonomy by developing national capacity and diversifying external partnerships.

South Korea offers a prime example of this flexible approach. For decades, Seoul has pursued a middle-power strategy centered on building domestic strength and forging partnerships with democracies everywhere while deepening its alliance with Washington. South Korea has been adaptable, recalibrating its security alignments, economic strategies, and diplomatic roles as regional and global conditions require. This has enabled a country smaller in size than most of the world’s states to punch far above its weight, exercising substantial influence in Asia and beyond.

Not every government can follow Seoul’s path, and South Korea’s strategy has its limits. As Seoul itself acknowledges, middle powers cannot escape great-power competition. But the country has begun to show what middle powers can accomplish when they leverage their partnerships and their own strength to protect their interests and deter larger threats. And by maintaining and even building its ties to the United States while asserting greater autonomy, South Korea has proven that middle powers can strengthen themselves without building whole new networks—contrary to Carney’s suggestion.

According to classic international relations theory, grand strategy is the exclusive provenance of great powers. The United States’s effort to contain the Soviet Union during the Cold War is a paradigmatic example; China’s pursuit of global economic primacy is another. Middle powers, by contrast, have little say in such strategies. Instead, these countries are seen as primarily reactive in their foreign policies, navigating around the decisions of the great powers rather than setting the agenda themselves.

But recent history suggests that the division between great and middle powers is less clear-cut than has been assumed. In practice, middle powers very much have their own affirmative visions that they pursue in the world. They have the leeway to decide which threats matter most to them, which partnerships to prioritize, and where to invest scarce resources. Japan, concerned with the threat posed by China, has been quietly exercising leadership in the Indo-Pacific by improving its ties with neighbors, increasing investment and aid to the region, and strengthening its own defense capabilities—all while remaining closely allied with the United States. India, worried about China, Pakistan, and growing economic headwinds, has successfully bolstered partnerships with the United States and other democracies but maintained its relationship with Russia.

Much like Japan and India, South Korea is worried about its neighbors. But for Seoul, this is nothing new. South Korean leaders have been concerned with the very thing Carney fretted about in his Davos speech—becoming collateral damage in conflicts between great powers— since at least the late nineteenth century, when it was caught between China, Japan, and Russia. The country has a well-known proverb, “When whales fight, the shrimp's back is broken,” that captures the sentiment that great-power feuds can harm countries caught in the middle.

To safeguard against this threat, Seoul has long sought to increase its self-reliance through industrial policy. The country transited from light to heavy export industries in the 1970s, turning it into a major player in the chemical, steel, and shipbuilding sectors. State investments helped it become a major global producer of batteries and semiconductors in the present era. South Korea takes great pride in what historians call “the miracle on the Han”—or the country's rapid rise from a war-torn and impoverished state to a rich one during the late twentieth century. They now use the state’s wealth to promote public goods globally, contributing to international development, global health, climate policy, and multilateral bodies.

Seoul has also built up a formidable military, one that experts consistently rank as among the strongest in the world. Today, many South Korean analysts argue that Seoul needs a nuclear arsenal for self-defense, even though the country officially remains under Washington’s nuclear umbrella. The state has simultaneously used its armed prowess to deepen relations with U.S. allies. Seoul, for example, has inked multibillion-dollar arms deals with European buyers, making it a key partner in rebuilding NATO’s industrial capacity. In 2025, it was the second largest supplier of weapons to European NATO members (after the United States). South Korea has also forged defense partnerships in the Middle East and Southeast Asia, widening Seoul's options and reducing its dependence on any single market. South Korea, in other words, has been able to convert its economic resources and bureaucratic capabilities into greater influence by expanding its partnerships, strengthening its voice in international bodies, and helping shape global rules and norms.

But even as it charts its own path, Seoul has no plans to abandon the United States, at least in the near future. In fact, the sanctity of the U.S.-South Korean relationship is a significant historical Korean narrative. Many South Koreans see the alliance as a bond inked in blood, thanks to the sacrifice both countries made during the Korean War, when they worked to stop North Korea, backed by China and the Soviet Union, from overrunning the south. Most South Koreans correctly believe that the continued U.S. military presence in the country—currently, 28,500 American troops are stationed on the peninsula—has played a major part in deterring North Korean aggression over the past seven decades.

At first glance, South Korea’s strategy may seem contradictory. On the one hand, the country wants to deepen its cooperation with the United States, on which it still depends for security. But on the other, it seeks greater autonomy.

Yet these twin goals are not actually at odds. By cultivating ties with Washington, Seoul has gained access to advanced military capabilities and technologies as well as a larger diplomatic platform to expand its regional and global interests. It is in part because of this relationship, for example, that South Korea has had such success at selling weapons to multiple NATO members, including Croatia, Estonia, Finland, Norway, Poland, and Romania. (Seoul has also built up a formidable diplomatic apparatus in its own right.) At the same time, South Korea helps make this compact worthwhile for the United States by providing it with valuable technology, including semiconductor chips. Seoul has also partnered with the Washington to help revive the American shipbuilding industry, agreeing to invest $150 billion in the U.S. sector.

Still, South Korea’s growing push for more independence in its foreign policy does result in some tensions with the United States that cannot easily be resolved. For instance, the current government’s push to give Seoul, not Washington, operational control over both states’ forces on the peninsula in the event of war has been rebuffed by the U.S. military. American commanders instead favor a conditions-based transfer after South Korea has proven it has the capabilities to lead a joint defense, rather than setting a fixed date. The two sides also have not reached a consensus on what role South Korea would play if Taiwan is attacked or blockaded by China.

Yet so far, Seoul has done an effective job at managing these differences. For example, in his second term, U.S. President Donald Trump has repeatedly accused Washington’s allies, including South Korea, of leeching off the United States and not paying their fair share. To extract more from Washington’s partners, Trump has implemented punishing tariffs. In response, South Korea could have been defiant and slapped on retaliatory restrictions, as other U.S. allies did. Instead, South Korean President Lee Jae-myung avoided rebuking the president and worked to meet American demands in ways that heightened the two states’ mutual dependence. For instance, Lee pledged to invest $350 billion in the United States to advance American economic and national security interests, including by purchasing more U.S. military equipment, and to increase South Korean economic support for locally stationed American forces.

Seoul’s strategy has not been a uniform success. The South Korean economy remains exposed to American and Chinese pressure despite its growth and diversification. North Korea is expanding its nuclear and missile forces and deepening military ties with Russia, creating new security headaches for which Seoul still needs U.S. assistance. The country is politically polarized, resulting in sharp policy shifts that can undermine long-term strategic planning. It also suffers from the lowest birthrate of any country in the world, threatening its tax base and the size of its armed forces. Beyond that, South Korea may not provide the ideal template for every middle power. Not every country in this category has similar economic resources, networks, or ties to the United States.

But on the whole, South Korea’s grand strategy has been effective, and it thus carries broader lessons. For one, it shows how adaptive middle powers can draw on their respective national narratives to find their strengths and to chart a path forward. Perhaps more important, it is proof positive that middle powers can exercise influence and achieve a measure of autonomy even without working together to build a neutral third pole, as Carney urged. It also illustrates that middle powers can remain firmly allied with the United States while developing their own independent capabilities. South Korea, then, demonstrates that states can reduce their vulnerability and widen their political options.

An earlier version of this article incorrectly stated that the South Korean government was known to be considering developing nuclear weapons.

With Its Allies, Seoul Has Enough Leverage to Push Back

Stephen E. Biegun and Emma Chanlett-Avery

#5923. After ‘Wild Horse Nine,’ 5 of the Best Movies About the CIA

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Review: After ‘Wild Horse Nine,’ 5 of the Best Movies About the CIA

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

In Martin McDonagh’s new film, Wild Horse Nine, there’s a moment where a younger CIA operative is trying to make his older comrade, who is dying of cancer and worried about his soul, feel better.

“You’re going to get to heaven, Chris,” Lee (Sam Rockwell) assures John Malkovich’s character. “There’s whole countries that wouldn’t be free if not for you. Guatemala. Indonesia. That place in Africa I can never remember the name of. Sure you killed some people. So fucking what?” Do people who work at the CIA really talk this way? God, I hope not.

In Martin McDonagh’s new film, Wild Horse Nine, there’s a moment where a younger CIA operative is trying to make his older comrade, who is dying of cancer and worried about his soul, feel better.

“You’re going to get to heaven, Chris,” Lee (Sam Rockwell) assures John Malkovich’s character. “There’s whole countries that wouldn’t be free if not for you. Guatemala. Indonesia. That place in Africa I can never remember the name of. Sure you killed some people. So fucking what?” Do people who work at the CIA really talk this way? God, I hope not.

But then again, Wild Horse Nine, set during the 1973 Chilean coup, isn’t really about the CIA. The dark comedy—a good bet for landing several Academy Award nominations—is about mortality and remorse and how even the most brazen people can have moments of clarity and reevaluate their past actions. It uses the CIA as a skin, the way McDonagh’s previous (and very similar) fatalistic comedy In Bruges did with contract killers.

Although its average employee likely has a job as mundane as any other government worker, an organization as key to world affairs and as necessarily shrouded in mystery as the CIA is understandably a great font for storytellers. But I can’t say there have been too many classic films that treat the CIA seriously.

There have been some marvelous paranoid thrillers, specifically Watergate-era classics like Three Days of the Condor, which tend to get a little far-fetched by the third act. There are some exceptions, like 2005’s Syriana and 2014’s A Most Wanted Man, that show the minutiae of spy craft in a compelling way that doesn’t devolve into silliness. These are rarities. I also don’t really consider Sicario or Zero Dark Thirty CIA films, because their sweep covers multiple government agencies.

The lengthy 2006 film The Good Shepherd, directed by Robert De Niro and starring Matt Damon, was an attempt to show how the agency grew out of Yale secret societies and reached a crisis point during the Bay of Pigs. Despite some good intentions, though, the movie is a significant dud and has few champions. (I recently revisited the film, but found myself needing several attempts, after I fell asleep on the couch on more than one occasion.)

Even for pure action-adventure absurdity, Britain has James Bond, but the United States has … James Bond’s friend Felix Leiter? Nice guy and all, but not 007. Mission: Impossible’s Ethan Hunt works for the Impossible Missions Force, remember, not the CIA. In the 1960s, Derek Flint, a Bond knockoff, worked for Z.O.W.I.E. (Zonal Organization World Intelligence Espionage), and Matt Helm worked for ICE (Intelligence and Counter-Espionage). Other than Jack Ryan, whose recent Amazon series and films have been absolutely horrible, Hollywood hasn’t given us a CIA man who’s become a household name.

There have, of course, been great movies about the CIA, but all of them are laced with comedy. Maybe this is some kind of national defense mechanism? Hey, don’t mind us, we may be tinkering in the affairs of other sovereign nations, but we know it’s a little naughty.

If the dark comedy of Wild Horse Nine has you itching for more examples of this curious phenomenon, here are five titles you can stream—all of which are intellectually zesty and a great deal of fun. (The Good Shepherd has value, too, should you find your stash of zolpidem depleted.) I’ve listed them from least-to-most zany.

Bryan Cranston and Ben Affleck in Argo.IMDB

This extremely watchable spy movie—with a healthy dose of comedy—was a major financial success and also won the Academy Award for best picture. (It beat both Steven Spielberg’s Lincoln and the aforementioned Zero Dark Thirty, both of which I’d place higher, but much like an operation to destabilize a country’s democracy, Oscar success isn’t always predictable.)

The story is rooted in a true event: During the Iran hostage crisis, a CIA agent (Affleck) created an elaborate cover story to smuggle home six Americans who were hiding with the Canadian ambassador. His cockamamie plan was to fake being a Canadian film production that was scouting for locations. To make it work, there had to be a real script (a Star Wars knockoff) plus actual Hollywood producers and craftspeople—so Alan Arkin and John Goodman (playing John Chambers, the real-life guy who did the prosthetics for Planet of the Apes) join in.

Naturally, there are setbacks and raised stakes as the baddies piece together which Americans are missing, then there’s a race against the clock for the big finish. Sadly (well, not for the real people involved), the action-adventure elements of the last act were made up for the movie. But Argo is still an engrossing heist-style picture. It has respect for the CIA, but also presents an agency with a prankster’s edge.

Funnily enough, a 1966 crime-comedy from Italian director Vittorio De Sica and New York playwright Neil Simon called After the Fox used the idea of a fake movie production as the cover for a grand scheme—but in their case, it was smuggling gold.

More recently, a group of Hollywood producers used the Argo technique as cover for an actual scheme in Poland, trying to recover a cache of stamps believed stolen from concentration camp prisoners. They even made a documentary film, The Stamp Thief, about the charade.

Julia Roberts and Tom Hanks in Charlie Wilson’s War. IMDB

In the months after 9/11, like many freaked-out Americans, I would stay up until dawn watching cable news stations, which is where I first learned the term “blowback,” and about how some felt that the CIA’s backing of the Mujahideen against the Soviets in Afghanistan caused a domino effect that eventually knocked down the Twin Towers. If I tried to explain what I’d seen the night before on C-SPAN 2, I sounded a bit like a paranoid lunatic, so I was very happy when screenwriter Aaron Sorkin and director Mike Nichols collaborated on Charlie Wilson’s War, a scathingly dark CIA satire that summed a lot of this up.

The always affable Tom Hanks stars as the sleazy titular congressman who, from a Vegas hot tub filled with Playboy models, sees a 60 Minutes report about Afghan refugees. It’s 1980, and he’s worried about Soviet encroachment, but also finds himself (somewhat to his surprise) genuinely caring about civilians caught up in large geopolitical events.

He finds likeminded spirits in Joanne Herring (Julia Roberts), a wealthy Houston socialite and political activist, and Gust Avrakotos (Philip Seymour Hoffman), a working-class CIA operative with a brilliant mind, well-honed bullshit detector, and extremely short temper who was just passed over for promotion. Like Argo, Charlie Wilson’s War is practically a heist picture, but about pushing levers in Washington to quietly appropriate funds and bring matériel to the Afghan fighters.

The dark punchline, of course, is how our heroes are powerless when it comes time to “win the peace,” as it were, leaving a power vacuum that, it can easily be argued, was filled by al Qaeda. The film’s release at the end of George W. Bush’s presidency functioned as a needed release valve.

Quite the opposite of seductive Sean-Connery-as-007 is hangdog Walter Matthau, but the dark comedy Hopscotch suggests they have some traits in common. Matthau plays Miles Kendig, a veteran CIA agent whose boss (Ned Beatty) takes him out of the field and sticks him with a desk job. As revenge, Kendig decides he’ll write his memoirs (much like Malkovich in Wild Horse Nine), and his spilled secrets make security agencies across the globe very nervous. As he sends salacious excerpts to international higher-ups from various international hiding spots, one of the few things the CIA and KGB can agree upon is: Get Matthau away from his typewriter!

Aiding him is an old flame (Glenda Jackson, who also starred with Matthau’s in the popular comedy House Calls) who lives in Austria, which is a nice touch because our lead character is an opera nut who likes to sing along (poorly) to Mozart in the car.

What Hopscotch has in common with Charlie Wilson’s War is presenting CIA bosses as absolute buffoons. The best part is when Matthau uses Beatty’s own vacation home as a hideout—which America’s intelligence forces then shoot to pieces. While Walter Matthau circa 1980 isn’t the most athletic secret agent, he’s quick with disguises, voices, thinking on his feet, and laying out elaborate traps. This is a great old school comedy with just enough post-Watergate negativity to keep it sharp.

Alan Arkin and Peter Falk in The In-Laws.IMDB

Hopscotch is a little wacky. The In-Laws is full-on madcap. It’s also one of the funniest movies ever made, though its serpentine plot is hard to explain in just a few sentences. But I’ll give it a go…

Alan Arkin plays a nervous dentist whose daughter is getting married to a nice kid, but Arkin hasn’t yet met the boy’s family. Turns out the father, played by Peter Falk, is a noisy madman who is either bank robber or a rogue CIA agent with blockhead bosses in the middle of an elaborate scheme to overthrow a Latin American dictator. Things soon spiral out of control, and this odd couple find themselves in several high-speed chases, dodging bullets while still hoping to make it to the wedding in time. The two great actors zing each other with quality barbs, and when that’s been exhausted there’s some physical comedy, too.

The screenplay, written by Andrew Bergman, who also worked on Blazing Saddles, Fletch, and Soapdish, offers a much milder version of the impudence toward the CIA that you see in Wild Horse Nine. “You win some, you lose some,” Peter Falk shrugs when asked about the Bay of Pigs. Though this movie is mostly a comedic joy, the notion that U.S. intelligence is in the hands of nutcases at best—or sheer incompetents at worst—finds quick purchase here and seems to have reverberated in the decades since.

Brad Pitt and Frances McDormand in Burn After Reading. IMDB

There’s a lot about John Malkovich’s Wild Horse Nine character, Chris Carlisle, that may feel familiar. In Burn After Reading, the Coen brothers’ shaggiest movie (besides The Big Lebowski), Malkovich plays Osborne Cox, a CIA agent who is demoted and decides to write a vicious memoir. But unlike the overlooked and pen-poised agents mentioned on this list already, Cox is a complete klutz.

But so is everyone in this tale, which includes Brad Pitt as dunderhead gym trainer Chad Feldheimer who, after a wild string of goof-ups and misunderstandings, ends up with a computer disc holding the first draft of Cox’s book. Feldheimer thinks he’s stumbled upon some serious material (it’s really just drunken grievances) and decides to blackmail Cox. This does not go well, and a ripple effect includes a murderer’s row of daffy performances from George Clooney, Frances McDormand, Richard Jenkins (more dumb than daffy), J.K. Simmons (exasperated by the stupidity around him, but powerless), and others.

This was the Coen brothers’ follow-up to No Country For Old Men, their Oscar-winning crime drama based on Cormac McCarthy’s dark tale of greed and fate, and it’s probably the most cartoonish thing they’ve ever done. The CIA is nothing but a punching bag for 96 minutes, but the remaining undercurrent of violence erupts from time to time in some shocking (but hilarious) moments. Burn After Reading pushes the silliness to the absolute edge, but still somehow feels like parts of it could be true.

This post appeared in the FP Weekend newsletter, a weekly showcase of book reviews, deep dives, and features. Sign up here.

#5922. Trump orders federal contractors to alert White House on congressional record requests

Fox News contributor Mollie Hemingway joins 'America Reports' to discuss the Democrats preparing for aggressive investigations targeting President Trump's associates with document preservation letters. Hemingway also discusses how court packing campaigns threaten to damage the Supreme Court.

President Donald Trump signed an executive order late Friday instructing federal contractors to notify the White House of congressional information requests and to withhold material the administration deems privileged, a move that comes as Democrats prepare to launch oversight investigations.

The order applies to all federal contractors and subcontractors. Companies that fail to comply risk losing their existing contracts or being suspended from future work, according to the order.

Trump framed the directive as pushback against lawmakers whom the order accuses of "harassing private companies" to get confidential executive branch information.

President Donald Trump listens during a news briefing in the James S. Brady Press Briefing Room of the White House Nov. 20, 2026, in Washington, D.C. (Kevin Dietsch/Getty Images)

SUPREME COURT URGED TO DEFANG ‘CRIMINAL TRAP’ THAT HAS SENT TRUMP OFFICIALS TO PRISON

The order could invite legal challenges, particularly if contractors are told to withhold records sought by subpoena. Although executive privilege lets presidents protect certain internal communications, courts can intervene in clashes over congressional oversight powers.

The order builds on a directive issued earlier this week by White House Counsel Will Scharf, who instructed federal agencies to clear employee congressional testimony with the administration first. Scharf added that some Democratic investigations could cross into criminal conduct, drawing pushback over what critics call a broader effort to shield the executive branch from oversight.

Democrats won control of both chambers of Congress in the midterm elections and plan to investigate the administration as well as the Trump family's business dealings and ties to private firms.

The U.S. Capitol in Washington, D.C., Nov. 17, 2026 (Al Drago/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

TRUMP ORDERS DOJ PROBE INTO DEMOCRATS OVER ALLEGED THREATS AGAINST ADMINISTRATION OFFICIALS

Other investigative priorities include how the administration conducted immigration enforcement and its handling of the Jeffrey Epstein probe after the release of the government's case files.

Rep. Robert Garcia, D-Calif., who will likely lead the House Oversight Committee next year, said the probes "will lay the groundwork for legislation which helps all Americans."

This week, Garcia sent document preservation letters to 12 current and former administration officials, including to Vice President JD Vance, Donald Trump Jr., Eric Trump, Jared Kushner, former U.S. Ambassador to Greece Kimberly Guilfoyle and White House chief of staff Stephen Miller.

Rep. Robert Garcia, D-Calif., speaks to reporters after the House Democratic Leadership Elections on Capitol Hill Nov. 18, 2026 in Washington, D.C. (Anna Moneymaker/Getty Images)

"Oversight Democrats have been investigating the potential corruption, self-dealing, and abuses of power that have funneled billions of dollars to the Trump family as Americans struggle with rising costs," letters to the president's two oldest sons read.

"While the Trump Administration’s failed policies have made life increasingly unaffordable for the public, you have engaged in business pursuits that appear to have allowed you to personally profit from your father’s presidency on a massive scale and at the expense of the American people."

The letter to Miller read in part, "Oversight Democrats have heard directly from the victims of the lawless conduct of President Trump’s mass deportation campaign and from whistleblowers appalled by agency behavior. There must be accountability for those responsible for orchestrating, carrying out, and covering up these abuses, at all levels of the agency."

#5920. America’s Hidden AI Advantage

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

JEFFREY DING is Assistant Professor of Political Science at George Washington University and the author of Technology and the Rise of Great Powers: How Diffusion Shapes Economic Competition.

The United States—Not China—Dominates Diffusion

In the view of most observers, China is winning the race to diffuse artificial intelligence across the economy. Even if the United States has a small lead in frontier AI models, the consensus is that China is better at applying the technology. As Kevin Rudd, former prime minister of Australia and a prominent observer of Chinese politics, declared at a conference in October, China is “miles ahead” in spreading AI “into the real economy.” This fits a familiar narrative about the technological rivalry between the two great powers: the United States innovates while China turns those innovations into practical applications at scale.

This narrative has become central to discussions of U.S.-Chinese competition in AI. But it doesn’t match reality. After the Chinese startup DeepSeek shocked the world in early 2025 with the announcement of an open-source large language model that rivaled the capabilities of frontier models made by American companies, the international media published breathless reports on how DeepSeek’s model had rapidly spread across state-owned enterprises, hospitals, and provincial and local governments. Months later, however, analysts determined that this adoption was shallow, narrow, and slow; the technology was being used on clunky “all-in-one machines” embedded on isolated hardware units within these institutions, rather than integrated more broadly into the software services they were using. Then, in January 2026, outside observers marveled at OpenClaw, a new open-source AI agent, as a sign of China’s diffusion advantage. Now, the excitement about these open-source agents has dissipated as security vulnerabilities and inefficiency have limited their uptake.

It is the United States, not China, that has a decisive lead in AI diffusion. This is because the United States has comparative advantages in both its overall level of economic development and the nature of its growth model. China’s underdeveloped software market, weak technology transfer mechanisms, and centralized economic system make AI diffusion much more challenging. All told, the United States’ advantage in diffusing the technology through the economy is likely to persist for the foreseeable future.

Despite its reputation as a pioneer of digital technology such as cashless payments and messaging apps, China’s overall digitization levels—the extent to which software and other digital tools have penetrated various sectors of the economy—are far lower than those in the United States. American firms are on average more than twice as likely as their Chinese competitors to adopt cloud computing services. And the U.S. enterprise software market, which includes services to manage customer relationships and provide business intelligence, is approximately ten times larger than China’s. According to the 2022 Global Cloud Ecosystem Index, which ranked 76 countries on their access to and usage of cloud services, China sat in the bottom half; the United States, by contrast, ranked 17th, not far behind the top performers, in part because of its dominance of “software as a service” products in which businesses can gain access to enterprise software without needing to install and maintain the programs themselves.

China’s relatively low penetration of digital services is a barrier to diffusing AI. When small Chinese hospitals tried to adopt DeepSeek, for instance, they lacked information systems that could collect and manage the data necessary to take full advantage of AI models. In these hospitals, which include many small health facilities in rural areas, patient data is still recorded on paper; where digital records do exist, they are often incomplete or vary from hospital to hospital. By contrast, 95 percent of U.S. doctors maintain electronic health record systems in their offices. As a result, U.S. hospitals have more smoothly integrated AI into electronic health records to recommend treatments, predict health risks, and simplify billing.

China’s underdeveloped cloud and enterprise software ecosystem also explains why the OpenClaw hype did not translate to sustainable diffusion. Open-source tools like OpenClaw appeal to hobbyists and individual developers who are keen to experiment and are less sensitive to security issues. But small businesses have been slow to adopt such tools because they prefer reliable, standardized solutions that are easy to implement and update. Small businesses are more likely to rely on cloud-based tools and enterprise software to implement AI upgrades than to try to experiment on their own—two channels that are less robust in China than in the United States.

The United States also has an advantage over China because of linkages within the science and technology ecosystem that help disseminate advances. Collaboration between universities and businesses is critical to diffusing AI breakthroughs across the entire economy because these connections bring together innovators who can develop new AI products and implementers who can effectively commercialize them.

In this dimension, the United States is far ahead of China. Americans have long led the world in the publication of articles about AI co-authored by at least one researcher from academia and one researcher from industry. Between 2015 and 2019, U.S. researchers published more than twice as many such articles as their Chinese counterparts. The gap extends beyond AI: across the life sciences and natural sciences, the share of publications in the United States that involved both university and industry researchers was also more than double the comparable rate in China. Beijing recognizes this gap: China’s official state news agency ranked weak technical exchanges between industry and academia as one of the five crucial shortcomings of the country’s AI ecosystem.

Although Chinese policymakers are aware of this challenge and have promoted policies to try to spur collaboration between universities and businesses, especially in AI, it will be difficult to cultivate the requisite trust needed for these two sectors to work together. China’s weak intellectual property protections, for example, discourage universities from pursuing partnerships with industry actors because they fear losing ownership of their inventions. The United States, by contrast, has a tradition of university-industry collaboration. The 1980 Bayh-Dole Act, which provides incentives and patent protections that encourage universities to commercialize inventions and license them to the private sector, kick-started decades of collaboration. Without a similar connective tissue to link academic institutions and businesses, Chinese universities are not as equipped to form profitable partnerships with companies. Chinese technical and engineering education is also disconnected from commercial enterprises, meaning that schools do not teach students the specific skills that companies need in their workforces.

Weak technology transfer mechanisms also undermine one of China’s main strengths in AI diffusion: its massive talent base. Analysis by the Information Technology and Innovation Foundation showed that in 2022, China awarded around 50,000 doctoral degrees in science, technology, engineering, and math, compared with only about 34,000 in the United States. Although the sheer quantity of Chinese computer science graduates may eventually help the country adopt AI advances at scale, the quality of that education determines how quickly or effectively education can encourage AI diffusion. If training is divorced from practical applications, graduates will struggle to adapt cutting-edge advances from industry labs into the sectors in which they work. Consider one commonly used standard to measure the baseline quality of AI education: whether a university employs at least one researcher who has published at least one paper at a leading AI conference. According to data from 2023–24, only 38 universities in China met this standard; the United States accounted for 150.

China’s centrally planned approach to AI development is also counterproductive when it comes to diffusion. In 2025, China developed an “AI Plus” action plan that outlines targets for AI adoption over the next decade and released guidelines for government agencies and local officials on how to put the plan into effect across the country. For many observers, the mere existence of a plan showed that the highest levels of Chinese leadership were committed to spreading AI widely and that China was better positioned to win the AI race than the United States, which lacks any kind of similar diffusion blueprint. Rudd’s claims about China’s edge in AI adoption, for instance, cite Beijing’s ambitious policies and targets for AI diffusion as supporting evidence.

But centralized approaches such as China’s often impede technology diffusion, especially when the state tries to intervene in domains in which the development trajectory is uncertain. One of the planks of China’s AI Plus plan identifies “global cooperation” as a priority sector for AI adoption. If local governments were to implement the plan as outlined, it would shift resources from areas in which diffusion has been proven to be effective, such as health care, to the undefined space of “global cooperation.” More broadly, Beijing’s favoritism toward state-owned enterprises and its preference for picking national champions often prop up inefficient technologies that would have been weeded out by market-based competition. Other businesses are unlikely to adopt inefficient technologies, making nonmarket forms of AI development less likely to diffuse widely.

Counterintuitively, the lack of a clear U.S. policy on AI diffusion is a boon, not a hindrance, to the United States’ attempt to diffuse the technology across the economy. At the height of U.S.-Japanese competition in the 1980s, for instance, when the two countries were racing to train software engineers who could spread computer technology, Japan’s Ministry of Education, Science, and Culture, as it was then known, exercised centralized control over universities. But emerging trends in computer science developed faster than the government could adapt. By concentrating state funds on centers of excellence and imposing one-size-fits-all rules on the computing curriculum, Japan could not match the adaptability of American universities, and the United States surged ahead. Cross-national studies have shown that decentralized science and technology systems are correlated with faster diffusion of new innovations.

Another historical case with parallels to China, the Soviet Union, further suggests how centralization is antithetical to diffusion. In the twentieth century, the Soviet Union boasted many plans for technology development. Yet its top-down approach, which focused on large-scale engineering projects, undercut market-based instruments that encourage technology diffusion through competition. As a result, although the Soviet Union pioneered new technologies in many areas, including the casting of steel, it fell behind the United States in adopting general-purpose technologies such as computers. A prescient U.S. intelligence report published in 1969 deemed the Soviet Union’s mechanisms for technology diffusion as “much more balky” than those of the United States because of the absence of competitive pressures as well as limited linkages between the R & D system and the broader economy.

The myth of China’s lead in AI diffusion persists despite clear data showing U.S. advantages. In part, this may be because China’s innovation system has facilitated the widespread adoption of certain visible technologies. In less than 20 years, China has built more than 30,000 miles of high-speed rail, for example, blanketing the country with ultra-fast trains that now reach even the most remote provinces.

But infrastructure presents a very different challenge than AI does. The centralized pressure that officials can use to effectively develop large-scale infrastructure projects is much less effective for general-purpose technologies like AI because it is infeasible to mandate that all small- and medium-size firms, spread across every sector of the economy, adopt artificial intelligence. Those companies would do so only if it made sense from a business perspective, and few have the digital foundations or savvy employees able to harness AI in a way that would help them grow. Moreover, the United States has outbuilt China in the infrastructure most essential to AI development, data centers, in which it retains a ten-to-one edge. In fact, in some cases, the data centers that China has built remain idle because of weak AI demand.

The misperception that China leads AI diffusion may continue unabated because analysis of the country’s technology ecosystem is overly focused on frontier firms such as ByteDance and DeepSeek and first-tier cities, such as Beijing and Shanghai. Yet AI diffusion ultimately rests on whether adoption occurs in medium size companies, small towns, and inland provinces, where it has been extremely limited. Because these settings rarely feature in outside media coverage, analysis of China’s AI diffusion landscape underestimates the difficulty of translating cutting-edge advances from frontier labs and universities in Hangzhou or Shenzhen to, say, a small firm in a county seat in northwestern Qinghai Province.

Perhaps the simplest explanation is that such a myth is useful, even if ultimately untrue. In some ways, the United States is trumpeting Beijing’s AI diffusion in the way that Chinese “tiger parents” try to motivate their children to work harder by raising the success of “that other family’s kid.” “Look at how well that other family’s kid performed at the piano recital,” they might say, without listening closely to ensure that the notes were exactly right. In great-power rivalry, too, there is a natural tendency to overhype the other side’s capabilities, in part to spur more effort at home.

But such a “useful” myth can backfire if it pushes the United States to overlook its own advantages or even to mimic China’s more top-down approach to AI development. By any measure, the United States maintains a comfortable lead in AI diffusion, and China, despite its impressive successes, has serious challenges. Although the narrative of Chinese dominance endures, for now all signs point to a U.S. lead that will remain strong.

In the U.S.-Chinese Tech Race, Diffusion Matters More Than Invention

Regulating AI Will Not Set America Back in the Technology Race

Helen Toner, Jenny Xiao, and Jeffrey Ding

To Stay Ahead of China, Trump Must Build on Biden’s Work

How to Defuse Looming Threats to U.S. National Security

Beijing’s Overreach Threatens the Détente With America

How AI Is Reshaping Influence Operations in America

Beijing’s Overreach Threatens the Détente With America

U.S. Dealmaking Is Strengthening the Chavista Regime

Only a Radical Course Change Can Win Back Its Most Important Ally

Arab Citizens of Israel Can Bring Change, but They Can’t Do It Alone

#5919. What in the World?

1. Which group kicked off its leaders’ summit in Manila, Philippines, on Sunday?

The Association of Southeast Asian Nations

U.S. President Donald Trump attended the summit for the second year in a row, but not much came of it—other than an introduction to Filipino fast-food chain Jollibee, Foreign Policy’s Joseph Rachman writes in Southeast Asia Brief.

2. Which object struck Pope Leo XIV in the face on Monday before he celebrated the final Mass of his trip to Peru?

The pope later forgave the worshipper who threw the rosary—continuing the Vatican’s strong postwar tradition of peace promotion, which Vanessa R. Corcoran wrote about in April.

3. Which landmark partly collapsed on Tuesday after Ukrainian drones and missiles struck it two days in a row?

The bridge is both a symbol of the Russian occupation of Crimea and a major chokepoint for the transport of military and civilian supplies, FP’s Alexandra Sharp reports in World Brief.

4. On Tuesday, the Pan American Health Organization announced that Bolivia, Mexico, and the United States had lost their designation of having eliminated which disease?

A measles outbreak has also been worsening in recent months in Bangladesh, which recorded around 200,000 suspected cases between mid-March and early October, FP’s Michael Kugelman wrote in South Asia Brief in October.

5. On Wednesday, Iranian forces targeted a U.S. air base located in which country?

The tit-for-tat strikes between Iran and the United States this week marked an end to a roughly two-month lull in such attacks, FP’s John Haltiwanger and Rishi Iyengar write in Situation Report.

6. The United States signed a military partnership agreement with which Latin American country on Wednesday?

U.S. officials said the deal was intended to help the two governments battle drug traffickers, but analysts believe that it could also be an opportunity for Washington to rein in the power of the Venezuelan military, FP’s Catherine Osborn reports in Latin America Brief.

7. On Thursday, the International Court of Justice ruled that Myanmar had committed genocide against the Rohingya people. Which country brought the case before the court?

This is the first time the court has found a state directly responsible for genocide, and it’s likely to have major implications for future cases, Andrew Nachemson writes.

8. U.S. President Donald Trump, Chinese President Xi Jinping, and Russian President Vladimir Putin held their first-ever trilateral meeting on Thursday. In which city did it take place?

The meeting was no Yalta Conference, but it did provide the leaders with plenty of attention and a few photo ops, FP’s James Palmer writes in China Brief.

9. Also on Thursday, Somalia expelled the ambassador of which country?

The decision was partly prompted by Abu Dhabi’s relationship with several Somalian regions, including the breakaway province of Somaliland—whose quest for international recognition has been picking up steam, Joshua Keating wrote in September.

10. On Wednesday, which spacecraft became the first human-made object to reach a distance of one light-day away from Earth?

Voyager 1 has now traveled more than four times the distance from the sun to Pluto—and as of next September, it will have been in space for 50 years, Smithsonian magazine reports.
