#5910. The Only Way to End the Netanyahu Era

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

AYMAN ODEH served in the Knesset from 2015 to 2026. He is the former leader of the Joint List and the Hadash party.

Arab Citizens of Israel Can Bring Change, but They Can’t Do It Alone

As the Israeli opposition leader Gadi Eisenkot struggles to assemble a governing coalition following last month’s elections, Israel finds itself in a familiar political predicament. The parties opposed to Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu and his far-right coalition command a clear majority in the Knesset, with 67 of its 120 seats. Yet the four Jewish-led parties in Eisenkot’s camp hold just 48 seats, leaving them unable to form a government on their own. The impasse echoes the prolonged political deadlock between 2019 and 2021, when Netanyahu’s supporters and his opponents remained locked in competing parliamentary blocs, and four consecutive elections failed to produce a stable governing majority.

But one thing is different this time. Israel’s Arab-led parties, the Joint List and Raam, are stronger than ever. Powered by extraordinary turnout among Arab citizens of Israel, they won a record 19 seats in the Knesset: 11 for the Joint List and eight for Raam. This political mobilization is important in itself, demonstrating Arab voters’ overwhelming desire for change and their recognition of the importance of participating in elections. But its significance goes far beyond the number of seats they have won. Together, the Arab parties now have the potential to be a decisive force in an anti-Netanyahu coalition, providing the parliamentary backing needed to form a government. The proof of this power has already been shown: on November 17, Arab support was crucial in the vote to replace the Likud speaker of the Knesset with a speaker from Eisenkot’s party. Now they have a historic opportunity to put the country on a different path.

As the first and former leader of the Joint List, I know how much is at stake. In 2019, I helped lead a groundbreaking effort to give Arab-led parties a direct voice in the Israeli political system. We took an enormous political risk by endorsing Benny Gantz, then a former chief of staff of the Israeli military and Netanyahu’s principal rival, as a candidate to form a government. We did so despite our profound political and ideological differences with him. Although that effort ultimately fell short, with Gantz choosing to pursue a “unity government” with Netanyahu instead, it established an important precedent: by showing a willingness to work with Jewish political forces with whom they had deep disagreements, parties representing the Arab public had a real chance to prevent a Netanyahu-led far-right government from holding power.

Today, after nearly four years of disastrous leadership by Netanyahu and his far-right allies, that possibility has become far more urgent. It will take political courage on both sides. Eisenkot must be able to offer something to the Arab-led parties, and the Arab-led parties will need to be comfortable offering support to a government that they may not agree with on many issues. But the remarkable Arab turnout and the sizable Israeli majority that is now against Netanyahu have pointed to a way forward. Not only Israel’s future but also its place in the region and the world may depend on the willingness of its leaders to engage with the country’s Palestinian minority. Ultimately, such a partnership will be essential to building the trust between Jews and Arabs that is a prerequisite to achieving any real progress toward a viable Israeli-Palestinian peace. This is the real choice now facing Israel: whether Israelis are prepared to replace the politics of supremacy and exclusion with a politics of equality.

The factors that drove record numbers of Palestinian citizens of Israel to the polls in October have been years in the making. Although the country’s Arab citizens make up more than 20 percent of the Israeli electorate and have long made vital contributions to Israeli society as doctors, lawyers, engineers, high-tech professionals, and in many other fields, the Israeli right has for decades demonized them as enemies: a fifth column and a threat to the state. In recent years, this hostility has increasingly manifested itself in harassment and open racism at universities and workplaces, in the media, in the parliament, and in everyday life.

Yet it would be a mistake to assume that Arab exclusion has been driven only by Netanyahu and the radical right. Zionist parties of the center and the left have also contributed, by keeping a distance from Arab voters or simply ignoring them. The belief that Jewish citizens should ultimately determine the country’s political direction while Arab citizens must remain within whatever limits have been set for them runs deep in Israeli politics. Netanyahu understood early on how to turn this contradiction into a political strategy. Since the 1990s, he has repeatedly used the same formula: inciting his supporters against Arab citizens, delegitimizing Arab representatives in parliament, and convincing Jewish Israelis that a government that relies on Arab political support would somehow be illegitimate.

These tactics proved to be among Netanyahu’s most effective political tools, especially given that Arab-led parties have long been some of his biggest foes. He didn’t need to convince everyone to support his Likud party or its various coalition partners. He only needed to convince enough people, including Jewish Israelis who opposed him, that working with Arab-led parties was dangerous and unacceptable. Netanyahu’s strategy also helped suppress Arab turnout. Since the Arab-led parties had little chance of changing the status quo, Arab voters had little incentive to vote: for many years, a significantly lower proportion of Arab citizens went to the polls than did their Jewish counterparts.

That began to change in 2015 with the creation of the Joint List, which for the first time brought together four parties representing the Arab public on a single electoral ticket and which became the third-largest faction in the Knesset. By embracing a broader vision for our society, grounded in Jewish-Arab solidarity and opposition to the racism of the right, the Joint List demonstrated that Palestinian citizens of Israel could play a new and more influential role in the country’s political system. Then, under my leadership in 2019, the Joint List decided to back Gantz’s bid to unseat Netanyahu. The decision was highly controversial, both within the Arab electorate and across Israeli society. Many Arab voters were deeply ambivalent about supporting a potential government led by a former army chief whose political positions, particularly on the status of Palestinian citizens of Israel and the occupation, differed sharply from their own. Nevertheless, we were prepared to take that political risk because we understood that defeating Netanyahu was more important.

In practice, our willingness to cooperate with the Zionist opposition was not, in fact, reciprocated. The Jewish opposition parties wanted our votes but could not bring themselves to accept us as equal political partners. And, ultimately, Gantz decided to form a government with Netanyahu instead. Nevertheless, the experience showed that Jewish-Arab political cooperation could open up new horizons for a large segment of the public. Since then, every time that Arab-led parties have recommended or supported the effort of a Jewish candidate to form a government, or whenever the Israeli center-left has depended on their support, additional segments of Israeli society have become more open to this kind of cooperation.

In 2021–22, an Arab party, Raam, joined the short-lived government led by Naftali Bennett and Yair Lapid. I had serious reservations about the model of partnership that emerged, which seemed to rest on the expectation that Arab political representatives would limit their involvement to civilian and domestic matters, while refraining from addressing political and national questions, particularly the occupation and the Palestinian issue. I reject this distinction. We are part of the Palestinian people, and our identity and political concerns cannot be separated from our citizenship in Israel.

But these kinds of slow and painful steps are how political change often happens in national struggles. It does not always begin with a moral or democratic awakening. Sometimes, it begins with pragmatic necessity. When there is no other option, people begin to reconsider what had once been seen as impossible. In the current election, that dynamic has become even more important. After the unending crisis of the past four years, many Israeli voters, including both Arabs and Jews as well as the leaders of most of the Zionist opposition parties, have concluded that getting rid of the Netanyahu government is more necessary than ever.

In recent past elections, voter turnout among Palestinian citizens of Israel has generally remained between 50 and 60 percent. What explains the unprecedented 20-percentage-point surge in turnout in the October election? Three developments stand out: the war of annihilation in Gaza and the killing of tens of thousands of Palestinians; the daily racism and hostility that Arab citizens now face in the streets, in public spaces, and in virtually every aspect of their lives; and the crime that has spiraled out of control in Arab communities under the national security minister, Itamar Ben-Gvir, whose policies have allowed it to flourish, and the relentless settler violence against Palestinians in the West Bank enabled by Finance Minister Bezalel Smotrich. All the grief and frustration accumulated over these years were brought to the ballot box.

But there was another crucial factor as well. In the run-up to the election, Arab citizens, following the nearly even polls between Eisenkot and Netanyahu, could see in black and white just how much the outcome would depend on them. Voting this time would be more than a political choice. It was for many a personal responsibility and a way of taking control of their own future. That is why hundreds of thousands of Palestinian citizens of Israel made their way to the polls, defying years of efforts to silence them and undermine their political agency. Many understood that the future direction of the country might be quite literally in their hands.

I saw elderly people, like my friend Jumah from Jisr al-Zarqa, taking to the streets and campaigning for the Joint List. Many of them had lived through the 1948 nakba, or “catastrophe,” which accompanied Israel’s establishment and involved terrible massacres, the destruction of hundreds of Palestinian villages and towns, and the displacement of around 750,000 Palestinians. I saw young people debating politics in the cafes and restaurants of my beloved Haifa, persuading their friends to vote. I saw women and men from Nazareth, Umm al-Fahm, Sakhnin, Rahat, Akko, Kafr Yasif, Majd al-Krum, and countless other towns and villages united by a shared sense of purpose. I saw political consciousness become a living, collective force. For a moment, the boundaries between generations and communities seemed to disappear.

The result marks a remarkable advance of the process of Arab participation in the Israeli political system that began in 2019. In this election, Arab citizens of Israel turned out in record numbers, dramatically narrowing the historical turnout gap with Jewish voters. And that has made another political reality increasingly clear: Israel’s liberal and centrist camp cannot form a government without the parties that represent the Arab public. If it excludes them, as well as the ultra-Orthodox and the far right, it will not reach the 61 seats needed. This is the demographic reality. Israeli Jews who oppose Netanyahu must decide whom they are willing to build a political future with.

The debate about what Eisenkot should do and whether he should ask for Arab support is now unfolding beyond the Knesset, in people’s homes, cafés, in universities, and on the street. It is a deeply controversial question, and many Israelis disagree strenuously about it. Yet for the Arab-led parties, gaining sufficient importance to be able to provoke these conversations is an achievement. The fact that this debate is even taking place shows how the political process has caused Israelis to consider possibilities that until recently seemed unimaginable.

In the end, Eisenkot may have few other options if he wants to replace Netanyahu, as he has promised. In most cases, political parties support coalitions to advance positive changes and achieve concrete goals. But there are moments when political cooperation is necessary for a different reason: to prevent something far worse. The most immediate and overriding objective today is to bring an end to the far-right Netanyahu government. That is a priority on which both the Zionist opposition and the Arab-led parties can agree.

Beyond this shared basic goal, the Arab-led parties should use their political influence to work on two levels. First, they must seek tangible improvements in their constituents’ lives: combating organized crime in Arab communities, securing budgets, addressing the housing crisis, ending the brutal treatment of Palestinians living under occupation in the West Bank, and pushing for a permanent cease-fire and the reconstruction of Gaza. These are fundamental issues of human dignity and civilian life. On a broader level, they should continue to push for real progress on Israeli-Palestinian peace that is ultimately based on two states coexisting, side by side.

I do not expect the current Israeli political establishment to embrace these positions, but that does not mean that the Arab parties should not raise them at every opportunity. At the same time, the Arab parties can use their newfound political clout to build alliances between Jews and Arabs around shared political and strategic goals. The resulting partnerships must be about more than parliamentary arrangements; it must also create the foundations for a different future. That will require Jewish and Arab citizens of Israel to engage with one another on the basis of equality, rather than mere political convenience.

Thus far, Eisenkot has given few indications that he is prepared to go down this path. Since the election, it has been encouraging to see him condemn Netanyahu for fueling what he described as a “racist and violent” campaign against Israel’s Arab citizens. Yet many of the mainstream Zionist parties in Eisenkot’s camp have vowed not to work with the Arab parties. Still, Eisenkot must consider what that would mean. Thirty years from now, people will ask how Jewish supremacy became so normalized that even politicians who called themselves democrats accepted the exclusion of one-fifth of their fellow citizens. They will ask why Israel’s opposition could recognize the danger posed by Netanyahu’s government but not confront the assumptions that made his politics possible.

There is an irony in the fact that, in a country that seeks to keep political hegemony exclusively in Jewish hands, the Palestinian minority, after decades of struggle for political recognition and equal rights, has emerged as one of the most significant forces shaping the country’s political future. We have come to understand that we must be courageous and cooperate with the Zionist parties of the center and left in order to oust Netanyahu, Ben-Gvir, and Smotrich. The question now is: Do these parties, which call themselves democratic and liberal, have the same courage?

#5909. FBI probes second cyberattack in months as hackers breach key crime database, sources say

Fox News' Madeline Rivera provides details on a reported cyberattack after a string of outages with the FBI at the center of this attack. Rivera says this is the second breach in recent months.

Hackers breached the FBI's National Crime Information Center (NCIC), three sources familiar with the matter told Fox News, marking the second cyberattack against the bureau in months.

The FBI is investigating the hack to determine the unauthorized entry point, but, for now, it is unclear what information was compromised, sources said.

The NCIC database is a critical information-sharing tool for federal, state and local authorities, helping them track fugitives, monitor registered sex offenders, check whether someone has failed a gun background check, among other information.

In an interview with Fox News' Kayleigh McEnany Saturday morning, President Donald Trump said he had "just heard" about the hack.

The FBI headquarters building in Washington D.C., Feb. 20, 2026. (Celal Gunes/Anadolu via Getty Images)

TECH OUTAGE CASCADE EXPOSES US VULNERABILITIES TO FOREIGN CYBER THREATS

"I will say that it’s not good. I don’t like that," Trump added.

The FBI did not immediately respond to a request for comment. CNN first reported the hack.

In September, a group called ShinyHunters hacked into the FBI's job portal website intending to steal sensitive employment records.

The mysterious group, known for extortion plots, claimed it stole sensitive information belonging to almost every FBI agent, including names, home addresses, Social Security numbers, assignments and, in some cases, the names of family members.

FBI agents walk down Florida Avenue, between 14th Street NW and 13th Strreet NW, Aug. 13, 2025, in Washington, D.C. (Tasos Katopodis/Getty Images)

FBI STRIKES BACK AT HACKING GROUP WITH OVERSEAS ARREST OF ALLEGED LEADER

Canadian cybersecurity executive Edward Dubrovsky was arrested Oct. 8 and faces charges in the FBI's investigation into ShinyHunters, according to court records and law enforcement sources who spoke to Reuters.

The details of Dubrovsky's alleged crimes are unknown because the court records in relation to his case remain sealed.

FBI Director Kash Patel announced the arrest of ShinyHunters "suspected co-conspirator" on Oct. 9, a day after Dubrovsky was taken into custody.

FBI Director Kash Patel speaks during a news conference at the Department of Justice in Washington, D.C., Dec. 4, 2025. (Daniel Heuer/AFP via Getty Images)

"This is the latest arrest this FBI has made in a matter of days involving this network, as we work non-stop to dismantle the group, pursue new leads and evidence and act quickly," Patel said in a statement at the time.

"We will continue to work closely with our partners to disrupt what’s left of the ShinyHunters group and their associates, no matter where they operate."

In 2023, hackers infiltrated a computer network at the FBI's New York field office used in child sexual exploitation cases, a system that also stored images tied to the Jeffrey Epstein probe, CNN previously reported.

#5907. States Are Playing Serious Games on Virtual Battlefields

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Review: States Are Playing Serious Games on Virtual Battlefields

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

The biggest single media event of the decade took place on Thursday, and most of Washington missed it. Grand Theft Auto VI, the latest installment of the most popular video game series in the world, racked up nearly $1.5 billion in sales even before it launched, already making back what may have been a more-than-a-billion dollar budget. In the next week, total sales are projected to hit over $3 billion. The previous game in the series still makes half a billion dollars a year through its online version alone. (For comparison purposes, Taylor Swift’s Eras tour, the biggest concert tour of all time, took in just over $2 billion, while Avengers: Endgame grossed roughly $2.9 billion.)

Video games are cultural juggernauts, and governments have noticed. As industry consultant George Osborn points out in his new book, Power Play: Video Games, Politics, and the Battle for Global Influence, video gaming, once a solitary activity, has become a largely communal one. Online play creates a “third place” where people, especially the young, can mingle. As Osborn puts it, “what happens in video games communities bleeds out into reality—all the way into our political discourse.”

The biggest single media event of the decade took place on Thursday, and most of Washington missed it. Grand Theft Auto VI, the latest installment of the most popular video game series in the world, racked up nearly $1.5 billion in sales even before it launched, already making back what may have been a more-than-a-billion dollar budget. In the next week, total sales are projected to hit over $3 billion. The previous game in the series still makes half a billion dollars a year through its online version alone. (For comparison purposes, Taylor Swift’s Eras tour, the biggest concert tour of all time, took in just over $2 billion, while Avengers: Endgame grossed roughly $2.9 billion.)

Power Play: Video Games, Politics, and the Battle for Global Influence, George E. Osborn, Prometheus, 376 pp., July 2026, $34.95

Video games are cultural juggernauts, and governments have noticed. As industry consultant George Osborn points out in his new book, Power Play: Video Games, Politics, and the Battle for Global Influence, video gaming, once a solitary activity, has become a largely communal one. Online play creates a “third place” where people, especially the young, can mingle. As Osborn puts it, “what happens in video games communities bleeds out into reality—all the way into our political discourse.”

Under Crown Prince Mohammed bin Salman, for instance, Saudi Arabia has become a major force in video games, mostly through the power of capital. The prince, himself a keen gamer, is trying to encourage the creation of a domestic industry. He’s had little success so far, but Saudi money has succeeded in buying up existing companies, such as the giant Electronic Arts for $55 billion and the company that created Pokémon Go for $3.5 billion.

Mohammed bin Salman wants to make money. But, as Osborn points out, he also wants to get gullible or greedy Westerners to help whitewash his corrupt, misogynistic autocracy. Buying gaming companies may be a more expensive route than donating to D.C. think tanks, but also perhaps a more effective one. Some industry insiders have already flagged worries about Saudi censorship in their games.

An artist rendering of “The Line” in an undated image. NEOM

But if the Saudi government is shaping video games, the influence is going the other way as well. Consider some of the crown prince’s grandiose projects. His plan for The Line, a 106-mile-long and 650-foot-wide city, appeared to be inspired by the futuristic cities that appear in games like Cyberpunk 2077 and Deus Ex. The difference, of course, was that in reality The Line project was suspended after only 1.5 miles of foundation were dug.

That feedback loop between gaming and reality is also evident when looking at another of Osborn’s case studies, Russia. Especially since the invasion of Ukraine in 2022, Russian studios have churned out propaganda games such as Squad 22: ZOV, which Osborn describes as an “objectively bad video game” but also a hub for anti-Ukrainian memes and a gathering place for ultranationalists.

The fantasies of Russian games hook back into wider influence efforts. With the help of influencers such as streamer GrishaPutin, these games aim to boost morale and recruitment while also luring in foreign fighters, roughly 11,000 of whom have died fighting on the Russian side. Australian Daryl Fladung has been missing, believed dead, for several months after being recruited to come and fight for Russia (and post propaganda videos) through online war games. His last videos show him terrified and in pain.

Australian Daryl Fladung (right) and two fellow Russia-supporting British nationals on arrival in Moscow in an undated photo. Telegram: Ben Stimson

Russia’s reactionary politics are also attractive to some gamers. The gender split in gaming is roughly equal nowadays but the third place of popular games has a habit of becoming dominated by the loudest young men. It’s not uncommon for female players to conceal their gender, or even use a voice changer, to avoid harassment.

That too has bled back into politics. Video game writers are generally liberal progressives, and there was a big push toward broadening representation in games in the 2010s, with female protagonists and people of color becoming more normalized. This was welcomed by many gamers but prompted a reactionary fury from others. The movement that resulted was Gamergate, which became part of the far-right’s attempt to woo younger male voters, with the encouragement of Steve Bannon. While it may have helped tip elections toward Donald Trump, it failed to produce any good games of its own.

Despite being the biggest video game market in the world, compared to Moscow, Beijing is far more reluctant to throw its weight around in gaming propaganda overseas. That’s in part because of a puritan communist dislike of any form of fun, leading to repeated crackdowns on minors playing games online and long delays before new games are officially approved.

Censorship at home is massive but, as Osborn notes, “Chinese games businesses invested billions of dollars in games businesses across the world to soften the blows landing on them from the CCP.” (pg. 151). Apart from some policing around Hong Kong and Taiwanese representation in games, those investments have mostly remained business-focused rather than trying to impose political limits. As with much else, China’s focus has been domestic control first, and foreign influence second.

The Honor of Kings International Championship in Shenzhen, China, on Dec. 3, 2022. Honor of Kings, a multiplayer online battle arena game, is operated by Tencent, China’s largest video game publisher.Honor of Kings/Tencent

But perhaps gaming’s biggest global influence lies in its role driving the AI industry, a story Osborn doesn’t cover in depth because much of it is so recent. Gaming depends on video cards, which are built with the high-end chips that also fuel the AI revolution. In fiscal year 2018, for instance, Nvidia made roughly 57 percent of its income from gaming. Last year, that figure was 7 percent, with roughly 90 percent coming from AI data centers.

Osborn highlights the role gaming played in driving forward the innovation needed for newer and better technology, especially in chip design. But the shift of the industry toward AI has left gamers deeply angry at Nvidia, a company they once adored. (As a result, some of the best investigative reporting on Jensen Huang’s fall and Nvidia’s role in evading U.S. sanctions on China has come out of the games media.) Gaming hardware used to be getting cheaper and cheaper; the same setup would be half the price two years later. Thanks to the AI race, the reverse is now true. The gaming PC I bought for $1,600 the week Trump was elected for a second time, anticipating tariff rises, now retails at $2,600. It might seem a little silly to care deeply about your toys getting more expensive. But the contestation between gamers and AI companies is a microcosm of a wider issue: Who controls technological power?

Screen captures of the survival horror game Resident Evil Requiem, released in February, showing Nvidia’s new graphics rendering model DLSS 5. It uses AI to infuse pixels with photorealistic lighting and materials. NVIDIA

Personal computers have been the domain of tinkerers and inventors since they were first introduced at home. Now Sam Altman has put forward, then rapidly retracted, the idea that having RAM sitting in your own PC is “terrible” because it should be used by mega-corporations instead. In this context, the growing revolt against AI in video games—with players boycotting games that use AI art, AI writing, or even AI code—feels like part of something bigger.

Osborn’s final chapters are dedicated to promoting games that educate citizens and support the fight for democracy worldwide, from pro-Ukrainian stories to “green game jams” to promote climate awareness. But as long as the industry, and the technology that powers it, is in the hands of autocrats and tech tycoons, these aren’t the games we’ll be playing.

This post appeared in the FP Weekend newsletter, a weekly showcase of book reviews, deep dives, and features. Sign up here.

James Palmer is a deputy editor at Foreign Policy. Bluesky: @beijingpalmer.bsky.social

#5906. Avoiding Colombia’s Escalation Trap

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

ELIZABETH DICKINSON is Deputy Director for Latin America at International Crisis Group.

How a U.S.-Backed Offensive Could Help Bogotá Reclaim Its Conflict Zones

On September 21, José Luis Pérez Villanueva, better known as “El Cholo”—the second-in-command and financial architect of the criminal group Autodefensas Conquistadores de la Sierra Nevada—was killed during a Colombian police operation on the rural outskirts of Santa Marta. Within hours, militants from that organization launched a dramatic campaign of terror. The group, known colloquially as Los Pachenca, demanded that businesses across the city shutter. Some 90 percent of shops did, as videos circulated of gunmen shooting up storefronts that stayed open.

For two decades, Los Pachenca has been the unquestioned criminal authority in the beachfront city and the adjacent Sierra Nevada mountain range, charging extortion payments from businesses and residents and controlling the flow of drugs out of the Port of Santa Marta, en route to Europe and beyond. But the level of control the group exhibited during the strike was particularly jarring, given that Santa Marta is the vacation home of many of the country’s elite and a common destination for international visitors. Colombia’s government responded by militarizing the city, sending in hundreds of soldiers and police officers, who rolled into town on the main highway with tanks and air support. Many shops still stayed closed until the morning of September 25, when the group announced that it was ending the strike. For locals, Los Pachenca’s reaction was a message about who remains in charge.

Calm has since returned to Santa Marta, but the incident may be indicative of risks to come. Colombia’s newly elected president, Abelardo de la Espriella, has promised a steep military escalation against illegal groups, which have grown precipitously in recent years. As he does so, armed and criminal organizations will undoubtedly respond. Often, their target will not be the state. Instead, they will likely direct their violence against the civilian populations, whom they have spent years coopting and coercing. That is because armed and criminal groups across the country have learned that their true leverage is their ability to govern local communities—and to intimidate and punish them into silence. Even when they do attack state forces, their use of weapons such as improvised explosives and makeshift drones means that bystanders often suffer. In one recent incident in the Pacific coast department of Valle del Cauca, four drone attacks launched by an armed group in a single day left five civilians injured.

These dangers are particularly acute as de la Espriella’s pledge of a zero-tolerance crackdown against armed and criminal groups has won enthusiastic support from Washington. Both countries want big and visible victories against these outfits. They triumphantly announced a new era of cooperation when the president took office in August, including a promise of upgraded military ties. (The United States has not yet publicly pledged new weapons, but it is already providing greater intelligence and logistical assistance on some operations.) After two years of tensions and threats between President Donald Trump and leftist former President Gustavo Petro, the political realignment means that Colombia is once again the United States’ closest defense ally in South America. This is likely to continue, despite major Democratic wins in the U.S. Congress, as the relationship’s focus on security has a long history of bipartisan support.

This week, during U.S. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth’s visit to Bogotá, the de la Espriella administration signaled that it would seek congressional approval for joint military operations with the United States in Colombia. This cooperation will likely yield initial results. The Colombian government has said that it aims to ramp up attacks against armed and criminal groups and to go after leaders who enjoyed protection as counterparts in peace talks during the past administration.

Yet Colombia’s true challenge is not how to carry out attacks but what to do afterward. Surgical strikes, which see the military enter and exit quickly, leave civilians more vulnerable in the aftermath, as armed groups retaliate against alleged informants and tighten their grip in areas they control. Bogotá must ensure that every operation includes contingencies to anticipate criminal groups’ reactions and build on successful offensives by weakening their grasp on communities. The government will need to strengthen the police’s ability to patrol contested areas, a costly but necessary process that the United States and other partners should support.

The stakes are high. Bogotá has a chance to leverage its increased military pressure to open the door for the state, rewriting the story of who governs conflict areas. If the government misses this opportunity to consolidate state control, however, Colombia risks entering an escalatory spiral in which civilians pay a mounting price for a campaign intended to protect them.

Armed and criminal groups have spent the last decade expanding their reach across Colombia. According to the local think tank Pares, the number of municipalities with an armed group presence rose from 212 in 2018 to 518 last year—or about half of all municipalities. This deterioration reversed dramatic and hard-won improvements in security. After the historically violent decades of the 1990s and 2000s, military pressure and negotiations yielded a 2016 peace agreement demobilizing the former Revolutionary Armed Forces of Colombia (FARC), once the country’s dominant rebel group. Colombia’s countryside breathed a sigh of relief as the FARC pulled out of areas where it had been in control, which represented a quarter of the country’s land.

The FARC’s exit, however, did not pave the way for Colombia’s highly centralized and bureaucratic state to claim control of those vast geographies. Instead, old armed groups such as the National Liberation Army and newer ones such as the Gaitanista Army of Colombia (also known as the Gulf Clan) saw a chance to expand their access to trafficking routes, coca crops, illegal mines, and, ultimately, vulnerable rural populations. Kidnapping for extortion and mass displacement began to resurface, shattering optimism that progress would move in one direction. Over the last four years, membership in illegal groups has nearly doubled, to some 27,000 fighters today.

As a candidate, de la Espriella successfully harnessed public frustration by promising mano dura, or iron-fisted tactics, including aerial attacks and increased incarceration, facilitated by the construction of mega-prisons. He blamed his predecessor’s administration for accelerating insecurity. The first leftist to be elected president in modern Colombia, Petro had entered office in 2022 vowing to de-emphasize the military’s role and instead hold peace talks with various armed organizations. But not every group participated, and many of the ones that did sought to take advantage of cease-fires to cement their control over communities. Midway through his term, Petro pivoted back toward deploying the armed forces. But much of the harm was already done.

Colombia’s new president has been in office only a little more than 100 days, but the shift is marked. De la Espriella carried out three bombing raids in his first weeks. He has extradited dozens of individuals to the United States, including senior leaders, and the administration has killed or captured many others. The president is doggedly pushing to restart aerial fumigation of coca crops, which the state carried out in the early 2000s to try to reduce the number of hectares under cultivation; the practice has since been heavily restricted by court rulings over concerns about health and environmental consequences. Both in private and in public, military leaders laud the government’s new support for their operations.

The Trump administration has also welcomed these developments, which align with a regional trend favoring Washington’s approach on counternarcotics. For Colombia, the new era of security cooperation with the United States represents a return to the status quo ante of the last quarter century, which was briefly disrupted by the Petro administration. U.S.-Colombian military ties were forged through antidrug efforts and deepened in 1999 with Plan Colombia, a decadelong $10 billion aid package that funded, among other things, the expansion and professionalization of the security forces and fumigation. The Colombian police and military are deeply integrated with their American counterparts. They use U.S.- and NATO-compatible systems, travel by the hundreds to the United States each year for instruction, and receive American intelligence. In nearly every operation, at least one step (and often far more) in the logistical chain is either coordinated with Washington or relies on U.S. support. That relationship never fully stopped under Petro, but the two sides were notably wary of each other. Now bilateral trust appears to be restored.

Even before the elections, de la Espriella impressed U.S. officials by outlining his plans to reclaim security momentum for the state. The Colombian president is now acting on several explicit U.S. priorities, including extraditions and legislation to designate numerous groups as terrorist organizations. Meanwhile, Bogotá’s coca eradication targets are even more ambitious than Washington’s. At a personal level, relations are strong, too. Two of Colombia’s top military officials previously served as envoys to U.S. Southern Command.

Bogotá says that it intends to deliver results for Washington’s antidrug goals and has explicitly asked for additional support in the form of funding, aircraft, surveillance technologies, and maintenance. Although Colombia’s military remains among the best trained in the region, its logistical needs are critical. Only 13 of Colombia’s 53 Black Hawk helicopters are in working order, and defense spending has shrunk as a share of the national government’s budget, from 22.4 percent in 2003 to just 14.7 percent in 2025, with a growing proportion of those funds spent on salaries and pensions.

How much the United States will provide remains to be seen. In August, U.S. Southern Command’s General Francis Donovan discussed ways to shore up the deteriorating air fleet during a visit to Bogotá. The United States has also redeployed surveillance drones from Africa to Colombia and Ecuador, where they are already aiding intelligence collection. But Secretary of State Marco Rubio was reluctant to pledge specific aid while in Colombia in September, and most of this assistance is likely to be in kind or to stay in the hands of the U.S. military and contractors, with little entering the Colombian budget. Much of it appears to be focused on short-term offensive wins.

Colombia has returned to tactics such as aerial bombardment because they arguably worked against the FARC. Founded as a Marxist rebellion in the 1960s, the group behaved like a conventional army, dressed in uniforms, and sought out combat with the state. This meant that the Colombian military could bomb encampments and that capturing leaders left the organization at times rudderless. Slowly chipping away at the FARC’s hierarchy and control helped push the group to finally negotiate its disarmament, beginning in 2012.

But today, those same strategies are yielding diminishing returns. The current generation of armed and criminal groups has little interest in confronting the state. Instead, they behave like businesses, seeking to safeguard access to natural resources, supply chains, and trafficking routes. They live within the civilian population and rarely wear uniforms. Leaders are easily replaced and hierarchies are fluid. Critically, Colombia’s armed groups have learned that wars with the military are costly but that controlling local populations—through intimidation and violence, but also by offering economic incentives, jobs, and services—is comparatively cheap. The battlefield has evolved from a conventional conflict to a contest over who can govern territories and the people living there.

Santa Marta offers an example of how this new model of warfare works. On most days, an outsider in the city would never perceive the presence of Los Pachenca. The group’s members do not patrol or identify themselves. Yet residents know them well from the weekly or monthly extortion payments they are forced to make. Local companies and government officials essentially view the group as an inescapable reality. Pushing back carries a violent price, but paying a protection tax, taking a bribe, or facilitating illegal commerce keeps business running smoothly.

The criminal governance of rural areas is even more suffocating. Groups that emerged from splinters of the former FARC, known as dissidents, often force local residents to hold identity cards, issued so that militants have a full census of who lives in the area and can detect possible enemy fighters. In the Amazon region, these groups keep a land registry more complete than that of the state so that they know exactly whom and how much to extort.

With this intense surveillance over society, armed and criminal groups frequently deploy the civilian population as human shields against military offensives and police patrols. The most egregious example is the widespread use of forcibly recruited children as bodyguards for senior commanders. De la Espriella’s initial bombardments, which killed at least six children, were even temporarily halted by a court over concerns about minors. Senior officers admit that these types of casualties are inevitable around high-value targets, and that the groups know that the military is far less likely to proceed if it means killing children.

Armed and criminal groups will also try to use the military operations to their political advantage. When children are killed in bombardments, for example, FARC dissidents spin the local narrative into a story about how the state cares little for rural populations. The argument sticks because there is rarely a credible state presence that can serve and protect these same communities and earn their trust.

There are more immediate consequences, as well. In the aftermath of bombardments and high-level captures, armed and criminal groups often undertake what amounts to a purge of the population, killing or exiling anyone they believe to have collaborated with the state. And when the state takes out a leader, intra-group violence frequently ensues as former associates compete to take his place. Many residents shelter inside to avoid being caught up in the fighting, leaving children without school and parents without income. If new splinter groups emerge, the combat for control over neighborhoods can place even more civilians in the crossfire. This fallout widens the gap between immediate tactical results and long-term security gains. Taking out a senior leader wins applause, but conditions may well worsen on the ground.

De la Espriella’s administration has rightly identified winning territorial control, permanently dislodging criminal groups, and restoring state governance as its ultimate goals. The dilemma it now faces—exacerbated by U.S. support that focuses on attention-grabbing wins—is how to both achieve short-term results and ensure that these do not unleash new waves of violence, retaliation against civilians, and, in turn, criminal consolidation.

The challenge is twofold. First, the military needs to anticipate groups’ violent reactions and to safeguard the population. In the case of Santa Marta, for example, the government could have increased police presence in the city in the hours after El Cholo was killed. Before the next bombardment or high-level capture, the state should ensure that a ground team of soldiers or military police is ready to hold the ground, protect the community, and prevent the armed group from swiftly stepping back in to reclaim territory.

The second challenge—establishing lasting territorial control—is trickier. A permanent rural security presence might offer the best chance for Colombia’s government to change the direction of the conflict. The country’s military leadership controls only the tip of the spear, not the rest of the state. Time and again, governments “send only the military to the hardest areas,” a retired senior officer told Crisis Group. Moreover, soldiers are operating under heavy pressure for quick wins, which are measured in the number of combatants and especially high-value targets that are killed or captured. There is a real risk that by focusing largely on these statistics, the state will overlook more comprehensive policies necessary to achieve durable security.

Pressure to deliver fast results has in the past incentivized unacceptable practices. In the early 2000s, intense political demands for combat kills fueled elaborate schemes in which soldiers murdered thousands of civilians and counted them as guerrilla fatalities, a scandal known as “false positives.” These crimes deeply compromised public trust in the security forces, and the military has worked for years to build a culture and procedures that ensure they do not happen again. Today, the country’s senior military leadership is likely the first and best brake on any attempts to use excessive force. Commanders say that they are cognizant of the need to guard against rules being slowly stretched until they are broken. “We need a stronger security approach, but it also worries us when we hear there is pressure for ‘results,’” one officer said. But U.S. rhetoric and behavior, including its decision to sink what it claims are drug-smuggling boats on the high seas, compound these risks.

Success, then, may demand a strategy that is ultimately anchored in a much stronger and larger police force, fit for the job of safeguarding civilians rather than merely carrying out offensive strikes. Military operations can generate a window of opportunity by shuffling dynamics on the ground or removing certain dangerous players. Yet those reconfigurations will turn out in the state’s favor only if the resulting vacuum is eventually filled by reliable law enforcement. Take, for example, an operation that temporarily dislodges a group from a rural hamlet. If the military acts alone, it will likely enter and leave in a matter of days or even hours, allowing the armed group to quickly return. If, however, the offensive is followed up with permanent police patrols, access to the justice system, and social interventions, criminals will have a harder time regaining control.

This, of course, is easier said than done, given the government’s resource constraints. To create effective and permanent patrols, police departments operating in the countryside need more officers and equipment to maintain a presence in the most remote communities. Colombia has roughly 180,000 uniformed police officers, a figure that, per capita, puts it on the lower end of OECD countries, many of which are far more urbanized and therefore easier to patrol. In Colombia’s conflict-affected areas, outgunned officers must often stay inside their local stations for their own safety, limiting their ability to build trust and channels of communication with the population. Other state institutions, such as the justice system, say that they cannot establish a durable foothold in villages without guarantees of protection in the medium term—exactly what rural policing should strive to provide. The U.S. State Department’s Bureau of International Narcotics and Law Enforcement Affairs has funded the building and equipping of rural police stations in the past; it should expand this work. International partners with long-standing expertise in rural policing, such as Canada and France, could provide critical support, as well.

In cities, the challenge is different. In Santa Marta, for example, the state is present, but it is weak and vulnerable to the pressure and cooptation of the same groups that it must confront. The police need both more officers and better working conditions—including salaries, benefits, access to education, and social support for families—that could help them resist bribes. Regular vetting and counterintelligence checks can help ensure that police are fighting on the side of the state and not its adversaries.

Expanding the police will be an expensive, long-term undertaking. In the interim, Colombia could prioritize more personnel for critical areas and ensure that officers have a strong grounding in community policing, allowing them to understand the most pressing risks facing civilians. That means regular outreach to communities to build trust and make certain that officers are seen as having a stake in local well-being. The United States, meanwhile, must continue to help Bogotá strengthen its police and courts with direct financial and technical assistance.

Colombia, backed by renewed U.S. support, cannot afford to repeat past mistakes or ignore how the conflict has changed. Bogotá must therefore plan for and capitalize on military pressure to do something no government in Colombia’s history has done: govern well in conflict areas.

#5904. Trump says Vance, Rubio are 'doing fantastically' ahead of 2028 Republican presidential primary

President Donald Trump joins 'Saturday in America' to discuss the recent FBI hack, bipartisanship and his recent trip to China.

President Donald Trump told Fox News' "Saturday in America" that JD Vance and Marco Rubio are "doing fantastically" and are "both very, very popular" when asked who he would like to see run on the Republican and Democratic tickets ahead of the 2028 presidential election.

Trump spoke highly of the vice president and secretary of state after being asked for his choices by host Kayleigh McEnany.

"Well, I think it's boiling down now. You're starting to see some people emerge. I was a little surprised to see Kamala [Harris] is doing pretty well," Trump said about his 2024 challenger and former vice president when discussing the Democrats' options.

TRUMP SIGNALS SURPRISING APPROACH TO CHOOSING HIS SUCCESSOR AS GOP'S 2028 FIELD GROWS

"I think AOC [Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez, D-N.Y.] is doing well and just watching that, you know, look, you could have people. And, most likely, I think, at least on the Democrat side, you're going to have maybe somebody that nobody ever heard of," Trump continued.

"That's a real possibility. Somebody that maybe is very good but not known.

President Donald Trump, Vice President JD Vance and Secretary of State Marco Rubio hold a news conference in the James S. Brady Press Briefing Room of the White House in Washington, D.C., Friday, Nov. 20, 2026. (Sipa USA via Reuters Connect)

"On the Republican side, you know, you could have — there are quite a few people that could be very good. Obviously, JD's vice president. Marco is right up there. The two of them are doing fantastically," Trump added.

"They're both very, very popular. But you could have some surprises. We have some very capable people."

In addition to Vance and Rubio, other Republicans whose names have been mentioned as possible 2028 candidates include Sen. Ted Cruz of Texas, Florida Gov. Ron DeSantis, former Vice President Mike Pence, former Georgia Gov. Brian Kemp and former Virginia Gov. Glenn Youngkin, among others.

TRUMP RESPONDS AFTER VANCE BLAMES IRAN WAR FOR MIDTERM LOSSES, VP SAYS HIS JOB IS TO SUPPORT PRESIDENT

Former Vice President Kamala Harris speaks to Democratic supporters at a rally at Clark Atlanta University in Atlanta, Ga., on Oct. 29, 2026. Harris was campaigning with Sen. Jon Ossoff ahead of early voting. (Getty Images)

When Trump was asked by McEnany if he thinks it "should be an open primary with lots of robust debates, or (if) there should be some sort of unity ticket that the party coalesces around," the president said, "Could be either."

"There could be some great unity tickets. I'll tell you what, the unity tickets are fantastic. You save all that money. You use it against the Democrats. I think it could be either," Trump added.

"It's going to be a very interesting political season, and it's now just about, in another few weeks, it's going to be starting hot and heavy."

Trump also vowed that, regardless of who the GOP nominee is, "we'll be involved and we'll be watching."

Greg Norman is a reporter at Fox News Digital.

#5903. EU, U.K. Agree to a Brexit Reset

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

World Brief: EU, U.K. Agree to a Brexit Reset

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at joint EU-U.K. efforts to reverse Brexit, Russia’s latest incursions into Poland, and the future of Myanmar’s Rohingya population.

Ten years after the United Kingdom voted to leave the European Union, Brexit is back on the table. This time, though, London appears eager to reverse some of the barriers created by Brexit and bridge the decade-long divide between Britain and the bloc.

Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at joint EU-U.K. efforts to reverse Brexit, Russia’s latest incursions into Poland, and the future of Myanmar’s Rohingya population.

Ten years after the United Kingdom voted to leave the European Union, Brexit is back on the table. This time, though, London appears eager to reverse some of the barriers created by Brexit and bridge the decade-long divide between Britain and the bloc.

At a highly anticipated EU-U.K. summit in Brussels on Friday, British and EU leaders unveiled a modest deal to begin resetting the relationship. Under the agreement, the EU and United Kingdom will realign their carbon emissions trading systems; relax restrictions on food and agricultural trade—a major win for British farmers who have long complained about tedious export paperwork; and kick-start a “youth experience” program focused on travel, study, and work.

Although these are minor changes compared to the breadth of the 2016 Brexit framework, British Prime Minister Andy Burnham said on Friday that the deal could inject more than $22 billion into the U.K. economy in what he called the “single biggest growth measure we could take in this Parliament.”

“Today we draw the line on a decade of drift and division by reconnecting with old friends, by building a much more constructive relationship with our nearest neighbors and our largest market,” Burnham said.

According to a YouGov poll published in June, a majority of Britons believe that leaving the EU was a mistake and that London should move toward rejoining the bloc. Burnham has capitalized on this discontent, ignoring warnings from Conservative and Reform UK lawmakers who are against reopening the Brexit debate.

“It’s like the emperor’s new clothes—where no one is prepared to say the obvious truth,” Burnham said on Monday ahead of the summit. “We have lost more than we have gained in the last 10 years.” A National Bureau of Economic Research working paper found that by the end of 2025, Brexit had cut British GDP by 6 percent to 8 percent and investment by 12 percent to 13 percent.

Analysts suggest that global insecurity fueled by Russia’s war in Ukraine, the U.S. trade war, and energy market unpredictability have further convinced Britons that a post-Brexit world comes with greater instability.

“Our security and defence partnership, as well as our united support for Ukraine, are central to our commitment to address together the growing geopolitical challenges we face,” European Council President António Costa wrote on X on Friday, adding that the “EU and the UK are both stronger when we work together.”

It is unclear how much closer the bridging of ties will go. For every step toward dismantling Brexit, Burnham is expected to face backlash back home. “This is a total betrayal of Brexit Britain,” far-right Reform UK leader Niger Farage, a chief architect of the 2016 vote, said last month after Burnham floated restoring freedom of movement with the EU.

On Brussels’s side, though, the EU remains open to having “the closest possible relationship” with the United Kingdom, Costa said.

Missile incursions. Two Russian cruise missiles and a suspected drone entered Poland on Friday, adding to the growing number of reported Russian incursions into NATO territory in recent months. The incidents occurred while Russia was carrying out a massive overnight bombardment on Ukraine.

The intrusions coincided with European military exercises in Poland that began on Nov. 9; one Russian missile reportedly crashed only 22 miles from where the drills were taking place. No injuries were reported; however, Polish Defense Minister Wladyslaw Kosiniak-Kamysz said it was “not accidental” that Russia’s incursions and the European military drills occurred in tandem. “We are dealing with an intentional provocation” by Moscow, Polish Prime Minister Donald Tusk added. Moscow has yet to comment.

According to Ukrainian Deputy Energy Minister Artem Nekrasov, Russia’s nighttime barrage also triggered emergency outages across 11 Ukrainian regions as strikes on critical energy infrastructure forced three nuclear power plants to reduce generation. Russian attacks on Friday also reportedly hit apartment buildings, medical facilities, and bridges. Moscow’s Defense Ministry said it targeted sites where weapons were being manufactured.

Genocide ruling. Myanmar’s military-run government on Friday denounced an International Court of Justice (ICJ) ruling that found the country’s security forces guilty of genocide against its minority Rohingya Muslim community. The landmark decision, issued on Thursday, marked the first time that the court has found a country to be directly responsible for the crime.

“The court finds that Myanmar’s security forces killed thousands of Rohingya civilians, perpetrated widespread acts of sexual violence, as well as acts of extreme violence against children, and attacked fleeing Rohingya as they sought to flee in large numbers,” ICJ President Yuji Iwasawa said during a readout of the 9-6 ruling.

Myanmar’s Foreign Ministry called the decision a “great disappointment” and noted that the judgment was not unanimous. It also said that the roughly 1.3 million Rohingya refugees in neighboring Bangladesh will only be allowed to return “when situations permit.”

However, Tom Andrews, the former U.N. special rapporteur on human rights in Myanmar, told Foreign Policy that a 9-6 result is still a “strong majority.” But he said that the international community must take additional steps to ensure that the country’s ruling junta faces real repercussions for its actions. “Because the thing is, if we establish that there is no consequence for genocide, then we can be guaranteed that genocide is going to happen again,” Andrews said. “And it’s only by imposing consequences that we can begin to see justice prevail, not only in this case but in future cases.”

Resetting relations. Chinese President Xi Jinping celebrated a “comprehensive turnaround” in his country’s relationship with Australia on Friday. Speaking on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit, Xi said that Beijing is eager to set aside the two nations’ differences and expand cooperation in several critical sectors, such as artificial intelligence, energy, agriculture, and mining.

China is Australia’s top trading partner. However, ties between the Indo-Pacific powers began to deteriorate in 2020 after Canberra called for an inquiry into the origins of the COVID-19 pandemic, prompting China to impose punitive tariffs on Australian goods. In recent years, though, Australian Prime Minister Anthony Albanese has sought to restore bilateral relations, especially as the future of his country’s critical iron ore industry comes under threat.

For decades, Chinese iron ore purchases have invigorated Australia’s economy; the vital resource was exempt from China’s 2020 duties. But with Beijing diversifying its partnerships in Africa and Latin America, where iron ore can be supplied more cheaply, as well as exercising increased leverage over iron ore pricing, Canberra is worried that its export earnings will take a major hit.

On Wednesday, Iranian forces targeted a U.S. air base located in which country?

While Trump can brag about Xi meeting him on the tarmac in Shenzhen, China, this week, Mexican President Claudia Sheinbaum has something she can brag about from her trip, too: going viral on Chinese social media. After spending the roughly 21-hour trip from Mexico City to Shenzhen (with a stopover in Tijuana, Mexico) sitting in a middle seat on an economy flight, Sheinbaum was greeted at the airport by a military guard and a dancing troupe. But they didn’t meet her at her plane—instead, she arrived to the welcome ceremony via airport bus.

Her down-to-earth travel style seems to have appealed to many Chinese netizens: A hashtag about her arrival was seen 10 million times on the Chinese social media platform Weibo, according to Bloomberg.

The tit-for-tat strikes between Iran and the United States this week marked an end to a roughly two-month lull in such attacks, FP’s John Haltiwanger and Rishi Iyengar write in Situation Report.

#5901. America’s Bad Bargain in Venezuela

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

Javier Corrales is Dwight W. Morrow 1895 Professor of Political Science at Amherst College and the author of Autocracy Rising: How Venezuela Transitioned to Authoritarianism.

U.S. Dealmaking Is Strengthening the Chavista Regime

After the U.S. military captured then Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro in January, the United States set out on a mission to fix what President Donald Trump called a “dead country.” With broad authority over Venezuela’s agenda, officials in the Trump administration promised to free the country from socialism. As Secretary of State Marco Rubio said, when he was called before the Senate in late January, the administration’s ultimate objective was to establish “a friendly, stable, prosperous Venezuela, and democratic, in which all elements of society are represented in free and fair elections.”

Rubio laid out a three-stage framework for managing this democratic transition: stabilization, economic recovery, and, finally, transition. Washington is currently focused on the second stage, recovering the economy, but it is proceeding so quickly and with such little transparency that it is jeopardizing the stated end goal entirely. U.S. dealmaking with the country’s oil sector, in particular, is reifying rather than transforming Venezuela’s economic rentism: a model of governance in which the state controls important sectors of the economy and offers business contracts and market privileges to firms that support its agenda. This is precisely the system that facilitated the Venezuelan state’s rampant corruption over the past 25 years, allowing autocratic leaders to drown out democratic forces and win elections.

As it pursues its economic goals with dizzying speed, the Trump administration is also slow-walking democratization efforts. For months, Washington excluded opposition leader María Corina Machado and her party, Vente Venezuela, from any direct involvement in negotiations. Machado had left the country last December to receive a Nobel Peace Prize after nearly 20 months in hiding, and after Maduro’s removal, the United States did little to help her return. Venezuela’s pro-democracy forces have thus been left feeling like they are pushing the regime in Caracas to democratize with the acquiescence, but not necessarily the full enthusiasm, of the United States.

Last month, Machado returned to Venezuela, and now she has the potential to change the game. Machado could become the greatest asset or, possibly, the greatest disruptor to Venezuela’s democratic transition. If Machado coordinates with the faction of the opposition that is currently negotiating with the government, any outcome will be seen as legitimate. But if she opposes and speaks out against the result, Venezuelans will find themselves in a familiar and dangerous place: with a divided opposition, a government with few constraints on its power, and a transition in purgatory.

So far, Washington has squandered its immense leverage over Caracas. By dragging out the transition and pursuing questionable economic deals, Washington is endangering not only its own end goal but also the greatest opportunity Venezuela has had to overcome more than a quarter century of autocratic governance.

During Trump’s first term, his administration centered its criticism of Maduro on the country’s socialist model. In February 2019, weeks after recognizing the opposition leader Juan Guaidó as Venezuela’s interim president, Trump promised in a speech that the “twilight hour of socialism has arrived in our hemisphere.” That campaign to oust Maduro failed, but Trump’s diagnosis of what ails Venezuela was wrong to begin with. Over the past two decades, Venezuela has not been operating primarily under a socialist model but an extractivist one. And since January 3, the United States has seemed only to reinforce it.

Venezuela is an oil state. Under Chavismo, the ideology named for Maduro’s predecessor Hugo Chávez, the state used oil revenues as a political slush fund to reward allies. Little went toward investments in the oil sector or the rest of the economy. Secret deals with oil magnates at home and abroad guided most governance decisions. Whereas the government’s critics faced legal attacks, the well-connected received impunity. Chávez implemented elements of socialism, of course, with massive nationalizations and price controls, but the key priority was collusion and special deals. Many of the state’s allies were poor Venezuelans whose votes kept the autocrats in power, but less noticed were the economic elites. These private actors shunned opposition figures and refrained from any public criticism of the regime; in exchange, they accumulated regulatory favors, access to preferential exchange rates, or exclusive contracts with the state. This model even applied internationally; around 2007, Chávez began redirecting oil sales from the United States to China, both to reward China for being so friendly toward the regime and to preempt a possible U.S. divestment from the Venezuelan oil sector. Oil booms in the first decade of the century allowed the country’s populist leaders to build vertical patronage systems that rewarded loyalty and starved out dissent.

Eventually, however, this system ran its course, leaving Venezuela’s oil industry in shambles. In the 1990s, before Chávez took power, oil production hovered around 3.5 million barrels per day; by 2019, oil production had dropped to 700,000 to 800,000 barrels per day. The government has recognized this dismal state; acting President Delcy Rodríguez has said that the oil sector will need as much as $76 billion in external capital to recover over the next five years.

The United States is a plausible partner for Venezuela’s oil recovery. Washington is responsive to high domestic and global demand for oil, especially since the war in Iran has disrupted flows from the Middle East. American oil firms have the resources to rebuild Venezuelan production and, just as important, the refineries needed to process Venezuelan oil, which is among the world’s heaviest and most sulfurous.

But rather than replacing, or at least revamping, this Chavista system, the United States seems to have embraced its structure. In August, the U.S. government struck an agreement with North American Blue Energy Partners (NABEP), a private Venezuelan corporation, that Trump called “the biggest oil deal in the history of the world.” According to the White House fact sheet of the deal, the Venezuelan state granted NABEP a 100-year concession to operate 17 oil fields, which could cover one-fifth of Venezuela’s total proven reserves. The company is led by Alejandro Betancourt, a Venezuelan citizen being investigated in Spain and Switzerland for money laundering and widely regarded as having collaborated with Chávez and acting as an intermediary between Trump and Rodríguez. Meanwhile, the agreement gives the U.S. Defense Department a 35 percent stake in NABEP, and the State Department gets the right to buy 20 percent of NABEP’s oil at the cost of production—meaning NABEP makes no profit on that share—and has preferential access, at market prices, to the rest.

Outside of these details, little is known about the arrangement or how it came to be. There was no competitive public bidding on the fields—a practice that is generally recommended every time governments assign contracts to private and foreign actors—and there was no explanation of how Washington will manage the export proceeds or clarity on who will fund the necessary investments. The U.S. Congress has not reviewed the deal, although Democrats, with a new majority starting in January, have promised to investigate it.

This deal is also wildly unpopular across Venezuela’s political spectrum. Although Venezuelans have generally celebrated the return of U.S. capital to the country, nearly 80 percent of Venezuelans did not support the oil deal, according to a poll released in September by Meganálisis. Even Machado, who rarely criticizes the United States, has expressed reservations, saying that Betancourt has been responsible for “obscene corruption.” Hardcore leftists see the deal as capitalist robbery. Nationalists see it as neoimperialism. Democratic forces see it as a bailout for the dictatorship. Ordinary citizens see it as a missed opportunity to spread Venezuela’s wealth more equitably.

These oil and gas deals could generate an oil boom if investment comes quickly, production capacity is restored, competitive bidding expands, and government spending and welfare institutions are reformed. But as Trump said when celebrating the deal at the United Nations in September, “To the victor belong the spoils,” and the victors, in this case, are Washington, the ruling party, and a private businessman.

The Venezuelan state has also shown little interest in helping the Venezuelan people and directing petrodollars toward human capital or public welfare. Instead, it will likely do what Chavismo perfected in previous booms: bankroll patronage networks, buy off corporate allies, deny funds to competitors, and spend on electoral campaigns. These new deals could very well end up financing the ruling party’s political rebirth.

The rest of the Venezuelan economy’s recovery, meanwhile, remains a mystery. There have been no efforts toward establishing universal property rights, modernizing contract law, reforming the financial sector, or dismantling state monopolies. Without such inclusive economic rights and democratic rules, oil windfalls seldom produce equitable growth. They produce skewed gains for the state and its friends.

The White House sees the deal as locking Venezuela into a secure economic relationship with the United States for at least 100 years. This week, he referred to the country as “our new acquisition.” But this might be a false sense of security. The agreement is so distasteful to so many Venezuelans that, rather than inoculate Venezuela against anti-imperialist socialism, it is making a radical backlash more likely.

Almost a year after Maduro’s departure, very few concrete steps have been taken to organize a free and fair election in Venezuela. Instead, the Rodríguez government and an opposition delegation led by Dinorah Figuera, who is president of Venezuela’s exiled National Assembly in Spain, held a series of talks this fall. The parties did make progress, including agreeing to unblock certain banned websites and to allow the government to reclaim sanctioned gold reserves held by the Bank of England. Most significantly, after months of pressure from the Venezuelan opposition, the Venezuelan government agreed in August to replace all 32 judges in the Supreme Tribunal of Justice, accepting nominations from civil society that included the opposition. This is a gigantic baby step. In Venezuela, the Supreme Tribunal of Justice long served at the center of Venezuela’s famous autocratic legalism. It was the regime’s preeminent weapon to disqualify opposition candidates and elected officials, accepting laws that justified repression, refusing to investigate extrajudicial killings, and persecuting opposition figures on fabricated charges. An independent judiciary could invalidate current laws that criminalize dissent and establish new ones to promote fair electoral rules. Establishing even a modest framework for an independent judiciary is indispensable for any transition to minimal democracy.

And yet committing to reform the judiciary, however important, is not enough. A full transition to democracy will require far more: a new constitution with robust institutional checks and balances, new laws promoting civil liberties, and more oversight of intelligence and security organizations, which have been known to engage in arbitrary detentions and torture. This will also require retraining the law and order apparatus, namely the National Police and the military’s internal security forces, the Bolivarian National Guard, to respect civil rights; redesigning the electoral system and naming independent electoral authorities; and rebuilding civil society and a free press. Judicial reform in isolation will not dismantle an entrenched autocratic regime.

This week, Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth announced an expansion of U.S.-Venezuelan military cooperation, mostly targeted at fighting crime. But fighting crime effectively does not necessarily require more brute force but rather good intelligence, a robust criminal justice system, a professional and law-abiding police force, and investments in community building, namely in strengthening ties of trust between neighbors and police. It is not clear that these elements will be part of this new U.S.-Venezuelan security cooperation. Unless new agreements come with commitments for internal state reform, the United States risks helping a dictatorship acquire even more coercive power.

Most important, the transition process has ignored the will of the people. Most Venezuelans want a fair election, plain and simple. In a Meganálisis poll conducted in April, 87 percent of respondents agreed that the “solution for Venezuela is a presidential election in 2026.” Even with so much material deprivation—inflation is in the triple digits, and close to 70 percent of the population still lives in poverty—the people want to vote first.

Rubio and Rodríguez have argued that stabilization is necessary before elections can be held. But there is reason to believe that elections are not the result of stability but the source of stability. Elections produce legitimacy for authorities. The current authorities not only aided the suppression of the last election, in 2024, but they have also passed the limit of 180 days that an interim government can serve, according to Venezuela’s constitution. Any policy that it advances is, thus, also less legitimate than those put in place by an elected government.

Moreover, Machado’s absence from the process so far risks making any major deal between the government and the opposition fragile. She is the most popular politician in the country and the leader of the largest pro-democracy movement that Latin America has seen since the first decade of this century. Across multiple polls, including by Meganálisis and AtlasIntel, she has approval ratings between 57 and 76, compared with Rodríguez’s single digits, and multiple international accounts have shown that her substitute in the 2024 election, Edmundo González, easily won. This popularity makes Machado a threat to the Chavista regime, and her exclusion likely came at least in part as a request by the Rodríguez government.

Rather than inaugurating a democratic and economic renaissance, the United States is potentially enabling the survival of a rentist regime. During the oil boom of the first two decades of this century, the Chavista regime insulated itself from domestic accountability by exchanging oil rents for votes and elite support. China served as its primary geopolitical patron, unconcerned with accountability so long as the crude flowed eastward. The United States’ playbook today eerily imitates this model. Venezuela’s main external partner has now changed, but that partner has similar functions. By prioritizing narrow resource extraction over deep institutional reform, the United States has opted to become the senior partner in a rentier compact. Washington is Venezuela’s new toll collector.

Thus far, Venezuela’s democratic transition has delivered only a commitment to reform the Supreme Tribunal of Justice. Nearly a year after Maduro’s ousting, there is no election calendar, no clear plan to reform the electoral system, and no meaningful internal changes to how the government generates and spends its revenues. Despite all the leverage Trump claims to have over the Venezuelan government, outside of business deals, he has very little to show for it.

If this trajectory persists, the United States will not propel a transition to democracy, market principles, and the rule of law in Venezuela. It will instead re-create the very conditions that produced Venezuela’s autocracy in the first place.
