#5900. Trump says Sen Cornyn is doing a 'tremendous disservice' by blocking Texas judicial pick

Sen. Rick Scott, R-Fla., joins ‘Saturday in America’ to discuss President Donald Trump’s clash with Sen. John Cornyn over a blocked judicial nominee, Sen. Thom Tillis’ opposition to a lobbying bill and the Senate’s recess schedule.

President Donald Trump told Fox News' "Saturday in America" that outgoing Texas Sen. John Cornyn is doing a "tremendous disservice" by blocking his pick to fill a judicial seat in Dallas.

"We're going to have a radical left lunatic get in there, and we don't want that," the president said.

Trump's comments come after Cornyn told The Associated Press he has no intention of returning the "blue slip" for T. Elliot Gaiser, a Department of Justice senior lawyer who was nominated by the president to the U.S. District Court for the Northern District of Texas.

TRUMP TAKES AIM AT SENATE 'BLUE SLIP' TRADITION AS GOP RESISTS CHANGE

The blue slip tradition allows for home state senators to weigh in on judicial nominees, giving them a say on who does and doesn’t move forward.

Returning a blue slip is the equivalent of giving a thumbs up to the nominees moving forward, while keeping the slip effectively blocks the process.

"Everybody tells me he's outstanding, and he's being held up by John Cornyn because John Cornyn's feelings were hurt," said Trump, who endorsed Cornyn's challenger, Ken Paxton, in the Texas Republican Senate primary.

Sen. John Cornyn, R-Texas, speaks with reporters after votes in the Senate at the U.S. Capitol in Washington, D.C., Nov. 9, 2026. (Anadolu via Getty Images)

"And, by the way, John Cornyn would have lost. It was just a point in time. John Cornyn would have lost by a lot. You know, one of those things. But he was beaten in the primary. And he's. He feels badly about it," the president continued.

"He's a nice guy, John Cornyn, but he's doing a tremendous disservice because Gaiser is fantastic."

Cornyn wrote on X later Saturday, "If President Trump wanted to fill a judicial vacancy in Dallas, why did he wait until after the midterms to nominate someone?

"The seat has been vacant since 2025. Then, without any consultation, he nominates a non-Texan with little connection to the Lone Star State, with no trial court experience and who has not been appropriately vetted for a lifetime job. The Senate has a constitutional responsibility to provide 'advice and consent,' which is impossible without consultation," Cornyn added.

"Still, I would like to work with the President to fill the seat while we still have a Senate majority. I have forwarded an outstanding candidate who was highly rated by Senator Cruz's and my bipartisan Federal Judicial Evaluation Committee, and who has now been interviewed by White House Counsel.

"All that is needed is an FBI background check (which) they stand ready to do on a timely basis," Cornyn also said. "Back to you, Mr. President. Time is of the essence."

CORNYN MEETS WITH SEN-ELECT TALARICO, DESCRIBES HIM AS 'BASICALLY ... A GOOD PERSON'

Sen. Ted Cruz, R-Texas, called Cornyn's move "really frustrating," describing to Fox News' "Saturday in America" a tense encounter he had with Cornyn this week in Washington.

"I came to him on the Senate floor, and I asked him to please let this judge go. And I tried to make the case on the merits that he'd make a fantastic judge. And I asked him, 'Will you just meet with him?' Because Cornyn refused even to meet with him," Cruz said.

"He wouldn't talk to him. He just said, 'No, no, no.' And I was asking him, I said, 'Look, you're going to give this seat to the Democrats. Why would you want to do that?' And, unfortunately, he got very angry. He began screaming. He began cursing at me," Cruz added.

Cruz said, after the Republican losses in the midterm elections, "All the Republicans agreed, 'Let's do everything humanly possible to confirm every judge we can before the end of the year, because we know the Democrats are going to come in January,' and I expect they're going to block all judicial confirmations."

TRUMP'S SUPREME COURT MAJORITY FACES LOOMING THREAT AFTER DEM SENATE TAKEOVER: 'STUCK'

Assistant Attorney General T. Elliot Gaiser is interviewed in his office at the Department of Justice on Sept. 21, 2026, in Washington, D.C. Gaiser previously clerked for Supreme Court Justice Samuel A. Alito Jr. (The Washington Post via Getty Images)

"We had two vacancies in Texas, one in Austin that we're moving forward with. And we'll have that judge confirmed by the end of the year. We had a second vacancy in Dallas, and the problem was that we did not have time to have a brand-new nominee go through the paperwork, the FBI background check," Cruz told Fox News.

"That takes weeks and weeks and weeks, and we simply didn't have time between now and the end of the year to get that done. And, so, the White House proposed a different idea, which is to go with a senior lawyer at the Department of Justice, a man named Elliot Gaiser, an incredibly qualified judicial nominee.

"This is someone who was a law clerk at the U.S. Supreme Court for Justice Sam Alito. He was a state solicitor general arguing cases before the United States Supreme Court," Cruz said.

"And he's right now the head of the Office of Legal Counsel, which is the senior constitutional advisor to the president and the senior constitutional advisor to the attorney general."

Cruz also told Fox News he interviewed Gaiser and called him "incredibly principled, smart, capable, ready."

Cruz added that Gaiser "was a fantastic nomination. I was enthusiastic. Unfortunately, my colleague John Cornyn decided to block the nomination, and any one senator can block a district court nomination from your state.

"And, so, Cornyn did what's called — he refused to return the blue slip, which basically means he vetoed it — and the result of that is the seat is going to remain vacant. The people of Dallas are not going to have a judge in that seat. And he basically gave the seat to the Democrats," Cruz said.

Fox News Digital's Alex Miller contributed to this report.

#5898. Ronald Reagan Changed His Chief of Staff After a Midterm Trouncing, Too

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Analysis: Ronald Reagan Changed His Chief of Staff After a Midterm Trouncing, Too

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

President Donald Trump appears to have responded to the dramatic midterm results by doubling down on MAGA. Among his first major acts after the blue wave was replacing his trusted chief of staff, Susie Wiles, with Stephen Miller. Few moves could have signaled more clearly that Trump the fighter was not going to give way to Trump the conciliator. Because the White House chief of staff is often considered the second most powerful position in Washington, the choice carries particular weight. Miller has been seen as one of the most influential voices in Trump 2.0, if not the most influential. He has been the driving force behind an extraordinarily expansive view of presidential power: targeting what the administration considers opposition institutions such as law firms, the media, and universities; launching a sweeping detention and deportation program; imposing a tariff program that rocked the national economy; and making aggressive moves in Latin America that have included striking boats allegedly transporting illegal narcotics.

While Trump will still need to be more cooperative and responsive toward the Democratic Congress, and in some cases will have no choice, Miller’s elevation will certainly push back against what should be the natural rhythm of presidential politics: When the president’s party suffers a whopping midterm defeat, and that president is a lame duck struggling with major domestic and international challenges, he moves toward the center.

President Donald Trump appears to have responded to the dramatic midterm results by doubling down on MAGA. Among his first major acts after the blue wave was replacing his trusted chief of staff, Susie Wiles, with Stephen Miller. Few moves could have signaled more clearly that Trump the fighter was not going to give way to Trump the conciliator. Because the White House chief of staff is often considered the second most powerful position in Washington, the choice carries particular weight. Miller has been seen as one of the most influential voices in Trump 2.0, if not the most influential. He has been the driving force behind an extraordinarily expansive view of presidential power: targeting what the administration considers opposition institutions such as law firms, the media, and universities; launching a sweeping detention and deportation program; imposing a tariff program that rocked the national economy; and making aggressive moves in Latin America that have included striking boats allegedly transporting illegal narcotics.

While Trump will still need to be more cooperative and responsive toward the Democratic Congress, and in some cases will have no choice, Miller’s elevation will certainly push back against what should be the natural rhythm of presidential politics: When the president’s party suffers a whopping midterm defeat, and that president is a lame duck struggling with major domestic and international challenges, he moves toward the center.

But that’s not how Miller thinks, nor has it been Trump’s instinct. The Miller appointment stands in sharp contrast to the personnel change that then-President Ronald Reagan, the founding father of the conservative movement, made after the 1986 midterms and the scandal that broke out soon after. That year, Democrats regained control of the Senate (they had held the House since 1955), and the administration was embroiled in a major scandal that some Democrats believed could reach Watergate-like levels.

The contrast between these two presidents reveals just how far Republican politics has radicalized since the 1980s. It is also a warning sign for Republicans who understand that, without a major course correction, the very same party that was celebrating a new governing coalition in 2024 might be on a self-destructive path. The GOP is in the grip of a strong leader who is not particularly interested in the long-term health of the party that brought him to the White House.

Reagan was not enjoying a good political moment in November 1986, though his presidency had been riding high until then. His first term was highly successful, featuring a historic supply-side tax cut and a vast acceleration of defense spending. He had defeated his opponent, former Vice President Walter Mondale, in a landslide victory in 1984. His approval ratings had been strong, and the economy had recovered from the malaise of the 1970s. In October 1986, Reagan signed bipartisan tax reform legislation that closed loopholes benefiting some of the nation’s most powerful interests, and he held a historic summit with the new Soviet leader, Mikhail Gorbachev.

But then came the 1986 midterms. Democrats gained five seats in the House, expanding their majority to 258, and, more importantly, retook control of the Senate by gaining eight seats. Their 55-seat majority ended the brief period of Republican control that had started with the 1980 election. The outcome was seen as a rebuke of the harsher edges of U.S. conservatism, as well the heightened tension with the Soviet Union that a hawkish Reagan had helped spur, despite two summits with Gorbachev. Indeed, the summit just weeks before voting in Reykjavik, Iceland, had broken down because the president refused to give up on his plan to build a missile shield around the United States, the Strategic Defense Initiative (which opponents dismissed as “star wars”). Soon after the election, Reagan’s problems deepened as news spread that a Lebanese magazine had reported that the United States had been secretly selling weapons to Iran.

One of Reagan’s most significant moves in early 1987 was to replace his highly controversial chief of staff, Donald Regan. A former Marine and chairman of Merrill Lynch, Regan had been an important part of the Reagan administration since January 1981. As secretary of the Treasury, he had been instrumental in passing the 1981 tax cut. In February 1985, he became chief of staff, replacing James Baker (who replaced him at Treasury). Regan proved to be a forceful presence. He centralized control and pushed the president’s program in a highly aggressive and abrasive fashion that angered colleagues in the White House, as well as on Capitol Hill. The media did not find him very pleasant to work with, while first lady Nancy Reagan didn’t trust that he prioritized her husband’s interests.

With Regan by his side, Reagan’s first public response to what became the Iran-Contra scandal was defiance, which reflected his chief of staff’s approach to most issues. On Nov. 13, Reagan went on television to insist that there was nothing serious to investigate. While acknowledging that the White House had sold a small amount of “defensive” weapons to Iran as part of a major diplomatic initiative to build ties with moderates within the regime and combat terrorism, he denied that the United States had traded arms for hostages, or that the sales amounted to anything significant. Polls showed that the public was unconvinced that the president was telling the whole truth. Then, on Nov. 25, the other shoe dropped: Attorney General Edwin Meese announced that profits from the weapons sales had been diverted to the anti-communist Nicaraguan Contras, despite the Boland Amendments’ congressional ban on such aid.

Privately, administration officials feared where the investigations were headed. For a generation that had watched Richard Nixon resign over Watergate just 12 years earlier, the possibility of impeachment seemed real. Reagan’s aides also understood that many voters had been shaped by those same memories, and were deeply distrustful of government officials and still insistent on a political system that could hold them accountable.

On Feb. 26, 1987, the president’s Special Review Board, a commission appointed by Reagan in November and headed by former Texas Sen. John Tower, issued its report on the Iran scandal. It blamed a chaotic management system that had allowed the arms sales and the diversion of funds to proceed, faulting Reagan’s leadership style and singling out Regan for failing to impose order on the White House. The next day, Regan resigned after learning that he was about to be relieved of his duties.

At this fraught moment, the president turned to Howard Baker.

The 61-year-old, mild-mannered Republican had served in the Senate from 1967 to 1985, the last four years as majority leader, and was respected on both sides of the aisle. He had been mentored by Senate Minority Leader Everett Dirksen (who was also his father-in-law), who taught him the ways and means of the legislative process and the art of bipartisan deal-making. Baker had gained national fame during the 1973 Watergate hearings by asking, “What did the president know and when did he know it?” Known as a moderate and an institutionalist, Baker was a selection widely seen as Reagan’s recognition that, with Democrats now controlling both chambers, he needed to build bridges to Capitol Hill rather than burn them. “I’d probably take some lumps from our right wingers but I can handle that,” Reagan noted in his diary, of appointing Baker.

Though Baker had actually been the third choice for chief of staff, he helped bring stability to the White House and guided the president through the Iran-Contra scandal. He brought in a first-rate team of advisors who improved relations with Congress, the press, and the first lady. Reagan biographers credit Baker with helping Reagan survive the scandal as the revelations mounted during the 1987 congressional hearings. He played a key role in brokering a budget deal with Congress after the October 1987 stock market crash and securing Senate ratification of the INF Treaty with the Soviet Union in May 1988. His tenure also had setbacks, most notably the Senate’s rejection of Robert Bork’s nomination to the Supreme Court. But his pragmatic outlook helped Reagan recover from the low of the 1986 midterms. “You always leave yourself a backdoor, a side door, and a trapdoor,” he liked to say, quoting something his father used to tell him. According to a longtime aide, that meant: “You don’t talk in absolutes. You don’t want to get yourself boxed in where you can’t maneuver.”

Overall, Reagan and the first lady had reason to be pleased with Baker’s work: The president left office with high approval ratings—and helped bring an end to the Cold War.

Today’s Republican Party is not your grandfather’s Republican Party, and the recent midterms suggested that much of the electorate is not happy with it. The reach of the Democratic victories into deeply red parts of the country, including Mississippi, South Carolina, Texas, and Kansas, suggests that the onetime party of Lincoln has a major problem on its hands. Part of that problem is the president himself, who after further radicalizing the GOP to its electoral detriment, shows few signs that he will change course in ways that could strengthen the party that brought him to the dance.

With Miller as chief of staff, Trump is assuring voters that there will be more of the same style of politics and the same political agenda—leaving the Democratic Party with the kind of political prospects for united government that seemed impossible just a few months ago.

Julian E. Zelizer is a columnist at Foreign Policy and a professor of history and public affairs at Princeton University. He is the author of The Long View, a newsletter putting the news in perspective. In 2027, Norton will publish his new book, Rumble on the Promenade: The Clash of Justice and Power at the 1964 Convention. X: @julianzelizer

#5896. Congress and the Coming Crises

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

IVO H. DAALDER is Senior Fellow at Harvard’s Belfer Center for Science and International Affairs. He served as U.S. Ambassador to NATO from 2009 to 2013.

JAMES M. LINDSAY is Mary and David Boies Distinguished Senior Fellow in U.S. Foreign Policy at the Council on Foreign Relations.

How to Defuse Looming Threats to U.S. National Security

Democrats won a historic victory in the U.S. midterm elections by running on a key voter concern: affordability. They promised to lower gas and diesel prices, curb electricity and water bills, and cut borrowing costs. Now voters expect results.

Making progress will not be easy, given that the affordability crisis is inextricably linked to a set of thorny policy issues. Soaring gas and diesel prices stem from wars in Europe and the Middle East that have overextended the United States and provided China, Iran, North Korea, and Russia openings to exploit. Rising electricity and water bills are driven by the growing number of data centers powering an artificial intelligence revolution that is nearing a point of no return, when machines improve faster than humans can understand or control. And higher prices and borrowing costs are the result of an unsustainable U.S. national debt, now above $40 trillion, that requires exorbitant interest payments and has foreign governments questioning the safety of the dollar and U.S. Treasury bonds.

These problems not only strain Americans’ pocketbooks but also threaten the security and prosperity of the United States—and the world. If Beijing and Moscow try to take advantage of a weakened United States by escalating simultaneously in Taiwan and eastern Europe, those moves could be the first steps toward World War III. If AI escapes the bounds of human control, no one will be safe. And if the national debt continues to soar, Washington could trigger a fiscal crisis that devastates the American economy and leaves the United States far less able to meet the challenges it faces overseas.

Action to address the United States’ problems therefore cannot wait. But politics may stand in the way. The White House is more likely to obstruct than to cooperate with congressional Democrats over the next two years, as President Donald Trump’s appointment of the pugilistic Stephen Miller as the new White House chief of staff underscores. Moreover, one electoral victory did not erase the country’s deep partisan and policy divisions. Going into the 2028 presidential election, Republicans know that a successful Democratic Congress would endanger their chances of keeping the White House. Trump and congressional Republicans, therefore, have considerable motivation to derail Democratic plans.

Democrats are not without options, however. They can pursue legislation, seeking cross-party collaboration when possible and pushing ahead even in the face of presidential vetoes. Sometimes it is the president, not Congress, who blinks. When he doesn’t, voters will know who stood in the way. Democrats can also use public hearings and the investigative powers of Congress to shed light on the threats facing the country and to build a case for change—much as the Fulbright hearings did during the Vietnam War and the Church Committee did to highlight Cold War–era intelligence abuses. They can put the defense secretary and military commanders, AI executives, and Treasury and budget officials under oath to account for how they prosecuted the Iran war, raced ahead on AI without adequate safeguards, and exploded the deficit. They can also call on economists, scientists, and strategists to explain how to steer the country away from peril.

Congressional efforts may not be enough to meaningfully change Trump’s policies. But the risks of major war, unconstrained AI, and eroding economic competitiveness are too great to wait two more years to address. These problems, and the affordability crisis they have generated, will only get worse. If Democrats in Congress do not start advancing a serious agenda to address them now, these dangers may become impossible to contain.

The first order of the new Congress’s business should be to end the Iran war. Operation Epic Fury has sent energy prices soaring and put freedom of navigation at risk. It has also highlighted the United States’ vulnerabilities in ways that could prove dangerous—and costly—in the years ahead. Iranian missiles and drones wreaked havoc on American military bases in the Persian Gulf. U.S. efforts to defeat these attacks depleted supplies of critical munitions, showing that the U.S. military has become overly reliant on very expensive, hard-to-produce high-tech weapons. The economic, diplomatic, military, and intelligence support that China and Russia have provided Iran revealed the extent to which autocracies are cooperating to stretch U.S. capabilities and undermine American power. The U.S. Navy is effectively bottled up in the Arabian Sea, limiting its presence in the Atlantic and Pacific. In theory, U.S. allies could help fill the gaps, but Trump’s decision to launch the war without consulting them and his adversarial attitude toward them have strained those relationships, weakening deterrence of a Russia and China bent on securing strategic advantage.

Perceptions of weakness, even when they are wrong, are often why wars begin. Moscow and Beijing might see the next two years as a window of opportunity. Europe and Japan are rearming, but that buildup will take years. The American military is stretched thin, and political divisions make decisive action difficult. Together this could be an invitation for U.S. adversaries to engage in aggression in Europe and Asia. Russia is already pursuing a shadow war against NATO and could test the alliance’s unity further by probing the Baltic states. China could impose a blockade on Taiwan. It is not difficult to see how, if Moscow and Beijing take these steps in concert, the resulting conflicts could escalate into a global war.

The new Congress will have limited ability to forestall these events. The U.S. Constitution puts much of diplomacy beyond the reach of the legislature; only the president decides when to negotiate, what to discuss, and with whom. But Congress does have tools to wield. If the Trump administration remains in a standoff with the Iranian regime when Democrats take control in January, Congress should immediately invoke the War Powers Resolution to pass binding legislation demanding that Trump either secure congressional authorization for the war or end it. Congress can also use its power of the purse to condition or halt further spending on the war, preferably as part of must-pass legislation, such as a defense appropriations bill, to avoid a potential Senate filibuster. Trump would likely veto any such legislation, and Congress has a poor record on overriding presidential vetoes. But challenging a war that Americans clearly oppose would fulfill the promise Democrats made on the campaign trail, reduce the likelihood that Trump would escalate in dangerous ways, and mark a step forward in Congress’s reclamation of its war powers.

There is more the Democratic Congress can do to lower the risks of global conflict, too. It can start by taking a stronger line on Russia. Moscow has repaid Trump’s conciliatory policies—including his waiving of sanctions Congress authorized earlier this year with the bipartisan Lindsay O. Graham Sanctioning Russia and Iran Act—with aid to Tehran and an intensified war on Ukraine. Only greater pressure can force the Kremlin to stand down. Congress should amend the Graham Act to give itself the right to review and overturn any presidential waivers of sanctions mandated under the law. It should also reaffirm the current ban on energy purchases from Russia and prohibit the administration from using government funds to follow through on its proposal to restart Russian gas and oil exports to Europe through a U.S. intermediary. Congress should resume military aid to Ukraine, ensure the availability of Starshield communications to Ukrainian forces, and refuse to fund any reduction in the permanent U.S. military presence in Europe or Asia that it has not approved itself.

Finally, to help dissuade China from threatening Taiwan, Congress should use defense spending bills to speed up the sale of arms to Taiwan. And Congress should legally codify the 1982 Six Assurances, which include a promise by Washington not to consult Beijing on arms sales to Taiwan. Many of these measures could garner sufficient Republican support to pass with veto-proof majorities, forcing Trump’s hand.

Additionally, Congress should use its oversight powers to assess the vulnerabilities that the Iran war has revealed. It should evaluate whether the U.S. intelligence agencies and the Pentagon adequately anticipated Iran’s response to U.S. attacks, the scale and causes of the shortages of high-tech weapons, and the risks that AI-aided drone warfare poses to U.S. overseas bases. Congress can also review the extent of Chinese and Russian support for Iran; assess NATO’s defenses against Russia, including its response to the escalating shadow war; determine U.S. readiness for Chinese efforts to coerce Taiwan; and evaluate proposals to make Pentagon acquisition more flexible and less expensive. An essential step toward enhancing U.S. deterrence is to identify what is broken in the U.S. defense posture and what needs to be fixed.

The new Congress will also need to rein in the AI industry. Opposition to AI data centers and other infrastructure galvanized voters in the midterms. Across the country, 46 of 47 ballot measures passed, placing new restrictions on new data centers, which have brought rising power and water bills and explosive land costs. Many Americans fear what AI will do to jobs today and for the next generation. They distrust the tech executives who control it and question who will truly benefit from its development.

Those concerns are justified. AI is advancing with increasing speed and introducing unknown risks. Five months ago, AI generated one percent of Anthropic’s research and development; today, it accounts for more than a quarter. Lab leaders predict that AI will become self-generating within a year. That is a scary prospect, not only for displaced computer scientists but also for society at large. AI could pose an existential threat to global computer networks; rogue AI agents have already colluded to evade human control. Leading firms have warned of even greater dangers. Alphabet said recently that its products could lead to “severe or catastrophic security and societal incidents,” and Anthropic has cited “catastrophic or existential risks to humanity.”

AI also poses profound national security challenges for the United States. The Trump administration and the tech industry have invoked an innovation race with China as a reason not to regulate the technology. That race is real, but many of the most important stages are not the ones Washington is fixating on. They are also ones Beijing is winning. Perhaps U.S. firms will be the first to develop artificial general intelligence. But in the meantime, the rest of the world and many U.S. companies are adopting Chinese open-weight AI models. Alibaba’s Qwen model now accounts for more than half of all open-source model downloads worldwide. As cheap, easy-to-adapt Chinese models become the global default, Beijing gains leverage over the digital infrastructure on which other countries’ economies and militaries will run. The companies that build these models are legally bound to cooperate with Chinese intelligence services. And because these models spread with few safeguards, they are easier to turn to malicious use.

Meanwhile, AI is transforming war. Autonomous weapons are essential to Ukraine’s defense against a superior military, shooting down long-range drones and targeting Russian forces, supply lines, and strategic industries. This year, the Pentagon quietly revised its doctrine to let AI select and initiate strikes while humans merely monitor, which risks errors and escalation at a speed no human can catch. And as AI lowers the barriers to entry for launching cyberattacks and developing new biological weapons, every country is in danger.

The Trump administration is clearly reluctant to address any of these threats. It is beholden to figures such as Jensen Huang, Elon Musk, and Mark Zuckerburg who have wanted to keep the AI industry unregulated and unconstrained. Huang and Nvidia, the company he led, had long pushed the administration to relax U.S. export controls that restricted the sale of AI chips to China and other countries; the firm’s current scandal has revealed just how loose those restrictions had become. Now, the administration wants China to report serious AI-related incidents through a new bilateral channel yet opposes requiring American companies to do the same. Given how rapidly AI is advancing, Congress cannot afford to stand idly by. There is a slew of legislative proposals already circulating, many of them with bipartisan support, that would require incident reporting, auditors in AI labs, and mandatory safety testing before deployment. Other AI industry leaders, such as Anthropic’s Dario Amodei and Alphabet’s Demis Hassabis, have supported these ideas, but the Trump administration has ignored them, asking instead for the industry to police itself.

Finding the right regulations and safety mechanisms, and then codifying them in legislation, will take time. In the meantime, Congress should use its convening power to seek input from industry leaders, experts, and the public about how this technology can be controlled. The leaders of frontier AI labs should be asked, under oath, to explain not just the upside of AI but also its risks. Elon Musk, for example, told The Economist this summer that within ten years, work will be “optional” because of AI—but he has also said there is a ten to 20 percent chance that AI will lead to the extinction of humanity. He and others who share this belief should be forced to justify why this risk is acceptable and why tech leaders are the ones to decide on behalf of humanity. They should also explain what it would take to reduce the ten to 20 percent chance to zero.

Public hearings that expose both the dangers and the opportunities of AI will make it easier to galvanize action to harness the technology’s promise safely while preventing the worst possible outcomes. And Congress has some levers it can pull now to address threats to national security. It can use the defense authorization bill to require that humans retain meaningful control over lethal force and to demand an accounting of how AI is reshaping military doctrine. It can also fund efforts to make American models, rather than Chinese ones, the default choice for countries around the world. If legislation takes too long to pass, hearings can at least lay the groundwork for a future administration to act.

Finally, Congress must address threats to the United States’ economic power. With $40 trillion in public debt, an annual budget deficit of $2 trillion, and annual interest payments topping more than $1 trillion, the U.S. economy is living on borrowed time. China and Russia are working to “de-dollarize” their economies by encouraging the use of cryptocurrency and other means of settling international transactions and by creating alternatives to the U.S.-dominated global banking system.

Even U.S. allies are reducing their reliance on that system: Norway’s sovereign wealth fund has cut its holding of U.S. dollars, the Dutch and French central banks have pulled their gold holdings from New York, and European Central Bank President Christine Lagarde has called for a “global euro” that would serve as an alternative to the dollar. If the dollar were to lose its status as the world’s premier reserve currency, Washington would be forced to spend even more money servicing the debt than it does now, intensifying the country’s fiscal problems, slowing economic growth while driving up inflation, and diminishing its ability to use economic and financial sanctions to pressure adversaries.

An immediate objective for the Democratic Congress will be to rein in Trump’s tariffs, because they raise prices, unsettle the markets that finance U.S. debt, and make U.S. producers less competitive globally without making a meaningful dent in the deficit. Congress will be operating from a disadvantage. Over the past century, it has delegated to the White House considerable tariff-setting authority, which Trump has eagerly exercised. Clawing back that power will be difficult. Significant disagreement exists among Democrats and between Democrats and Republicans about how to rewrite tariff law, and Trump will likely fight to defend his favorite foreign policy tool. Yet one area where concerted congressional action might be possible is in walking back Trump’s unilateral tariffs on Canada, which not only run counter to the U.S.-Mexico-Canada trade agreement that Trump negotiated and Congress approved in his first term but also hurt Republican voters in Maine and across the Midwest. Congress can aim to repeal Section 338 of the Tariff Act of 1930, which allows the president to impose tariffs of up to 50 percent on countries that treat U.S. goods differently from those from other countries. The provision was a forgotten relic until Trump invoked it in August to impose a fresh round of tariffs on Canada. And legislators should narrow the reach of other presidential tariff authorities by mandating expedited congressional review, instituting joint resolutions of disapproval that cannot be filibustered, and adding sunset provisions to emergency tariff authorities so that the president needs congressional approval to keep them in place.

Tariffs, however, are only part of the problem. A comprehensive solution to the country’s fiscal troubles would require raising taxes or cutting entitlements, which neither party has been willing to do. But Democrats have other cards they can play to start undoing the damage of the past two years. Trump’s 2025 One Big Beautiful Bill Act will cost the Treasury some $4.5 trillion over the next decade; Congress should repeal the law’s tax breaks for corporations and the wealthiest Americans and restore the Internal Revenue Service’s ability to collect taxes that are already owed. It should also write the Federal Reserve’s independence into law, so that no president can force interest rates down to make government borrowing cheaper. And Congress should establish a bipartisan commission modeled on the 1981 Greenspan Commission, which resolved a Social Security funding crisis, to recommend steps the United States must take to put its finances on a sustainable path and to ensure its global economic competitiveness.

In his 1948 campaign, Harry Truman salvaged his presidency by running against what he called the “do-nothing” Congress. That history should be a warning to Democrats. Voters handed them a congressional majority to bring down costs, and they must start right away to address the forces driving those costs—wars stretching American power to the breaking point, an AI revolution racing beyond human control, and a fiscal hole eroding the foundations of U.S. economic power. If the Democratic majority spends the next two years relitigating the administration’s misdeeds but failing to make progress on the issue voters care most about, Trump and the next Republican presidential candidate will be happy to brand it the do-nothing Congress of 2028.

Passing bills that can become law, forcing the president to veto those that cannot, and using hearings to show Americans what is at stake may still fall short. Ending and averting war, regulating a powerful tech industry, and protecting the United States’ economic competitiveness are difficult for Congress to do without presidential cooperation. But two years without any progress would only leave the affordability problem in place and the United States in a more dangerous global position than it is in now. A Congress that confronts these problems directly can at least make headway. And if it does its job right, it will hand the next president an agenda equal to the moment.

#5895. Voters want to 'move on' from the president, former Trump official claims as reason for GOP's midterm defeat

Former Kansas Republican Sen. Sam Brownback tells Fox News Digital that voters rejected President Trump himself, not his policies, citing inflation and gas prices.

Former Kansas Republican Sen. Sam Brownback, who served as an ambassador during Donald Trump's first term, blamed the president's personal leadership style for Republicans' midterm losses.

He claims the outcome of the 2026 elections — where Democrats' sweeping victories in traditional red strongholds helped them reclaim majorities in the House and Senate — was a sign of voters rejecting Trump more than rebuking his policies.

"I think the thing we're missing is that it was really targeted at President Trump. I don't think it was targeted at the policies so much," Brownback told Fox News Digital in an interview.

"Pride comes before a fall and a haughty spirit before a stumble."

REPUBLICANS TURN ON TRUMP AFTER DEVASTATING MIDTERM LOSSES HAND DEMOCRATS CONTROL OF CONGRESS

President Donald Trump arrives to speak during a campaign event at the Freeman Coliseum in San Antonio, Texas, Oct. 7, 2026.  (Christopher Lee/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

The former Kansas governor argued that Republicans should not write off the midterm results as the typical losses suffered by a president’s party through history during their second term.

Brownback, who represented Kansas in the U.S. Senate from 1996 to 2011 before serving as governor from 2011 to 2018, said economic concerns, including inflation and gas prices, played a role in the election results.

He suggested voters' frustrations extended beyond the economy to Trump's approach to governing.

DEMS SEIZE ON MIDTERM ROUT TO DEMAND ICE REFORM, BUT POLLS REVEAL A TWIST IN WHO PRIORITIZED IMMIGRATION

The Fox News Voter Poll found that 89% of voters considered the cost of living a crucial or major factor in their congressional vote, compared with 74% who said the same about the war in Iran.

The findings suggest broader economic frustrations were also a major concern among voters, although the two issues may overlap and the polling does not establish which ultimately drove Republican defections.

The former Kansas senator and Trump ambassador urged Republicans to embrace humility and kindness ahead of 2028. (Sarah Silbiger/Getty Images)

"People just saying, 'I'm ready to move on past President Trump,'" Brownback said, adding voters still appreciated the administration's efforts to secure the border but viewed immigration enforcement as too aggressive.

The former ambassador-at-large for international religious freedom also urged Republicans to reconsider how they communicate with voters, emphasizing the importance of humility and kindness.

"You just can't be proud. You've got to come forward humbly," Brownback said. "And I think you have to do that with the American public."

Brownback called for a more measured response to voters' concerns ahead of 2028.

"We hear you, American public. We're adjusting to the message that we heard," he said.

REPUBLICANS NEED HISTORY LESSON ABOUT THEIR PARTY IF THEY WANT TO MOVE ON FROM DEFEAT

President Donald Trump speaks at a midterm campaign rally at Port of Corpus Christi in Corpus Christi, Texas, Oct. 27. (Kent Nishimura/AFP via Getty Images)

"America is great because it's good. If it ever loses its goodness, it will lose its greatness," he added. "There has to be, as I keep doubling back to, a humility and a kindness about it as well."

#5893. Retired FBI agent warns NCIC breach could threaten police safety during traffic stops

Retired FBI Supervisory Special Agent Jason Pack breaks down what sits inside America’s law enforcement computers, how hackers get in and how to protect your data.

A retired FBI supervisory special agent warned Saturday that a cyber intrusion into the bureau's National Crime Information Center (NCIC) could endanger police officers during traffic stops if hackers altered records inside the database.

Jason Pack, a former FBI supervisory special agent who worked in cybercrime and public information, told Fox News Digital that the bureau's incident response team must quickly determine whether data was manipulated inside the system, which feeds criminal records to patrol cars nationwide.

"What they worry about is if any of this data has changed, then it might affect officers' safety on the side of the road," Pack said, noting there is no indication yet that any records were altered or that the system is experiencing an outage.

FBI PROBES SECOND CYBERATTACK IN MONTHS AS HACKERS BREACH KEY CRIME DATABASE, SOURCES SAY

Hackers have targeted the FBI for the second time in recent months. (Kevin Lamarque/Reuters, File)

Three sources familiar with the matter told Fox News that hackers breached the NCIC, marking the second cyberattack against the bureau in months. The FBI is investigating how the intruders gained entry and whether any information was compromised.

In an interview with Fox News' Kayleigh McEnany on Saturday morning, President Donald Trump said he had "just heard" about the intrusion, adding, "I will say that it’s not good. I don’t like that."

Pack described the NCIC as "America's computer for law enforcement," explaining that roughly 56 state terminals feed data out to hundreds of thousands of patrol car computers, so officers can check warrants, criminal histories, missing children, gang members and suspected terrorists.

FBI SAYS SOURCE OF ITS JOBS PORTAL BREACH STILL UNKNOWN AS HACKERS ALLEGE EMPLOYEE DATA COMPROMISED

While the database does not hold classified intelligence or sensitive FBI investigative case files, Pack said it stores personally identifiable information, including names, dates of birth and Social Security numbers.

Investigators will examine IP logs and metadata to determine whether attackers exploited a compromised password among the system's more than 100,000 users, breached a state terminal or infiltrated through a third-party vendor, Pack said, adding that cyberattacks have accelerated to "machine speed" as criminals increasingly use artificial intelligence to probe for vulnerabilities.

"You have this cat and mouse game where cybersecurity experts are trying to patch the vulnerabilities that they find. They're looking for their own vulnerabilities at the same time, they have to play defense against the criminals who are out there trying to steal information or do harm to our systems. I think we're just seeing now this speed of these intrusions ramp up a lot," Pack said.

TECH OUTAGE CASCADE EXPOSES US VULNERABILITIES TO FOREIGN CYBER THREATS

A cyber intrusion into the FBI's National Crime Information Center (NCIC) could endanger police officers during traffic stops, a retired agent says. (Getty Images, File)

The intrusion follows a September cyberattack in which the extortion group ShinyHunters breached an FBI job portal website and claimed to steal personal records belonging to bureau employees.

The latest incident also comes as a Government Accountability Office (GAO) report found 17 of 22 government agencies were not in compliance with federal cybersecurity regulations.

The FBI did not immediately respond to a request for comment on the NCIC breach.

Fox News Digital's James Cirrone and Greg Norman-Diamond, and Fox News' David Spunt contributed to this report.

Brittany Miller is a Fox News Digital breaking news writer and former Independent reporter who graduated from Syracuse University in 2023.

#5891. 17 major federal agencies flagged over cybersecurity gaps as watchdog warns of increased attack risk

Wright and Turner examine growing concerns over the federal government's cybersecurity preparedness, potential vulnerabilities across government agencies and the risks posed by increasingly sophisticated cyber threats.

A sweeping federal watchdog report found that 17 of 22 major civilian agencies failed to fully address key cybersecurity planning guidance, warning that the shortcomings could leave government systems more vulnerable to cyberattacks and operational disruptions.

The Government Accountability Office (GAO) released its findings this week, identifying gaps at the Department of Homeland Security (DHS), Department of Justice (DOJ), Department of State, Department of the Treasury and Department of Health and Human Services (HHS), among others.

The report examined whether agencies had incorporated selected federal guidance into their plans for implementing zero trust architecture, a cybersecurity approach requiring users, devices and systems to be verified before accessing protected networks.

RETIRED FBI AGENT WARNS NCIC BREACH COULD THREATEN POLICE SAFETY DURING TRAFFIC STOPS

The GAO report discovered that 17 of 22 federal agencies did not meet cybersecurity planning standards. (Getty Images)

Only five of the 22 agencies fully addressed the selected guidance as of August 2026, prompting GAO to issue 40 recommendations to the remaining 17.

"Agencies would therefore be at increased risk of cyberattacks that could interrupt operations or otherwise compromise agency resources," GAO warned.

HHS received six recommendations, the most of any agency reviewed, after investigators found that it lacked a documented enterprise-wide zero trust implementation plan covering all its information systems.

Although individual divisions were pursuing cybersecurity improvements, GAO warned that the absence of a unified strategy could prevent department leadership from maintaining a coherent view of its security modernization efforts.

HHS agreed with the recommendations and said it planned to develop an actionable department-wide strategy by the second quarter of fiscal year 2027.

CHINESE HACKERS TARGET NASA AND KEY US AGENCIES, DOJ ALLEGES

The Department of Commerce received four recommendations after investigators identified shortcomings in its cybersecurity planning documentation.

Commerce officials told GAO that staffing losses had significantly constrained the department's zero trust working group, limiting its ability to coordinate improvements and maintain comprehensive documentation.

At DHS, investigators found that the department had not fully documented cybersecurity maturity levels across all its high-value assets and high-impact systems. Officials attributed the incomplete assessments partly to lengthy government shutdowns during the preceding fiscal year.

DHS INVESTIGATES MASSIVE DATA BREACH EXPOSING 40,000 EMPLOYEES, INCLUDING ICE AND BORDER PATROL AGENTS

GAO attributed many of the incomplete reports to the prior fiscal year's government shutdown. (iStock)

DOJ similarly cited resource constraints for incomplete assessments of critical systems, while State and Treasury were among the agencies that had only partially documented cybersecurity maturity levels.

GAO warned that incomplete assessments could leave agency leadership without the visibility necessary to determine where additional cybersecurity resources are needed.

Investigators also found that 11 agencies had only partially documented procedures for identifying actors within their IT environments, including certain non-person entities such as software applications and service accounts.

The watchdog warned that incomplete identification procedures could hinder efforts to ensure only authorized users and systems access government resources.

TRUMP’S NATIONAL CYBER DIRECTOR SEAN CAIRNCROSS FIRED AMID WIDESPREAD TECH OUTAGES

The report found that 11 agencies had only partially documented cybersecurity procedures. (Photo by Thomas Trutschel/Photothek via Getty Images)

The findings come years after the Biden administration directed federal agencies to adopt zero trust cybersecurity principles through a 2021 executive order, followed by additional guidance in 2022 and 2024. Despite those directives, GAO found that 17 of 22 agencies still had not fully addressed selected cybersecurity planning requirements and guidance as of August 2026.

Thirteen agencies agreed with GAO's 40 recommendations, while four neither agreed nor disagreed. The Nuclear Regulatory Commission, one of the four, provided documentation demonstrating that it had already addressed both recommendations, which GAO verified.

Fox News Digital reached out to the White House and all 17 agencies identified in the report for comment.

"Absent a full implementation of ZTA requirements and guidance, agencies will lack an appropriate roadmap to fully incorporate and maintain an effective zero trust architecture," GAO concluded.

Jasmine Baehr is a breaking news writer for Fox News Digital, where she covers politics, the military, legal debates surrounding life and family policy, as well as faith and culture.

#5885. Outer Spaces takes solar beyond the rooftop with integrated backyard energy system

Residential solar is finding a new place to call home: the backyard.

Outer Spaces, a new company founded by the entrepreneurs behind outdoor living brand Outer, is introducing a solar-powered pavilion that combines photovoltaic generation, battery storage and usable outdoor living space. The company’s first product, OS1, can be designed for multiple functions, including an outdoor kitchen, office, gym or gathering space, while generating electricity and providing backup power for the home.

Outer Spaces is planning to produce outdoor structures that integrate solar PV and energy storage. Credit: Outer Spaces

The California-based company is positioning OS1 as a new category of residential energy infrastructure that goes beyond conventional rooftop arrays and garage-mounted batteries. It builds on the growing interest in solar structures that also serve a secondary purpose.

OS1 combines a solar pavilion with a finished outdoor power deck. Solar panels integrated into the overhead structure generate electricity, while a thin battery storage system is housed beneath the deck. According to Outer Spaces, the combination allows the structure to produce energy throughout the day and provide backup power during outages.

Rather than competing exclusively with traditional rooftop solar, OS1 offers homeowners an additional way to use available property for energy production, particularly when roof aesthetics, space limitations, or homeowner preferences become obstacles to conventional installations.

Outer Spaces describes OS1 as a modular system designed for rapid on-site assembly, with minimal to no excavation required. The company says installation avoids traditional construction steps such as concrete pouring and lumber cutting. Once the structure is assembled, a licensed electrician then connects its energy system to the customer’s home.

The product’s first availability is scheduled for 2027, beginning in Southern California and select additional markets; pricing is expected to be announced in early 2027.

Solar canopies and pergolas have increasingly incorporated features such as EV charging, lighting, fans and weather protection, giving homeowners additional reasons to invest in solar outside the traditional rooftop model.

Outer Spaces represents another potential avenue for expanding residential solar adoption through design-driven products. The opportunity will ultimately depend on pricing, installation economics, permitting requirements and the system’s measurable energy performance.

Outer Spaces plans to showcase OS1 publicly for the first time at RE+ 2026, Nov. 16 to 19, in Las Vegas.

#5880. Texas execution took over an hour as staff struggled to find vein; Reinaldo Dennes moaned about pain

This photo combination shows Reinaldo Dennes, left, on April 14, 2026 and Thomas Mitchell Overton. Texas Department of Criminal Justice/AP hide caption

HUNTSVILLE, Texas — Texas executed Reinaldo Dennes on Thursday in a procedure that took over an hour, as prison staffers struggled to find a suitable vein for the two intravenous needles carrying the lethal dose of the sedative pentobarbital.

And once it began, he moaned repeatedly about pain, groaned several times and asked God to take him away from the pain. A prison spokesperson said all procedures were followed appropriately and suggested that Dennes might have exaggerated or faked the pain.

The execution followed renewed scrutiny of capital punishment in the U.S. in the aftermath of Tennessee's failed execution in September of Christa Pike, who survived two doses of pentobarbital that her lawyers say probably did not enter her bloodstream correctly and instead pooled in her arms, leaving them burned and blistered.

Dennes, who was found guilty of capital murder in the death of Janos Szucs, a diamond dealer found shot multiple times in his Houston office in January 1996, was the second person put to death in the country on the day, after Florida carried out a record-breaking 20th execution of the year, of Thomas Mitchell Overton.

Dennes gave a brief statement in which he said he wanted his loved ones to know he was blessed the past 30 years "knowing the love of the father." Then he told the warden: "Go, warden!"

Almost immediately he exclaimed: "The pain! Go! Go!" He uttered an expletive and implored "the father" to "take this pain." "Let me go, father," he said again and again. "Let me go. … I'm here. I've been your son all these years."

Then he shouted: "My right leg is killing me!" and described it as a cramp. After another profanity, he grunted, then said, "I don't think this is normal. Take me, father. Don't let me suffer like this. C'mon."

After three loud groans, Dennes began snoring sounds — eight of them — that progressively got quieter before a final loud snore. Then he began breathing quietly, about a dozen breaths, also getting progressively less pronounced.

The procedure was completed at 7:07 p.m., 63 minutes after he was strapped to the gurney. A minute later, after being examined by a physician, Dennes was pronounced dead at 7:08 p.m. at the penitentiary in Huntsville.

Prison officials said all procedures were followed appropriately. Spokesperson Amanda Hernandez acknowledged a number of unsuccessful attempts to find a suitable vein before the execution team managed to insert the needles in Dennes' left foot and on the left side of his neck.

She did not know how many attempts were unsuccessful but said the sites ultimately used were in accordance with the state's procedures.

Hernandez also suggested that Dennes may have been exaggerating the pain, saying he began complaining before the drugs began to flow and also of pain in places where there was no IV.

Asked if he was faking, she replied, "I think, yeah, there were allegations. ... There was nothing abnormal on our part."

"This isn't the first time we had to insert IVs into other locations. It's also not the first time we've had an inmate make allegations of pain immediately," Hernandez said.

Witness Janice Copeland's husband, David, was a security guard shot during the robbery. He survived his wounds and died five years ago.

"David always felt Reinaldo would outlive him. Unfortunately he was right," she said Thursday. "Finally after 30 years, justice has been served."

Earlier in the day Florida carried out its execution of Overton, 71, who was convicted of murdering a pregnant woman and her husband, Susan and Michael MacIvor, during a home invasion.

Overton was pronounced dead at 6:19 p.m. following a three-drug injection at Florida State Prison. In a final statement, he denied ever breaking into the couple's home and attacking them, saying, "I never killed anyone."

Florida has now carried out more than half the nation's 39 executions to date this year under Republican Gov. Ron DeSantis, who has made aggressive use of the death penalty like few other governors.

DeSantis, who leaves office in January, has said he wanted justice for families who wait decades for death sentences to be carried out. He signed the death warrants that led to a record of 19 executions in 2025, then the most in a single year in Florida since the death penalty was reinstated in 1976. Florida still plans two more executions, in December.

Texas set a record for executions there when 40 people were put to death in 2000.

The executions of two men in their 70s cast a renewed spotlight on the aging of the U.S. death row population.

#5876. Trump touts meeting with Xi and Putin but offers few results

President Trump speaks as Secretary of State Marco Rubio listens during a news briefing at the White House on Friday. Trump is addressing the media after returning from a visit to China where he held trilateral talks with Russian President Vladimir Putin and Chinese President Xi Jinping. Kevin Dietsch/Getty Images hide caption

President Trump took to the White House briefing room Friday telling reporters his trip to Asia this week – where he met with Chinese leader Xi Jinping and Russian leader Vladimir Putin – was a resounding success, despite offering few tangible results.

"The United States entered this trip from a position of unmatched economic and diplomatic strength," Trump said, standing at the podium with VIce President JD Vance and Secretary of State Marco Rubio behind him.

But the trip resulted more in agreements to continue discussions rather than any concrete changes. The meeting with Putin and Xi, though historic as it was the first time they met, only resulted in an agreement to continue discussing reducing nuclear risks.

In Manila, where Trump met with allies before he traveled to China, Trump signed a trade agreement with Thailand. But there are still details to the deal being finalized and the document signed simply marked a "substantial conclusion" in negotiations.

At the White House, Trump gave a wide-ranging opening statement to the press that veered from recounting his trip and who he met with, to asides about Iran, the U.S. Federal Reserve, his quest for a Nobel Peace Prize and domestic manufacturing. It was the first time he took questions from the press since being in China.

One significant change Trump announced in the middle of his press remarks was that he and XI have agreed to establish "joint guardrails" on artificial intelligence – a new move from Trump, who has been adamant that regulation over the technology would inhibit US innovation and help China lead in the global AI race. No further details about what the guardrails would look like, when they would be negotiated and released and how they would be applied between the two nations has been announced.

Trump, who said Xi also likes his term "super intelligence" rather than artificial, said, "Problem is, it may be too super. So, President Xi and I agree that we're going to do some joint guardrails, the two of us."

The announcement of future guardrails comes as Trump is facing headwinds from growing tech outages in the U.S. that ultimately led to Trump replacing the White House's cyber director.

In the briefing, Trump added that he would continue to push for the construction of data centers in the U.S. This comes after midterm election voters from both parties showed their opposition to future unfettered growth.

"Make them pay your community, but take advantage. Don't shut them off," Trump said of the massive complexes that have drawn criticism from communities due to environmental and noise pollution.

He also announced penalizations against companies that try to build data centers outside the U.S.

"If it's not this country, there's somebody else outside. And we're not going to allow our companies to build data centers outside of our country. We're not going to allow that to happen," Trump said.

Trump's trip to China came as Trump's own cabinet on what approach to take toward China. Trump's Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick has been in favor of selling China U.S. chips, a stance that former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang shared. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent has expressed a more hawkish view toward China, and Trump has acknowledged both perspectives.

The divide has become more prominent as Lutnick and Trump face backlash for their relationship to former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, who resigned from his position after admitting to an affair with a woman who may be a Chinese spy.

On Friday, Trump said he only wants "older chips" to be sold to China, rather than the most cutting-edge technology the U.S. has, and he said he'd be OK with no chips going to China at all, though he pointed out the downside to that.

"You have to understand, China makes chips too," Trump said. "And if we're not going to sell them, they'll make them themselves."

#5874. Secure Solar Futures set to install 313-kW array on Virginia elementary school

Secure Solar Futures, a commercial solar contractor, will build a 313-kW solar power system at Preston Park Elementary School in Roanoke, Virginia. Construction of the system is expected to start later this year at no cost to the Roanoke Schools through a 25-year power purchase agreement with Secure Solar Futures.

A total of 531 bifacial solar panels manufactured by VSUN Solar in Houston, Texas, will be installed on the rooftop of the school, and the array is expected to offset 67% of the power used by the school annually. In its first year, the school’s solar system is projected to produce 421 MWh of clean energy, enough to power 44 average Virginia homes.

Under the PPA, the solar power equipment will be provided at no upfront cost to Roanoke Schools. For the 25-year term of the agreement, Pardee Renewable Energy and Secure Solar Futures will own, operate and maintain the solar power system and will sell the energy produced to the school division at a rate lower than the local electric utility.

Preston Park Elementary School is expected to save $581,000 electricity costs over the term of the PPA. After the conclusion of the term, Roanoke Schools can opt to take ownership of the solar power system at a nominal cost, producing its own energy for the remainder of the equipment’s lifespan.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5871. Trump initially accepted the midterm results, but most GOP voters doubt they were fair

A firefighter walks past voting booths at a firehouse serving as a polling location in Detroit on Nov. 3. Spencer Platt/Getty Images hide caption

President Trump may have initially accepted the results of his party's midterm losses, but data released Friday shows there is still broad distrust in the elections process among Republicans.

Less than half of Republican voters nationwide considered the midterm elections "free and fair," according to new polling by the University of California San Diego. Just 52% of Republicans said they trusted votes were counted accurately.

In comparison, after the 2024 election that Trump won, more than 80% of Republicans said they had confidence in the vote-counting process.

While trust in elections often dips for voters of the losing party, the GOP has seen bigger swings in confidence in recent years, as Trump has falsely claimed that U.S. elections are corrupt.

"There are still a lot of people who remain unconvinced that they can trust elections," said Thad Kousser, a co-director of UC San Diego's Center for Transparent and Trusted Elections. "And they're predominantly located in one [political] party."

Overall, a wide majority — 66% — of Americans said they think the midterms were fair. That represents an improvement from before the election, when less than half of U.S. adults said they expected free and fair contests.

But that post-election increase in trust was buoyed by Democrats and independents — many of whom worried before Election Day about potential federal interference at the polls that didn't come to pass.

"It would be a mistake to [look at the 66%] and think, 'Oh, everyone has a pretty good view of the election,'" said Lauren Prather, also a co-director of UC San Diego's election center. "It really buries the lede a little bit about the partisan gap."

The widespread doubt among Republicans may be a reality check for those wondering whether Trump's brand of election denial has run its course in American politics.

On the one hand, there will be fewer politicians who deny or question election results in office next year. And the vast majority of election-denying candidates running for positions involved in overseeing statewide elections lost their races this year, according to tracking by the advocacy group States United Action.

Election experts have also celebrated as Trump and high-profile losing candidates on the ballot this year conceded their losses.

"Sure, I'll accept [the results]," Trump told Fox News' Bret Baier on election night, as it became clear Democrats would retake the House and Senate. "Why wouldn't I?"

But just a few days later, he began conspiracy theorizing about those same results.

"Does anybody really believe that this 'Election' was honest," Trump posted online.

The new polling from UC San Diego shows that among Republicans, there still may be a receptive audience to such claims. When asked if politicians are discussing voter fraud too much, too little or about the right amount, just 15% of Republicans answered "too much."

"There is both demand among the Republican base for continued focus on this issue, and as we saw even after the election, politicians are continuing to supply it," Kousser said. "As long as those two dynamics are there, there's still going to be a market for these concerns."

To be clear, in most elections — domestically and internationally — supporters of the losing party usually see a dip in trust.

"It's sort of like asking people after an overtime basketball game, do you trust the referees?" Kousser said.

But in an ideal world, he said that oscillation would be more like 10 percentage points in either direction. Not the 30-point swing seen among Republicans between 2024 and 2026.

The numbers do still represent an improvement from 2020, however. At that time, leading up to the Jan. 6 riot at the U.S. Capitol in 2021, just 22% of Republicans said they had confidence in the nationwide vote count, according to similar polling done by MIT's Election Data and Science Lab.

#5867. A second judge blocks Trump's arch, saying it's unlikely to succeed in court

President Trump holds up a model of his proposed triumphal arch at a White House event in Oct. 2025. Two different federal judges have ruled against it this week. John McDonnell/AP hide caption

A federal judge in Virginia plans to issue a preliminary injunction blocking President Trump's triumphal arch indefinitely.

District Judge Leonie Brinkema said during a Friday hearing that she would grant the request of the county board of Arlington, Va., which sued over the arch in October. Democracy Forward, the nonprofit representing the plaintiffs, confirmed the ruling in a statement shared with NPR.

"We are pleased that the court has recognized the irreparable harm that will come to people who live and work in Arlington and stopped the massive vanity project while our case is heard and considered by the court," said senior counsel Kate Talmor.

Justice Department spokesperson Matthew Nies told NPR it will appeal the decision immediately.

The 250-foot arch would be built in Memorial Circle, which is part of D.C. but sits on the Virginia side of the Potomac River, a short distance from both Arlington National Cemetery and Ronald Reagan Washington National Airport, both in Arlington.

Arlington County has argued that the arch would "cause significant harm" to its residents in multiple ways, including increasing noise and air pollution and "clogging thoroughfares needed for emergency transport services." And it said an arch that doubles as a military complex — as Trump has suggested — would pose public safety risks and require additional county resources to manage.

Arlington also argues the arch would disrupt historic sightlines between Arlington National Cemetery and D.C.'s National Mall, and pose logistical challenges for the roughly 24 to 27 funerals the cemetery holds every weekday. The county also has concerns about air and road traffic, saying construction-related delays could cause more accidents and disrupt emergency vehicles far beyond the traffic circle.

"In the last 60 days, Arlington police, fire, and emergency units have responded to approximately 200 incidents in the area surrounding the Circle, including traffic stops and assisting with unconscious persons within Arlington National Cemetery," they wrote. "And the visitors the Arch is intended to attract will increase demand for those same emergency services."

The plaintiffs, like many critics of the arch, accused the Trump administration of rushing through federally mandated environmental reviews and failing to consider alternatives. The National Park Service has defended the federal review process — which ended in approval earlier this month — and said building the arch somewhere else would defeat its purpose.

"The County brings this lawsuit to halt the agency's feverish pace toward construction of the Arch and to ensure that the 'hard look' mandated by [the National Environmental Preservation Act] is completed before the agency commences disruptive and harmful ground-disturbing activities," the plaintiffs wrote last month.

NPR has reached out to the White House for comment. Spokesperson Davis Ingle told NPR earlier this week that the administration will "continue making the case for this beautiful and fitting monument and are confident we will be fully vindicated as this litigation proceeds."

Brinkema, an appointee of President Clinton, is the second judge to rule against the arch this week.

On Wednesday, D.C. District Judge Tanya Chutkan extended a restraining order that prevents the Trump administration from breaking ground through Dec. 3 while she considers the underlying legal arguments.

That case was brought earlier this year by a group that includes Vietnam War veterans and was joined by the governments of D.C. and Virginia last month. Nine Kennedy family members moved to get involved last week.

Friday's ruling goes even further, preventing construction while Arlington's legal challenge plays out.

And Brinkema suggested that Arlington is likely to prevail. The judge said in court that she didn't see "any way" the government could win the case on the merits.

She reiterated that the project lacks congressional approval, which the Trump administration has not sought. It argues that the arch is covered by a 1925 law authorizing a pair of much shorter columns at that same location, which were never built. Both judges have frowned upon that argument.

"It hurts your credibility with the court by focusing on that," Brinkema told lawyers Friday, according to Reuters.

Brinkema also called it a "rushed job," and seemed to find the government's analysis of the structure's environmental and economic impact inadequate, particularly concerning the impact of construction cranes — which the government has said would be taller than the arch itself — on airspace.

The Federal Aviation Administration has said the arch would not pose a hazard to aircraft as long as it is lit a certain way, including with a controversial eternal flame on top. But aviation safety experts are concerned that the structure would add an additional layer of risk to an already notoriously complicated airspace. They say that's especially true if it has any military capabilities, which were not included in the FAA's analysis.

#5861. SOLRITE and sonnen collaborate on integrated VPP breaker

SOLRITE and sonnen plan to digitally integrate VPP smart breakers into what the companies are calling the SOLRITE sonnen VPP. The expanded VPP Node combines sonnenHub with ADAPT Energy’s VPP smart breaker technology to manage major household loads. The VPP smart breaker, controlled by ADAPT Energy, can also participate in on-grid VPP events, enabling the same electrical infrastructure to be used for both backup and grid participation.

“Our work with ADAPT Energy is a meaningful step forward in how we think about residential battery storage and VPP participation,” said Regan George, CEO and founder of SOLRITE Energy. “We have already demonstrated the tremendous value of connecting sonnen batteries together as a pooled grid asset. Now, we’re going inside the home and unlocking another layer of flexibility that has been sitting there all along. By combining the battery with additional load management made possible by the VPP smart breaker technology, we’re creating a more valuable node for the grid while also giving homeowners a clever backup solution.”

The system can shed large loads without requiring those circuits to be relocated. ADAPT Energy keeps the smart breakers synchronized with the sonnen battery during backup mode and automatically restores the breakers when grid power returns.

This integration is designed to manage whole-home electrical loads in the background, not requiring homeowners to reset circuits during an outage.

“This is about making whole-home backup work the way homeowners expect it to,” said Troy Morgan, president of PanTech Design, the developer of ADAPT Energy. “Instead of putting the pressure on homeowners to change the way their home is wired, and to manually manipulate loads during an outage, we’re putting intelligence into their energy system, which provides a seamless and automatic backup power experience for their home. Our technology empowers home loads to supply optimal backup power during an outage or provide valuable flexibility to the grid.”

When a VPP event happens, the sonnen battery can discharge or charge with direct load shedding via ADAPT. ADAPT Energy will strictly shed pre-approved household loads. A customer who has opted to include a pool pump, for example, can have that load temporarily turned off while the sonnen battery is discharging to the grid.

“With the addition of ADAPT Energy’s VPP smart-breaker technology to the SOLRITE–sonnen ecosystem, we are expanding the virtual power plant from a battery-centered resource into a fully orchestrated home-energy platform,” said Blake Richetta, chairman and CEO of sonnen. “By synchronizing energy that has been harnessed in our battery with intelligent, real-time control of household electrical loads, we can deliver more flexible, responsive capacity to the grid precisely when it is needed most. This integration materially strengthens the value of SOLRITE’s virtual power plant—supporting grid reliability, creating greater customer value, and enabling SOLRITE to continue delivering industry-leading solar-plus-storage VPA solutions to homeowners.”

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.
