#5856. An effort to circumvent the Electoral College may be within reach. But hurdles remain

Voting booths are seen at Glass Elementary School's polling station in Eagle Pass, Texas, on Nov. 8, 2022. Mark Felix/AFP via Getty Images hide caption

A national effort to ensure that only winners of the popular vote become president could reach a critical milestone soon.

It's a possible outcome of huge Democratic wins in this month's midterm elections.

Next year, Democrats will fully control five states that could sign the National Popular Vote Interstate Compact, an agreement among states to award their presidential electoral votes to the winner of the nationwide popular vote, not the winner of their respective states.

If they do join other 18 Democratic-led states — plus the District of Columbia — that are in the compact, the effort will have surpassed the needed 270 electoral votes required to go into effect, making an end run around the Electoral College. So far no Republican-led states have joined the compact and the party is largely not unified on the issue.

There are still plenty of political, practical and legal hurdles that this effort would need to clear. But proponents are optimistic many of those hurdles will be cleared – even perhaps in time for the next presidential election.

"I've never been more confident that 63% of American voters can get what they want in the 2028 presidential election, which is a national popular vote for president." Patrick Rosenstiel, a senior consultant to National Popular Vote, the group pushing for the compact in states across the country, told NPR, referencing Pew polling.

Right now, the compact has a total of 222 electoral votes. The five states that will soon be completely in the hands of Democrats – Arizona, Pennsylvania, Michigan, Nevada and Wisconsin – add up to 61 electoral votes. Combinations of at least four of those states would put the compact over 270.

So far, governors and incoming governors in Pennsylvania and Nevada have said they are open to signing legislation that would bring their states into the interstate agreement. But other governors have yet to publicly weigh in.

One of the looming questions over these efforts is whether these states – all presidential battlegrounds – would be willing to give up their immense influence in elections ahead of the 2028 presidential contests.

And leaders in each state will also have to weigh whether they want to be the tipping point in moving forward a movement that could upend how the U.S. decides presidential winners.

Already, some Democrats are more reticent on the issue than they were before. For example, Democratic Arizona Gov. Katie Hobbs, who had previously supported the measure, has not commented recently on whether the state's new Democratic majorities should sign on. NPR reached out to Hobbs' office for comment but did not receive a response before publication.

And then of course, there are the various legal and logistical hurdles.

Some legal scholars have observed that this agreement between states could run afoul of the compact clause in the Constitution, which says that "no state shall, without the consent of Congress … enter into any agreement or compact with another state … unless actually invaded, or in such imminent danger as will not admit of delay."

Norman Miller, a constitutional law professor at Willamette University, said that beyond the legal hurdles, he sees a range of practical issues with the compact.

Mostly, he said, it could upend what is a core feature of American presidential elections, which is that they are largely decentralized.

States individually decide who can vote and how they can vote. He said forcing electors from one state to vote according to the tallies in another state could create some tension.

"We would want to have a standardized, uniform set of rules regarding who gets to vote for president, how they vote, how those votes are tabulated, and to have a federal election authority doing that tabulation," he said. "I think trying to nationalize [the current system] and entrusting a national election to 51 separate election administrators would just sow discord and distrust."

But John Koza, the chairman of National Popular Vote, said if the compact works the way it is supposed to, none of that will be necessary.

"We don't need a centralized bureaucracy or a presidentially appointed commission that could be manipulated," he told NPR. "The simple fact is current state and federal law provide for the issuance of a certified vote count from each state. And all that has to be done is add them up."

But mostly, Miller said the biggest question before this effort is whether such a large reform should be done via a constitutional amendment.

Upending the Electoral College, some argue, would be a profound change to how the U.S. chooses the leader of the free world. Opponents of a national popular vote, such as Save Our States, argue that without the incentives created by the Electoral College, American politics will move more toward "toward more regional politics and would exacerbate existing divides."

Miller said even though it will be much more difficult to pull off, it would be worth going through a more fulsome process via Article 5 to get rid of the Electoral College, instead of working around it.

"This is where I think process matters," he said. "If you really want to have a national popular vote for the president and avoid all of the legal and practical difficulties, you need to do that through a constitutional amendment, because only a constitutional amendment can address the legal and practical challenges of conducting a national popular vote for the presidency."

A similar effort to pass an amendment to get rid of the Electoral College in 1969 failed, though. And very few amendments have even gotten close to passing in recent history.

Rosenstiel, the consultant to the National Popular Vote, said this is why he thinks groups like his are seeking a creative solution to something most Americans agree is a problem.

And, he said, he ultimately thinks a national popular vote will solve multiple problems at once. For one, he said, it will force presidential candidates to appeal to most of the country, as opposed to a select group of voters in battleground states.

And, importantly, he said, it would make all voters feel like their vote matters.

"We should have a system where every American citizen feels valued and is valued when they cast their vote," he said. "And there's only one reform that delivers it."

#5848. Solar lease holdouts still see value in privately-owned residential projects

The One Big Beautiful Bill Act drastically reduced the lifespan of federal subsidies available to certain segments of the solar market, and among the first to go was the investment tax credit for residential projects. The ITC is a historic renewable energy subsidy that provides a 30% tax credit against the total cost of an eligible project, and it is currently available to the commercial and utility-scale solar segments.

A residential solar project in New York installed by Nickels Energy Solutions. Credit: Nickels Energy Solutions

After 2025, homeowners hoping to install solar on their house couldn’t subsidize the project’s cost with this federal subsidy if it was privately owned. However, a loophole in the tax credit language has allowed leased or “third party-owned” (TPO) solar projects to still receive the ITC (25D) on residential solar projects. In this scenario, it matters who owns the solar project, not necessarily where it’s built. The project can be subsidized because it is owned by a commercial company, and not the homeowner.

Some residential solar installers are still strictly pursuing and installing privately-owned solar projects. Nickels Energy Solutions (NES), a residential solar contractor working in Central New York believes a privately-owned PV system is still the better deal for the customer.

“We don’t have the same offers as some of the bigger companies,” said Kevin Nickels, VP of sales with NES. “If you can offer leases, it’s like the ‘haves and have-nots.’ I don’t have that loophole or that trick up my sleeve, so it forces me to get the project sold, while I’m as sharp as I can be with margins to make it somewhat competitive.”

TPO solar transactions are typically arranged at a lower up-front cost than a wholly owned solar project. Lease contract lengths vary by provider, and financiers have surety in these transactions because the projects will be built with products from an approved vendor list (AVL). Products on an AVL can qualify for a domestic content subsidy, another tax credit in addition to the ITC, because they are manufactured using a certain percentage of domestic components.

These are premium products vetted for AVLs. But it can also mean that installers are paying premium prices for components in building TPO solar projects.

Nickels believes that, while a privately owned residential solar project will cost more at the start, the combination of expensive components and interest rates for financing over a lease term — with factors like rate increases — can cost a customer more in the long run.

“I don’t think all leases are bad,” he said. “I just don’t think it’s what’s best for the industry or the homeowner.”

Rudy Wright, owner of Wright-Way Solar of Tyler, Texas, has shifted the company’s focus from building new projects to servicing existing systems. As the ITC exited the residential solar market, so did many of the lenders financing these projects. Without lending options, Wright-Way’s installation output has slowed down, and those clients who can afford solar are paying with cash or using a home equity loan to cover the cost.

Despite that, Wright said he isn’t very interested in building leased solar projects.

Wright-Way Solar built a solar plus storage project at a Ranch in Texas featuring arrays that are facing east, west and south. Credit: Wright-Way Solar

“I do think it cost me some of those entry level clients, but every time I analyzed the financing end of it, I felt like I was benefitting my company and the industry more than the customer,” Wright said. “I haven’t seen the angle where bringing a pre-purchase agreement or bringing a lease agreement has enough ironclad return for the customer.”

Tyler is a unique market for solar because many of Wright-Way’s customers are trying to make their properties more resilient, and less reliant on the grid, by installing solar and storage, and not just seeking cost savings for their energy use. Wright has installed solar plus storage systems on Texan ranches at a scale close a small commercial project.

That promise of resilience is still enough reason for some people to commission a solar project in Texas, where the state’s independent electrical grid operator has faltered and experienced dangerous outages in recent years.

“Folks realize money in the bank doesn’t do you any good if the power goes out,” Wright said.

NES is still building a steady pipeline of residential solar projects in New York. Nickels said that has been possible in part because many of its clients are upper-middle class; but New York also maintains an incentive program for solar projects, NY-Sun, which includes a 25% state tax credit for residential systems.

“The OBBBA caught a lot of states flat-footed,” Nickels said. “Without federal support, it puts a lot of pressure on what the state is offering.”

Some states’ solar subsidy programs have stepped in where federal support has diminished since the OBBBA was passed. The North Carolina Clean Energy Technology Center maintains the Database of State Incentives for Renewables & Efficiency, which analyzes which states currently have policies and incentives supporting clean energy developments. Every state has policy enabling solar projects, but not necessarily cost incentives for individual projects.

While traditional residential solar projects have lost a significant subsidy, the reality is that energy costs have risen in response to new grid demands from hyperscale data centers and oil scarcity from the United States’ war in Iran. Sure, a privately owned residential solar project won’t receive the ITC, but it can still stabilize energy costs without them.

“We lost a big incentive, but the rate increase is proportional,” Nickels said.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5836. Three Routes to VPP Revenue for Installers, EPCs, and Developers Utility programs, wholesale markets, and bilateral contracts each come with different eligibility rules, payment structures, and commercial responsibilities.

By Intersolar & Energy Storage North America

In June 2026, Google and Voltus announced a three-year agreement to unlock up to 100 megawatts (MW) of distributed capacity annually in PJM. Under Voltus’s Bring Your Own Capacity model, Google funds procurement while Voltus aggregates qualifying distributed energy resources (DERs) and compensates participating customers.1

The deal is one of three distinct paths to earn revenue from DERs. Across the U.S., utility programs, wholesale electricity markets, and bilateral contracts each have their own eligibility requirements, payment structures and commercial responsibilities.

Utility programs offer an established pathway for compensating customers and asset owners who reduce demand, shift consumption or discharge stored energy during periods of grid need. Payments may be tied to enrollment, committed capacity, measured performance or a combination of these factors. Massachusetts, for example, offers residential battery customers $275 per kW of average summer performance through ConnectedSolutions2.

Beyond customer incentive programs, utilities also procure distributed flexibility through contracts to address local grid constraints or broader capacity needs. Unlike standardized incentives, these arrangements can establish specific delivery requirements and compensation terms, creating opportunities for developers and service providers to assemble and manage DER portfolios. During a July 2026 heat event, Green Mountain Power dispatched 90 MW of stored energy capacity, helping customers avoid an estimated $6 million in peak-related costs during one peak hour3.

Aggregated DERs can earn revenue from energy, capacity, and ancillary services in wholesale electricity markets, subject to regional market rules and resource qualification. Unlike utility-specific programs, wholesale markets can provide access to broader regional revenue opportunities and multiple grid services. For developers and installers, participation typically occurs through an aggregator or other qualified market participant rather than through direct market registration.

While Federal Energy Regulatory Commission (FERC) Order 2222 established a broader framework for DER participation, uneven implementation across regional markets continues to constrain commercial opportunities for aggregated resources. Market access does not automatically translate into revenue, as individual resources must still meet qualification requirements and comply with market-specific rules. New York Independent System Operator (NYISO), for example, requires DER aggregations to meet minimum capacity and real-time telemetry requirements, which can increase participation costs and limit the economic viability of smaller resources.

Wholesale participation can also expose VPP portfolios to fluctuating market prices, performance obligations and restrictions on receiving compensation for overlapping services. In Texas, ERCOT’s Aggregate Distributed Energy Resource (ADER) pilot allows aggregated DERs to participate in wholesale energy and ancillary services markets, subject to qualification, telemetry, and dispatch requirements.

Bilateral contracts allow utilities and large electricity consumers to procure distributed flexibility directly from aggregators and service providers. Growing interest from data center operators and other large energy buyers is expanding the potential market for VPP capacity beyond traditional utility programs. Unlike standardized programs, bilateral agreements can be tailored to a buyer’s specific needs, with negotiated terms for capacity, delivery periods, compensation, and performance obligations.

The appropriate route depends on existing customer relationships, market access, and the responsibilities each partner assumes. Developers and EPCs working on commercial storage projects may find opportunities through utility procurement or capacity contracts, while residential installers with established battery customer bases may be well positioned to incorporate VPP enrollment into utility programs. Wholesale markets can expand revenue opportunities through aggregation partners, and bilateral contracts can create opportunities to develop resources around specific buyer needs.

Sponsored Content by Intersolar & Energy Storage North America

#5833. Trump orders federal contractors to check with White House before responding to Congress

President Trump speaks at a news conference in the James Brady Press Briefing Room of the White House, Friday, Nov. 20, 2026, in Washington. Jacquelyn Martin/AP hide caption

NEW YORK (AP) — President Donald Trump is directing federal contractors and subcontractors to consult with his administration about inquiries they receive from Congress and to withhold any communications that the White House considers privileged, according to a new executive order.

The companies must also coordinate with the White House and U.S. agencies on "appropriate responses" to any such requests, the order issued late Friday says. If they fail to comply, the White House warns their contracts could be terminated and their future work with the federal government suspended.

The order is the Republican administration's latest effort to resist what are expected to be wide-ranging and aggressive oversight efforts from Democrats when they take over Congress in January. Democrats have made government contracts one of their top investigative targets, noting that federal money has been flowing to companies linked with Trump's family, particularly at the Pentagon.

White House officials have already begun taking steps to exert more control over how agencies and officials respond to congressional requests, anticipating there will be a flood of investigations initiated by Democrats.

Trump's counsel, Will Scharf, told federal agencies on Monday that they must seek approval from his office and the White House's Office of Legislative Affairs before agreeing to appear before Congress. Scharf also suggested in a recent letter to Rep. Robert Garcia of California, the top Democrat on the House Oversight Committee, that some congressional investigations may amount to "blatant criminality."

Garcia sent letters this week directing members of Trump's family and top administration officials to preserve documents. Scharf sought to dismiss the requests, noting in a letter to Democratic lawmakers that they are not yet in the majority and cannot compel compliance.

Responding to the new executive order, Garcia said it was part of the administration's "blatant attack on Congress' constitutional right to conduct oversight." He said that despite efforts to impede investigations, "we're just getting started."

In a fact sheet, the White House framed the new requirements as "protecting executive privileges from congressional overreach," arguing that some congressional leaders have tried to intrude on executive branch privileges by "harassing private companies that contract with the federal government."

Presidents of both parties have long used executive privilege to resist congressional inquiries, but the claims can set up messy, protracted legal fights as parties dispute whether it applies. Executive privilege allows the president to keep information from the courts, Congress and the public to protect the confidentiality of White House deliberations, but the protection is far from absolute.

The latest executive order applies to all contractors and subcontractors, a universe of tens of thousands of businesses.

#5829. ES Foundry ships more than 1 GW of U.S.-manufactured solar cells

ES Foundry, a U.S. manufacturer of high-efficiency solar cells, is at RE+ 2026 this week to showcase its American-made solar cells and announce a milestone in its operating capacity.

According to the company, it is the first and only crystalline silicon solar cell manufacturer to ship more than 1 gigawatt (GW) of domestically manufactured solar cells in the United States.

As one of the few U.S. solar cell manufacturers with a fully operational facility producing and shipping cells to customers, ES Foundry is helping move American solar manufacturing to commercial reality.

In July, the company announced it reached its full 3-GW manufacturing capacity. The company began production on its first 1-GW line last year and has now completed its 2-GW expansion line.

Solar Power World attended the opening of its Greenwood, South Carolina facility last year, reporting on its manufacturing outfit and its unique approach to supply deals that include working with large-scale project developers and utilities to guarantee domestic content supplies.

At RE+, attendees can see its U.S.-manufactured high-efficiency monocrystalline bifacial PERC solar cells and meet with company representatives to discuss domestic sourcing, manufacturing capabilities, and the evolving needs of the U.S. solar market.

“There’s an important difference between talking about building a domestic solar supply chain and actually making it happen,” said ES Foundry CEO Alex Zhu in a statement. “At ES Foundry, we’re manufacturing at commercial scale, shipping cells to customers and navigating the realities of U.S. production every day. That experience gives us a unique perspective on what it will take to build a competitive, sustainable American solar industry. Success requires more than manufacturing capacity. It requires the right economics, technology investments, customer demand and policies to support long-term growth. We’re proud to be delivering on the promise of American manufacturing today while helping shape the industry’s future.”

#5817. enSights brings AI-powered business management to solar and storage operations

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 16, 2026

As solar and energy storage portfolios grow in both size and complexity, managing performance is just one part of the equation. Owners and operators must also keep track of power purchase agreements (PPAs), warranties, service contracts, billing, compliance requirements and project-level profitability.

enSights is looking to bring those responsibilities together with the launch of its AI Studio, a new addition to its Energy Business Management (EBM) platform. The company announced the launch Nov. 10.

The AI Studio is designed to give solar and storage owners, operators, and asset managers access to a customizable team of AI specialists that can support business and operational workflows. Through no-code tools, users can create AI dashboards and reports using natural-language instructions, while still establishing their own workflows, routines, data-access permissions and approval requirements.

The approach here reflects a broader shift toward renewable energy software, it’s moving beyond monitoring individual assets toward managing the financial and operational performance of entire portfolios.

According to enSights, its EBM platform brings together energy management systems (EMS), production data, billing, PPA information, operations and maintenance, contractual obligations and compliance on a unified data foundation. That integration is central to the company’s AI strategy.

Rather than limiting AI applications to identifying equipment issues or answering generation-related questions, the platform is designed to connect energy production with the contracts and financial obligations associated with each project.

The AI Studio includes an orchestrator that coordinates specialized AI agents focused on areas like monitoring, operations, revenue, warranties, permits, lenders, insurers, and asset management. For portfolio managers, those capabilities could help reduce the time spent reviewing disconnected systems and manually reconciling information across departments. Automated reviews of contractual obligations, revenue performance and operational alerts could also help teams identify issues earlier and direct attention to matters that still require human intervention.

The enSights AI Studio dashboard provides solar and energy storage operators with AI-powered tools for asset management, operational workflows, and financial oversight. Credit: enSights

enSights emphasized that people remain in control of the AI Team. Users determine which workflows the agents handle and where approvals are required. Physical asset controls remain governed by established EMS and site-level systems.

The AI Studio also builds on an expanded financial management offering within the EBM platform. Its Financial Asset Management Module includes revenue assurance, contract, and obligation management, and profit-and-loss reporting.

The module is intended to provide project-level financial visibility, helping companies identify and prevent revenue losses while generating reports for investors.

The platform also includes real-time EMS, weather and tariff forecasting, along with dispatch planning intended to optimize revenue while maintaining grid safety and local compliance.

enSights says its platform is deployed globally across more than 100,000 sites, representing over 5 GW of assets. The company plans to demonstrate the AI Studio and its broader EBM platform at RE+ 2026.

As AI gains traction across the clean energy industry, the next competitive advantage could come from connecting operational intelligence with the financial and contractual decisions that determine how successfully renewable energy portfolios perform.

#5812. From Social Leasing to Social Fleets: A Proposed Law Lends Credibility to the Goal of One Million Affordable Electric Cars

Social leasing could soon change in scale, name, and business model. The bill introduced by MP Christine Arrighi aims to create a public service offering one million “low-cost electric vehicles” (ALM). Scheduled for December 3rd in the National Assembly, its examination is expected to place at the heart of the debate a now crucial question: how to make social leasing sustainable and widespread?

Several presidential candidates have made reaching one million vehicles (or even more) one of the hallmarks of their campaigns¹. While this convergence is excellent news, the battle of figures says nothing about how to achieve this goal.

The current system cannot simply be scaled up twentyfold. Its cost per vehicle, intermittent financing, imperfect social targeting, lack of a guaranteed solution after three years, and the resale risks borne by dealerships are all structural limitations. The 2026 edition also revealed its dependence on Renault and Stellantis, which can at any time restrict volumes to favor more profitable traditional sales.

The proposed law changes its approach. Sponsored by MP Christine Arrighi, it draws inspiration from the proposals of T&E France and Bernard Julien, stemming from their study on the future of social leasing, published in June 2026.

In this new model, the government would directly purchase the vehicles at an average net cost of €15,000. These vehicles would be kept for twelve years in a public fleet and leased successively to one or more households for an average monthly rent of €150 before being resold. The day-to-day management of the fleet would be delegated to groups of public or private operators within the local areas.

The initial investment to build the fleet would be financed by borrowing, with a maximum outstanding amount of €7.8 billion in 2035. Lease payments and resales, supplemented by €350 million in annual public support (equivalent to the current social leasing budget), would cover service costs and repay the debt within eighteen years. By then, the system would be self-financing, and the state would own one million cars worth approximately €10 billion.

“This model is much more beneficial for public finances,” explains Diane Strauss, director of T&E France. “It requires an initial funding effort, but it eventually pays for itself. Instead of subsidizing at a loss, we build up an asset that generates its own revenue. This is what makes the move to a million vehicles financially viable.”

In addition to providing long-term protection for low-income motorists against rising and volatile fuel prices, the proposal represents a genuine industrial planning policy. The announcement of a public order for two million vehicles over twenty years (including fleet renewal) would indeed give the French automotive sector the visibility necessary to invest and prepare for the future.

“This will also allow us to direct our production towards the vehicles that households truly need: affordable, fuel-efficient, repairable electric cars, produced in France and Europe,” concludes Léo Larivière, automotive transition expert at T&E France. “This proposal must now open a substantive discussion among all stakeholders. It is high time to prepare the next steps for one of the rare examples of policies that reconcile climate, social justice, and industrial planning.”

¹ Raphaël Glucksmann and Marine Tondelier aim for one million vehicles. Jean-Luc Mélenchon proposes a public fleet of 1.4 million cars by 2032, and François Ruffin raises the target to three million. Gabriel Attal and Dominique de Villepin also advocate expanding social leasing, without specifying a target.

Transport & Environment’s (T&E) vision is a zero-emission mobility system that is affordable and has minimal impacts on our health, climate and environment. Created over 30 years ago, we have shaped some of Europe’s most important environmental laws.

#5808. Solar Insure Launches 20-Year Commercial Battery Warranty, Expanding Protection for Solar & Storage Projects

Launching at RE+ Las Vegas, November 17–20 | Visit Solar Insure at Booth C4190

Commercial property owners invest in solar and battery storage to manage energy costs and strengthen resilience. One question can shape that investment from the start. Who pays when equipment needs repairs years after installation?

Solar Insure today expanded its SI-Commercial Suite with two new warranties. SI-Commercial Battery is a 20-year limited warranty for commercial battery storage systems, and SI-Commercial Total covers solar and battery storage under one warranty. Together with the existing SI-Commercial Solar warranty, these additions allow Certified Providers to offer protection tailored to each project, whether it includes solar, battery storage, or both. Solar Insure protects more than 100,000 systems nationwide.

“Commercial projects are built for the long term, and their protection should reflect that,” said Ara Agopian, CEO of Solar Insure. “By expanding our commercial warranty suite, we’re helping solar companies offer protection that fits the systems their customers are investing in. It’s about giving property owners greater confidence not just in the installation, but in the years of ownership that follow.”

A failed inverter or declining battery capacity can lead to costs an owner did not budget for. Even when a manufacturer covers replacement equipment, the owner may still be responsible for labor and service costs. The expanded suite helps Certified Providers explain which repair expenses are covered so customers can evaluate their potential repair costs alongside system performance and installation price.

“Commercial battery owners are making a long-term investment, but some of the most significant expenses can emerge years after installation,” said Dean Chiaravallotti, Chief Revenue Officer at Solar Insure. “A battery replacement or unexpected repair can change the financial picture of a project. When providers can explain how they address those costs before the customer signs, they help buyers make a more informed investment decision. That’s the value we’re bringing to commercial storage.”

Solar companies must become Solar Insure Certified Providers to offer SI-Commercial warranties. Certification includes a review of the company’s qualifications, business practices, and commitment to customer service.

For qualifying claims, Solar Insure pays covered repair costs directly to the Certified Provider or service provider performing the work. If the original installer is no longer available, Solar Insure helps coordinate next steps, which may include identifying another qualified service provider. When a property changes hands, coverage transfers to the new owner at no cost.

“A lot can change over the life of a commercial energy system. Properties change hands, service providers change, and equipment eventually needs attention,” said Chiaravallotti. “Property owners need to know what happens w          hen those changes occur. Our goal is to give them a clearer path forward, with protection and support that extend well beyond the original installation.”

Solar companies can contact Solar Insure to learn about becoming a Certified Provider. Commercial property owners can speak with a Solar Insure Certified Provider about which warranty fits their project.

Solar Insure is a leading provider of solar and storage warranties, focused on protecting long-term system performance and elevating accountability across the renewable energy industry. Through rigorous provider certification and governance, Solar Insure delivers durable protection that residential and commercial installers can stand behind.

#5804. CA Governor Talks Climate Action And Punches Nazis On European Tour

Tesla CEO and former California resident Elon Musk has been taking quite a bit of heat over his Nazi-adjacent rhetoric, compounded in recent weeks as reports continue to surface that he has manipulated Starlink military satellite service to Ukraine again, enabling Russian President Vladimir Putin to extend his war of aggression into other states of the former USSR. Why? Who knows! Regardless, another powerful California resident has been gearing up to push back against both Elon Musk and Vladimir Putin…

…If you guessed that someone is California Governor Gavin Newsom, run right out and buy yourself a cigar. California would be the fourth-largest single economy in the world if it was a nation unto itself. It offers living proof that decarbonization is a driver of economic growth, not a drag, and Newsom is happy to bring that achievement to the attention of the world.

Newsom has been eyeballed as a contender for the Democratic nomination in the 2028 Presidential election cycle. If so, he is off to a roaring start. Gavin chose the COP 31 climate talks in Türkiye to raise his profile on the international stage, while throwing down the renewable energy gauntlet against Putin and also taking jabs at Elon Musk.

Ukraine has considerable fossil energy resources that Putin can fold into the Russian economy (and his allies’ pockets), which explains why Putin launched an attempt to take Ukraine by storm in  February of 2022. Ukraine also has considerable renewable energy resources that it can bring to bear as an aspirational member of the European Union. With that in mind, it’s no surprise to see longtime Putin ally and fossil energy fan Donald Trump, and America’s shadow President Elon Musk, align with Russia against the interests of Ukraine.

It’s not just Ukraine. All of the former Soviet Union states are at risk, including members of NATO, the North Atlantic Treaty Organization. I visited NATO member and former satellite state Estonia on a clean tech tour after Putin invaded Ukraine, to get an up-close look at the tiny but innovative nation’s outsized influence on the renewable energy transition in Europe and beyond (here’s another example). Humming in the background of a round of site visits and presentations was an ongoing conversation about Estonia’s strategy to fend off Russia.

One manifestation of the defensive posture came this week, when Estonia announced the closing of border crossings, effective December 1, after accusing Russia of orchestrating an arson attack on one of its defense firms. The independent Ukrainian news organization Hromadske is also among those reporting that Estonia has been moving government services into remote digital platforms, safe from Russian attacks on data centers and other critical infrastructure.

Now let’s take a look at Governor Newsom’s all-but-in-name campaign launch this week, where a stopover in Poland illustrates how he intends to conflate anti-fascism with the renewable energy transition over the next two years — in other words, saving the planet and punching Nazis, too.

Considering that Elon Musk has spent a considerable sum of money to vault President Trump into the White House for a second term in office while also stirring up Nazi-adjacent sentiment in Europe and elsewhere through his “X” social media platform, Newsom’s highly public visit to the POLIN Museum of the History of Polish Jews in Warsaw was particularly ripe with symbolism. Among other historical exhibits, the museum commemorates the the Warsaw Uprising of 1943, known as the first — and for a long time the only — major urban battle against Nazi occupation during World War II.

After laying a ceremonial wreath at the Monument to the Ghetto Heroes at the museum, Newsom deployed his @CAgovernor account on X to underscore what appears to be the unifying theme of his campaign, calling the museum a “powerful reminder of fighting against the darkest forces.”

“The museum is a powerful reminder that we have to remain vigilant to protect what we value even in the darkest of times,” Newsom also elaborated in a press statement.

Newsom also used the occasion of the visit to sign a new climate partnership with Poland. The Memorandum of Understanding covers pollution reduction, energy efficiency, grid modernization, energy storage, and climate resiliency as well as renewable energy.

“Energy security, economic strength, and a livable climate are not competing goals. They are one mission, and no nation or state can achieve it alone. California and Poland come from different histories but share the same responsibility: to protect our people, power our economies, and leave future generations a safer world. This partnership puts that responsibility to work,” Newsom emphasized in a public statement that supports the running theme of fighting fascism with decarbonization.

Poland already has a running head start on renewable energy through its Baltic Sea offshore wind resources. Other clean tech sectors are on the move as well. To kickstart the MOU, Newsom met with the leading Polish battery firm Impact Clean Power Technology. “Impact designs and builds batteries for electric and hydrogen buses, trucks, rail and industrial vehicles, as well as for mobile robotics, with a particular focus on humanoid robots,” Newsom’s office explains.

“This place is all about entrepreneurial innovation, and it’s about imagination, the secret sauce,” Newsom adds, referring to Impact’s rapidly expanding plans for the future.

Poland was also the scene of the America-Ukraine Clean Energy Forum in Warsaw, providing another opportunity for Newsom to draw the connection between anti-fascism and the renewable energy transition. Referring to the new MOU, Newsom told participants at the form that the energy transition represents an “opportunity to think anew and act anew.”

“It’s the opportunity to begin with a different mindset. It’s an opportunity to be more resilient in every way, shape or form. That’s why clean energy should be front and center, self-evident. It’s a strategic imperative. Period. Full stop,” Newsom emphasized (emphasis added).

In a press release recapping the event, Newsom’s office also noted that the Governor told his audience that American voters “spoke loudly in the midterm elections,” in a reaction to “the Trump administration’s rampant corruption and embrace of Big Oil.”

“The subtext, he said, was that clean energy was subconsciously on the ballot, too,” the press release continued.

Newsom was not done yet. On November 16, he announced a new contribution of medical aid to Ukraine from California, including a new field hospital, more than 1,300 additional hospital beds, and millions of bandages among other supplies.

He also toured Ukraine in person, an extended trip that underscored the role of innovation and technology in Ukraine’s ability to withstand Russian attacks, despite efforts by Donald Trump and Elon Musk to undermine its defense.

In response to Musk’s repeated manipulation of SpaceX satellite services, Ukraine has already developed a workaround in the form of its own drone-based guidance systems, as capably demonstrated just yesterday by the destruction of a key Russian military asset, the Crimea Bridge.

Thoughts? Before you drop a note in the discussion thread, note that Musk’s SpaceX venture has emerged as a new, leading consumer of natural gas in the US. The startup will need much more of the same to make good on its plans for launching 30 rockets per day from its new “Starbase” in Louisiana. So much for saving the planet…

#5803. Soneil Spark introduces its Mobile Battery Energy Storage System

This week, Soneil Spark rolls out its Mobile Battery Energy Storage System (Mobile BESS), the portable, modular battery storage built into a towable trailer.

The company, offering bespoke, modular energy solutions, is introducing Mobile BESS at RE+ 2026. It delivers temporary backup and off-grid power, working with what’s available by taking power from the grid, a generator, or solar.

“Wherever your business goes, the power goes with it. Simply tow it where energy demand grows,” said Sonya Jain, CEO of Soneil Spark, in a statement. “Start with one unit and add more as your needs change.”

It’s a simple set up, the company said. Mobile BESS works with no crane, no trenching, and no utility wait. That makes it a natural fit for a job site which may be months away from permanent service, a film set or event that can’t tolerate generator noise and fumes, a utility crew restoring power after a storm, or a remote mine or farm beyond the grid’s reach.

On sites that already run generators, the Mobile BESS syncs with them to cover peaks and start-up gaps, so the generators run fewer hours. Where diesel is restricted, it offers a quiet alternative.

Where containerized storage is built to be placed once, the Mobile BESS is built to be towed and to work in any weather. It includes:

The Mobile BESS is built for generator and equipment rental, construction, emergency and backup power, utilities, electrical contractors, events, film and entertainment, and off-grid sites such as mining and agriculture. A hybrid option adds EV charging.

#5798. Europe’s Inland Barges Need a Cargo Plan Before an Electric Fleet Plan

Coal throughput at Rotterdam rose 15.3% in the first nine months of 2026. Crude oil and petroleum-product volumes also increased. An inland vessel operator looking only at this year’s cargo market could see decades of work ahead. But an engine, battery conversion or replacement vessel bought now may still be operating in 2050, when the mix of cargo travelling through European ports and rivers will be different.

Rotterdam’s latest throughput release explains the coal rebound through recovering coking-coal movements, expensive natural gas and power-market conditions. Meanwhile, Eurostat reports that EU inland-waterway freight fell 3% in 2025 to 118 billion tonne-kilometres. Coal and crude-petroleum freight rose that year, while agricultural freight contracted. These changes are reshaping the services that ports, vessel owners and customers need to buy.

The Central Commission for the Navigation of the Rhine counted 6,224 dry-cargo vessels in Rhine countries in 2025, down from 6,410 in 2023. Smaller vessels account for much of the decline. Tankers moved the other way, rising from 1,400 in 2022 to 1,477 in 2025. The numbers describe a fleet already adapting to changing cargo and operating economics.

My TFIE Strategy Briefing on the inland barges Europe will need in 2050 uses a cargo-first model to work from the freight task to the future electric fleet. The underlying 98-page Inland Freight Electrification report is available to read.

A vessel owner has two connected capital decisions. The first is whether the freight service will still have customers through the useful life of the asset. The second is which propulsion and charging system can perform that service economically. Starting with equipment and then finding a market for it risks spending scarce investment capital on the wrong vessels.

I modelled a large European industrial port corridor from a 2025-style baseline to 2050. The central scenario assumes bulk cargo falls from 57.8 million tonnes to 34.4 million tonnes, a decline of about 40%. Wet bulk accounts for much of the loss, falling from 34.5 million to 15.0 million tonnes. Dry bulk declines from 23.3 million to 19.4 million tonnes. The model is a transparent scenario for investment decisions across this type of corridor, rather than a forecast of Rotterdam’s exact future cargo volumes.

The largest change is fossil-linked cargo. Fossil fuels and refinery streams fall from 19.0 million tonnes to 1.5 million tonnes, while coal and other solid fossil fuels fall from 2.5 million tonnes to zero. Combined, those reductions account for about 20 million tonnes of the scenario’s 23.4-million-tonne net decline. Any operator whose principal customers depend on moving coal or petroleum faces a different replacement decision from one carrying grain, construction minerals, chemicals or scrap.

Steel illustrates what cargo substitution means. The model reduces imported iron ore and other virgin iron units from 4.5 million tonnes to 3.0 million tonnes, while scrap and circular feedstocks rise from 1.8 million to 3.5 million tonnes. The industrial function continues as the materials moving to and from factories change. Existing barges and terminals may find new work, although parcel sizes, loading systems and routes will also change.

Biofuel feedstocks create a second trap. In the central scenario those movements peak near 5 million tonnes in 2030 before falling to 1.5 million tonnes in 2050. A temporary freight boom can support profitable operations without supporting a new vessel or terminal investment with a thirty-year life. The durability of the contract matters as much as the volume carried this year.

Cargo tonnage alone cannot size an electric fleet. The model translates commodities into powered vessel-equivalents using parcel sizes, routes, loaded calls and productivity. It starts with around 1,150 powered vessel-equivalents and ends with 664, a reduction of 42%. Dry cargo falls 17% in tonnes but 43% in powered vessel-equivalents because the modelled freight service changes. The starting 1,150 powered equivalents represent about 1,234 physical hulls after counting unpowered barges moved by pushers. Battery conversion decisions concern powered units; terminal and cargo-handling decisions also concern the additional hulls.

That distinction is particularly important for pushed formations. A pusher provides propulsion for several cargo barges. Converting one powered unit does not necessarily mean replacing every physical hull. Counting all hulls as propulsion conversions would inflate the equipment and financing requirement.

Low Rhine water levels bring a different constraint into the same calculation. A barge can remain on the register yet carry less freight per journey when draught is restricted. Cargo owners may have to buy more voyages, use different hulls or shift some freight to rail and road. Route depth, seasonal load factors and terminal access belong in the service model alongside cargo demand.

Ports should begin with a living cargo and route register showing which freight is declining, which remains, and which changes its material inputs. Vessel owners can connect that register to remaining hull life, annual utilization, cargo contracts and the cost of electric conversion. Utilities and terminal operators can then size grid connections, charging facilities and battery-container pools against vessels that will run on bankable routes. Infrastructure developed around dependable freight customers has a stronger prospect of earning a return.

Europe has good reasons to electrify established, high-utilization inland freight now. Battery-powered vessels are already serving container and bulk routes. The commercial opportunity is to invest in routes with surviving customers and electrify the services they will continue to buy. Expanding an electric fleet before measuring that freight task puts the order of decisions backward.

The complete TFIE Strategy Briefing works through the cargo substitutions, fleet denominator and investment decisions. The open Inland Freight Electrification report includes route-energy modelling, battery-pool sizing, terminal requirements and programme economics. TFIE Strategy also undertakes scoped corridor and fleet assessments for ports, operators and cargo owners.

#5790. OMCO Solar offers solar-powered tracker control system

OMCO Solar now offers a version of its OMCO Star Tracker Control System powered entirely by solar energy, allowing tracker controls to operate without AC power at the site.

The system, first launched last year, is designed for OMCO Origin Factory-Direct Trackers. Its tracker controllers, array controllers and sensor bridges use all-weather battery packs charged by solar modules.

According to OMCO, the technology eliminates the need for AC power to tracker control components and an uninterruptible power supply. It also allows tracker controls to operate and protects modules against wind, snow and hail before AC power is available.

“Our industry has solved so many problems as we have grown – we’re now the leading source for new electric energy capacity in the US. This addition to the OMCO Star product line solves one more – how to protect modules if there’s no AC power to the site even for a short time. We’re excited to offer this solution to our existing customers and to new partners,” said Matt Kesler, vice president of solar technology at OMCO Solar, in a statement.

The OMCO Star system uses LoRa, or long-range, wireless technology with a communication range greater than 1 mile. According to OMCO, this reduces the number of network controllers required and allows direct communication with every tracker without relying on a mesh network.

The company says LoRa is 100 times less susceptible to electromagnetic noise than Zigbee and better able to connect to distant trackers despite obstacles.

OMCO says its all-weather battery packs allow the solar-powered controls to operate reliably in cold climates. The system includes field-replaceable batteries, a phone app that eliminates the need for buttons on controllers and a remote monitoring and control portal.

The portal allows operators to move trackers for activities such as mowing, snow plowing and module cleaning. The system is developed, manufactured and supported by OMCO Solar in the United States.

“We are excited about bringing this innovation to the Utility Scale and DG tracker markets. Over the past few years, OMCO has expanded its capabilities beyond just being a US steel manufacturer to now being a true technology leader in our space. We look forward to supporting our customers/partners with the OMCO Star Tracker Control System, improving reliability, and reducing the labor and materials needed to build their projects,” said Eric Goodwin, vice president of business development at OMCO Solar.

OMCO Solar will display the all-DC tracker control system at RE+ in Las Vegas, November 17–19, in the North Hall, booth N516.

The new state-of-the-art factory in Oklahoma City will create about 300 jobs, strengthen the domestic solar supply chain, and establish a new benchmark for transparency and quality.

This month, Nextnova celebrated the official inauguration of its new solar cell manufacturing facility in Oklahoma City, marking an important milestone in strengthening America’s domestic solar manufacturing industry.

The facility will manufacture high-efficiency solar cells using advanced automation, Industry 4.0 technologies, and comprehensive digital traceability.

Production is scheduled to begin in March 2027 with an initial annual capacity of 2 GW, designed to expand to 5 GW as market demand grows.

Nextnova celebrates the inauguration of its new solar cell manufacturing facility in Oklahoma City. Credit: Nextnova

Designed for maximum quality and reliability, the facility features fully integrated manufacturing execution systems (MES), automated inspection technologies, advanced process control, and a dedicated analytical laboratory supporting continuous quality verification throughout production.

#5778. The grid will fall short of AI’s power needs. These 6 resources can close the gap.

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

No single option is fast, firm and large enough on its own, writes Simon Irish, CEO of Terrestrial Energy.

Simon Irish is CEO of Terrestrial Energy, a developer of small modular nuclear reactors.

Most planning for artificial intelligence’s electricity demand starts with a forecast and asks whether it is right.

I would start somewhere else: Assume the grid will fall short, then ask how to close the gap — and how fast.

The evidence supports that starting point. McKinsey & Company estimates the U.S. will need 30 GW to 55 GW of additional capacity by 2030 as data center demand grows and aging plants retire. PJM Interconnection, the largest U.S. grid operator, has already seen the impacts of this trend. Its latest capacity auction fell about 6.6 GW short of its reliability requirement and cleared at the price cap.

Forecasts can be wrong, and requested load can be speculative or duplicative. But the PJM result is a market outcome, not a forecast. The useful question is which tools can close the gap, by when and at what cost.

The first four options below address serving data centers through the grid. The final two consider generation at or near the data center, with an optional grid connection. These categories overlap; on-site generation is a delivery strategy, not a generation technology, and can use multiple technologies.

Flexible demand. Duke University’s Nicholas Institute estimates the grid could absorb 76 GW of new load if that load accepted curtailment averaging 0.25% of annual consumption, in events of about 1.7 hours. It is the fastest option because it needs no new generation. It makes better use of capacity already on the grid.

But that capacity is finite, and beyond it, further load growth requires new supply. There is also a practical hurdle; to date, many data centers have shown little willingness to curtail AI workloads, because the value of computing far exceeds the cost of power.

Existing nuclear plants. Increasing output at operating plants through uprates and restarting closed plants can add reliable electricity supply, while extending operating licenses keeps existing plants running. A U.S. Department of Energy initiative targets 5 GW of added capacity at existing nuclear plants by 2029, and DOE has closed a $1.9 billion loan to restart Iowa’s Duane Arnold plant, returning 615 MW to service.

The limit is opportunity: Few operating plants remain candidates for significant uprates, and restarts are underway at nearly every plant where one is practical.

Natural gas. Gas offers firm power and a familiar path, but gas turbine supply has become the constraint. GE Vernova’s gas turbine backlog and slot reservations reached 116 GW at midyear 2026, and the company has said a heavy-duty turbine ordered today will not arrive until 2031. Buyers also take on fuel-price exposure and decades of emissions.

Wind, solar and storage. These resources can be deployed quickly and at scale. Their limit is that data centers require dependable power around the clock, so matching that requirement takes substantial overbuilding, energy storage and transmission infrastructure to deliver the same capacity as a firm resource.

On-site generation. Building a power plant at a data center can bring electricity online sooner by avoiding some grid upgrades and connection delays, where permitted.

However, it does not speed up equipment deliveries. An on-site gas turbine plant faces the same turbine shortages as one supplying the grid. Reliable operation also requires sufficient on-site backup capability or access to grid power during outages and maintenance. If a project relies on the grid, developers need clear terms defining that access and its cost.

Advanced nuclear energy. Advanced reactors offer firm, clean power and can be built as a fleet. They can serve data centers directly, with an optional grid connection. They will not close the gap this decade, and their real test is cost at fleet scale.

No single option is fast, firm and large enough on its own. Three principles follow.

Flexible demand, uprates at existing nuclear plants, and new wind, solar and storage can help meet near-term electricity needs.

Natural gas and on-site generation can help fill the gap while longer-term resources are developed, but only if equipment deliveries, permits and grid interconnection align with the data center’s schedule.

Advanced nuclear energy can contribute in the 2030s and beyond.

Utilities must plan now for both natural gas and advanced nuclear energy. DOE’s long-term modeling indicates 550 to 770 GW of additional firm, clean generating capacity could be needed by 2050, with advanced nuclear energy providing about 200 GW.

Infrastructure built to meet an immediate shortfall should also have a useful role later. A gas plant could provide firming capacity or operate during periods of peak demand as longer-term supply becomes available.

Make flexibility the price of speed. Utilities can offer earlier interconnection to customers accepting curtailment under defined, metered and enforceable terms. For data center operators, that is a choice between some power sooner and firm power later. Customers that can curtail load may get power sooner; those that cannot must wait for firm supply. This converts flexibility from an assumption into a contract and rewards developers whose flexibility is real.

Secure the long-lead items for firm, clean power now. Buyers who reserved gas turbine production slots early secured earlier deliveries; those ordering now may have to wait until 2031.

Firm, clean power has its own long-lead items, including fuel supply and equipment manufacturing capacity. Utilities and data center operators can sign reservation or offtake agreements that give suppliers the demand signal to build capacity before it is needed.

These principles also address the forecast debate. Some investments — flexibility, uprates and transmission — remain valuable across demand scenarios. Others are options worth buying, such as reservations for firm supply in the 2030s and beyond.

And some are bets to avoid: irreversible commitments sized to a demand number that may not arrive. If forecasts prove too high, the cost is the price paid to preserve an option. If they prove right and no one bought the option, the cost is the kind of shortfall PJM has already seen.

Planning for a shortfall is not pessimism. Of all the inputs AI needs, reliable power may prove the scarcest. The race to scale AI is a race to deliver power. Utilities, regulators and developers need tools that arrive on time, hold their value if forecasts change, and keep electricity reliable and affordable for existing customers.

#5775. New York state is short electric resources beginning 2033: ISO

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

New York will face an approximately 225 MW statewide resource deficiency, with the need for power resources growing to 1,850 MW by 2036, the state’s grid operator said Wednesday.

New York ISO’s 2026 Reliability Needs Assessment reveals the state’s increasing reliance on emergency operating procedures to maintain grid stability, highlighting the imminent resource shortfall. The ISO said it expects to call on demand response, voltage reductions, operating reserves, load curtailment and other measures, approximately 3.5 days next year, with that total growing to 21 days by 2036.

“New York’s reliability needs are no longer a distant concern,” Zach Smith, senior vice president of system and resource planning, said in a statement. “New generation and transmission resources take years to plan, permit, and build, so the reliability needs identified in this report must be addressed today.”

Along with the statewide resource deficiency, the report also identified local reliability needs. New York City is projected to be deficient by approximately 400 MW in 2032, growing to approximately 1,850 MW by 2036, while the Lower Hudson Valley deficiency grows from approximately 130 MW in 2032 to approximately 1,950 MW in 2036.

“While transmission investments can help address local reliability needs ... they cannot resolve the statewide resource shortfall,” the ISO said. “A combination of new generation, repowered resources, energy storage, dependable demand reductions, flexible large loads, and additional transmission will be required to maintain reliability over the next decade.”

NYISO expects demand growth of about 1,000 MW from manufacturing facilities and 1,700 MW from data centers will contribute to the shortfall. “The age and availability” of the state’s generation fleet is also a growing reliability concern, the grid operator said.

Approximately 4,600 MW of fossil generation will be more than 70 years old by 2036, according to the report. The assessment “accounts for this risk by projecting approximately 2,600 MW of generation as unavailable by 2036,” it said.

The ISO said it will continue to monitor the situation quarterly, reporting on the issue through its short-term reliability processes.

Once the 2026 RNA is approved by the NYISO board of directors, the grid operator said it will request updates to the base case “that may reduce or eliminate the identified reliability needs,” including changes in the status of generation and transmission projects, demand forecasts, demand response resources, large load assumptions and other system developments.

“If reliability needs remain after those updates, the NYISO will solicit and evaluate market-based solutions, regulated backstop solutions, and alternative regulated solutions” through the development of a 10-year reliability plan, the operator said.

#5771. The Return Of Project Hail Mary … And Next Steps For Artemis, Too

Word has just dropped that the mega-hit sci-fi film “Project Hail Mary” has returned to the big screen for the holiday season with its nail-biting tale of science, adventure and friendship. Catch a brand new Hail Mary trailer on YouTube while the action heats up here on Earth, including solicitations for the next class of astronauts, the formation of the new US Space Academy, and next steps for the Artemis mission to the Moon.

On November 7, NASA (the National Aeronautics and Space Administration) began taking applications for the next class of astronauts. The deadline is 11:59 PM ET on December 3.

NASA received more than 8,000 applications during the last round of astronaut recruitment, in 2024. Only 10 candidates were selected — but don’t be discouraged. Just look at the payoff as described by NASA:

The next generation of astronauts will train to operate on the lunar surface, live and work beyond Earth, and take on missions that will push the boundaries of human exploration farther than ever before.

“The next generation of astronaut candidates could help write history while inspiring future scientists, engineers, and explorers,” adds Scott Tingle, chief astronaut and chair of the NASA Astronaut Selection Board. Interested? Apply here.

If you blinked, you missed the formation of the new US Space Academy. However, the folks over at the Louisiana Economic Development agency were on the ball. On November 12, LED announced that Louisiana has thrown its hat into the ring for the privilege of hosting the the forthcoming Space Academy.

“The Space Academy is a proposed NASA-supported federal academy that will educate the next generation of astronauts, engineers, scientists, and leaders for America’s space programs,” LED explains.

If all goes according to plan, the first Space Academy class will occupy Louisiana State University in New Orleans with a roster of 300 students in the fall of 2028, with room for a total of 4,000 — or more — to follow.

If you’re wondering why Louisiana thinks it has a good shot at hosting such an endeavor, Louisiana Governor Jeff Landry summarized the case in a press statement. “The United States Space Academy belongs in a state that builds the rockets, supports our nation’s military and is now home to the world’s largest spaceport,” Landry enthused.

As described in an LED press release, the state’s qualifications include hosting “America’s Rocket Factory,” aka the Michoud Assembly Facility in New Orleans. The facility is owned by NASA and managed by the Marshall Space Flight Center, which among other projects has just released a new album of “baby star sounds.”

The idea of a dedicated center for space education and training bubbled up from the White House just a few months ago, in August. NASA promptly jumped on it, inviting the governors of all 50 states to compete for the new facility.

Louisiana is determined to beat them all to the punch. With LED in the lead, collaborators on the state’s Space Academy proposal include Louisiana State University, the City of New Orleans, the business organization Greater New Orleans, Inc., and the New Orleans subsidiary of the leading utility Entergy among others, with all of the state’s US Senators and Representatives enlisted in support.

“A dedicated state fund, the Louisiana U.S. Space Academy Fund, will cover startup needs and independent operating infrastructure, lowering the federal investment needed to launch and sustain the Academy,” LED adds, noting that two other schools — Southern University at New Orleans and Tulane University — are also lending their support to the LSU campus.

LED and its partners are leaning heavily on the presence of the Michoud Assembly Facility to leverage its claim to host the Space Academy. As LED notes, Michoud has been front and center in crewed space flight for 65 years, and it sits only about nine miles from the LSU campus

“Michoud built the Saturn V first stages for Apollo, every Space Shuttle external tank, and the core stages for the Space Launch System rockets that power NASA’s Artemis missions,” LED explains.

“The National Center for Advanced Manufacturing at Michoud develops the composite and metallic manufacturing processes behind that hardware,” the agency adds.

LED Secretary Susan B. Bourgeois also chipped in her two cents. “Louisiana is building one of the most consequential aerospace ecosystems in the country, and the U.S. Space Academy would give us the opportunity to build the talent pipeline alongside it,” Bourgeois said.

“Cadets would learn alongside leaders in government and commercial and space, including NASA’s Michoud Assembly Center and Stennis Space Center, SpaceX Starbase Louisiana, Michoud tenants including Boeing, Lockheed Martin, Textron Systems, and Vivace,” LED elaborates, also taking note of the presence of the Naval Information Warfare Center among other resources.

“Greater New Orleans anchors the middle of America’s southern space corridor, which runs from Kennedy Space Center at Cape Canaveral to Johnson Space Center in Houston and SpaceX’s Starbase in South Texas, with Marshall Space Flight Center in Huntsville to the north,” LED adds again for good measure.

As for the Artemis mission, as of October NASA seemed pretty optimistic about the chances for sending people to the Moon sooner rather than later. A new schedule calls for an Artemis III test flight in Earth orbit next year. The flight will include shakeouts of docking and other systems between NASA’s Orion spacecraft and lunar landers from both from SpaceX and Blue Origin.

“Four astronauts will fly aboard Orion, launched on NASA’s SLS (Space Launch System) rocket, for a planned 14-day mission in Earth orbit to reduce risk ahead of the Artemis IV moon landing mission,” NASA explained.

“The Artemis III mission will unfold in sequence,” NASA explained. “Blue Origin will launch a Blue Moon lander test article into Earth orbit, SLS will launch Orion and its crew, docking with the Blue Moon lander test article, then SpaceX will launch a Version 3 Starship lander test article while Orion will undock from Blue Moon and dock with Starship.”

Assuming that all goes off without a hitch, 2028 will see US astronauts on walking on the Moon, presumably before anybody else gets there.

The last time US citizens set foot on the Moon, millions of Terrestrials witnessed the fuzzy, flickering action on live TV. If you were among them, share the experience in the discussion thread What was it like? Did everyone cheer? Or did somebody say something random such as “I need a cigarette,” and is that the moment that burns especially bright in your memory? Asking for a friend…

#5770. Fuel constraints could challenge grid this winter, despite resource improvements: NERC

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Some areas in the Midwest, Southeast and New England could face resource shortages if transmission and pipeline constraints occur during periods of extreme weather, the reliability watchdog said.

Electric demand is growing, but “there has also been strong growth in resources,” Mark Olson, manager of reliability assessments, said Wednesday in a call discussing the outlook. “In many ways, the industry is on better footing going into winter this year,” he said.

However, Olson added that “historically, winter is the toughest operating period for the North American electric grid.”

While some models show the El Niño weather pattern can bring milder-than-average conditions that are a help to a constrained power system, “the reality is that extreme storms with ice and abnormal cold temperatures can lie within those averages,” Olson said.

“We're talking to our industry stakeholders about the need for extra vigilance. El Niño is a variable that adds to uncertainty for the winter,” he said.

Peak winter electric heating demand often coincides with higher residential gas usage, potentially leaving single-fuel generators that don’t have long-term gas contracts unable to show up when their generation is most needed, NERC officials said.

In the Northeast Power Coordinating Council’s Québec and Maritimes regions, “the supply can become strained during extreme winter peak demand,” NERC Senior Engineer Bob Tallman said on the call. “New England, as we've identified in the past several years, is assessed as elevated [risk] due to the constrained or limited gas pipeline infrastructure in that region.”

In the SERC Reliability Corp. East area, resources can fall short of extreme peak demand “during morning and low solar output periods of the day,” Tallman added.  And in the Southwest Power Pool and Midcontinent Independent System Operator territories, NERC identified subregions “where there are localized transmission constraints and it could be difficult to import into those areas if there was a wide-area winter event limiting local generation and increasing their reliance on imports.”

In North America, data centers are boosting the long-term investment case for energy storage and renewables as gas turbine backlogs and rising fuel volatility drive up peaking costs, according to a note from Wood Mackenzie.

#5768. SolarEdge unveils U.S.-made optimizer for C&I solar

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 17, 2026

SolarEdge’s new Duo Power Optimizer promises to simplify commercial and industrial solar installations with 50% fewer optimizer units, independent module-level power tracking, and enhanced safety features.

The commercial and industrial (C&I) solar market is getting a new hardware option aimed at making installations simpler without sacrificing system performance. SolarEdge Technologies has unveiled its new Duo Power Optimizer, a U.S.-manufactured solution designed to deliver module-level energy harvesting and monitoring while cutting the number of power optimizers in half.

Announced November 16 ahead of RE+ 2026 in Las Vegas, the new product introduces a 2:1 architecture that allows one optimizer to connect two solar modules. Notably, each module retains its own independent maximum power point tracker (MPPT), which preserves the module-level performance benefits associated with the traditional one-to-one optimizer configurations.

For developers, EPCs and installers managing large-scale rooftop and distributed generation projects, the value here lies in fewer components to purchase, wire and install, with no trade-off in module-level optimization.

Simplifying installation without sacrificing performance

SolarEdge’s optimization tech addresses the challenge of conventional string inverter systems, which is that an underperforming module can affect the output of an entire string. But by allowing each module to operate independently, the system limits the impact of shading, degradation or performance differences between panels.

The Duo brings demonstrates that capability in a two-module configuration. By reducing the number of optimizers by 50% compared with one-to-one designs, SolarEdge is targeting an opportunity to streamline balance-of-system requirements and reduce the installation labor.

SolarEdge’s U.S.-made Duo Power Optimizer connects two solar modules while maintaining independent module-level power tracking for commercial and industrial solar installations. Credit: SolarEdge

The hardware also incorporates design changes intended to improve jobsite efficiency. Each optimizer features two input pairs: a shorter connection for the nearest module and a pre-extended connection for the adjacent module. The arrangement reduces excess wiring and eliminates the need for field-made jumpers.

The configuration also accommodates modules with different orientations, making it particularly relevant for east-west and dual-tilt racking applications.

Beyond installation efficiency, SolarEdge is emphasizing electrical safety as a central feature of the new product.

The Duo extends the company’s Sense Connect technology to its input and output connectors, monitoring connector temperatures to identify developing issues before they potentially escalate into overheating or electrical arcs.

Also, the optimizer introduces what SolarEdge describes as an industry-first approach to arc fault detection: every optimizer acts as an individual arc sensor. Rather than relying exclusively on detection at the inverter, the system distributes monitoring throughout the array, allowing potential arc faults to be identified closer to their source.

SolarEdge says the technology provides coverage across strings with up to 3,280 feet of total cable length between the inverter and optimizer. It can also help pinpoint problems to the optimizer area, potentially making troubleshooting and maintenance more efficient.

Manufactured at SolarEdge’s Florida facility, the Duo is designed to meet U.S. Domestic Content and Foreign Entity of Concern (FEOC) requirements, according to the company. SolarEdge says its domestic-content eligibility levels are consistent with those of its previous U.S.-manufactured power optimizers, subject to applicable tax guidance and project-specific requirements.

For SolarEdge, the Duo represents an effort to combine three priorities that are becoming more and more important in commercial solar: installation efficiency, system-level safety, and domestic manufacturing.

The technology does not fundamentally change the value proposition of module-level optimization. Instead, it looks to make that architecture more economical and easier to deploy at scale.

SolarEdge will showcase the Duo Power Optimizer at RE+ 2026.
