#5766. Last Chance to Get It Right: The Industrial Accelerator Act Will Make or Break Europe’s Battery Industry

How the Franco-German auto deal, a wide Made-with-EU scope, and carmakers abandon Europe’s battery value chain to foreign competitors.

There is a growing consensus that Europe must reduce its reliance on Chinese cleantech, especially batteries

The IAA marks a major shift in EU industrial policy. It introduces Union content incentives to ensure scarce public subsidies and corporate tax incentives are used to support European manufacturing. This demand-side pull is crucial if the energy transition is to deliver high-value industrial jobs and lessen critical geopolitical dependencies. There is a growing consensus that Europe must reduce its reliance on Chinese cleantech, especially batteries. But several proposals currently before co-legislators risk severely undermining that overarching goal. Diluting battery criteria in the IAA means giving up on a strategic value chain for Europe, leaving it to China only.

T&E’s assessment shows that only an IAA with a calibrated timeline, robust battery requirements and a strict geographical scope will deliver substantial benefits for Europe. Without secured demand, announced industrial capacity will simply not materialise.

The Franco-German automotive deal from 16 October 2026 is a major blow for Europe’s battery industry as it severely dilutes incentives for batteries. It suggests battery sourcing requirements for eligible vehicles of 10% in 2028-2029, 20% in 2030-2031 and 70% from 2032 on. This is far below what the market already does: over 50% of cells in electric vehicles (EVs) sold in the EU are already Made-in-Europe. By 2030, the deal would only secure 13% (62 GWh) of Europe’s announced cell production: a further 206 GWh would likely find European buyers, though with no guarantee, while 220 GWh would find no demand at all. Even in 2032, the 70% would still not secure a substantial share of potential cell capacity (only 45%).

Another major risk is applying a wide Made-with-EU geographical scope to battery criteria. Compared to a strict EU-only scope, including free trade agreement (FTA) partners puts around 100 GWh of cell demand and 250 GWh of cathode active materials (CAM) demand at risk of leakage in 2030. Limiting the scope to trusted partners only helps to a limited extent. Since Europe can produce enough cells to meet demand under a Made-in-EU IAA, adding partner countries risks leaving EU production without offtakers: even with trusted partners only, a third of potential EU cell supply could be affected. For CAM, around 30% of expected EU supply could be left without a buyer under a trusted-partners scope, rising to 50% under an extended one.

The midstream, CAM and their precursors (pCAM), is the weakest link in Europe’s battery value chain. Boosting this key segment and diversifying away from China is essential for resilience as it shields European industry from supply disruptions and export controls. Under a strict Made-in-EU scope, reaching IAA CAM requirements is technically and commercially viable by 2030, requiring only limited sourcing from trusted partners (33 GWh cell-equivalent). From 2032, EU production can cover demand from EVs under support schemes. A Made-in-EU IAA would generate 323 GWh of extra EU CAM demand in 2032, around the output of six mid-sized EU CAM plants, bringing total EU-made CAM to 407 GWh (securing 94% of expected EU supply).

pCAM is just as vital for resilience and to establish local offtake for European refiners and recyclers. A 28% EU pCAM requirement in 2032 applied to a carmaker’s entire fleet sales (not only for vehicles eligible for public support) creates enough demand for expected EU supply to materialise and projects to reach Final Investment Decision (FID). Onshoring CAM and pCAM adds only a modest cost of $10.6/kWh for Nickel Manganese Cobalt (NMC) and $6.5/kWh for Lithium Iron Phosphate (LFP) batteries. This is a necessary sovereignty premium that will fall as the EU supply chain matures.

The car CO₂ standards are as critical to industrial delivery as the IAA. Even under a strict Made-in-EU scope, the European Parliament’s position as approved by the leading committee in November 2026 would sharply cut expected EV sales and reduce EU cell demand by 247 GWh between 2027 and 2032, which equals 3.7 million EU made EVs.

Finally, the proposed fleet booster mechanism fails to drive industrial onshoring and relocation if its compliance trigger is set below 85%. Carmakers’ efforts to lower that threshold aim at getting a stamp of approval on their sourcing strategy in China. A major risk for public subsidies ending up benefiting imported cars with Chinese batteries. Attempts to lower the Booster below a 85% threshold should be rejected, especially if combined with weaker battery criteria.

It’s key to reject the Franco-German deal proposal on batteries.

Battery criteria in this deal are too weak to create additional demand and undermine the IAA’s core purpose.

By Franziska Grüning, Raw Materials OfficerBrussels (EU), T&E.

Jordane Faizant, Analyst, FleetsBrussels (EU)

Jeanne Andre, Analyst, Raw MaterialsBrussels (EU)

Transport & Environment’s (T&E) vision is a zero-emission mobility system that is affordable and has minimal impacts on our health, climate and environment. Created over 30 years ago, we have shaped some of Europe’s most important environmental laws.

#5765. PJM urged to delay base capacity auction until data centers cut from forecast

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

State ratepayer advocates and the grid operator’s market monitor say holding the auction in December as planned threatens grid reliability and could hit consumers with billions in unnecessary costs.

The request from the ratepayer advocates comes amid nationwide concern about energy affordability and data centers, as reflected in the midterm elections. The letter says that given “the urgency of the approaching auction,” its signatories request a written response from PJM’s board no later than Nov. 23.

In PJM, affordability concerns have been driven by a recent spike in capacity prices caused by growing data center load forecasts. PJM’s current capacity market rules effectively prevent data center companies from paying for their own power supply and infrastructure costs.

PJM recognizes the financial impacts rising electricity bills place on households and businesses, Jeffrey Shields, a spokesman for the grid operator, told Utility Dive in an email.

“The December auction includes an established price cap, providing an important safeguard against how high prices can rise,” he said. “The system urgently needs capacity to meet rising demand. Postponing the auction this close to its execution could destabilize the market and increase the risk of power interruptions when customers need electricity most.”

Electricity demand is growing faster than new power supplies can be built, according to Shields.

“While decisions about whether data centers and other large electricity users can connect to the grid rest with policymakers and utilities, PJM must plan for the demand those decisions create.” Shields said. “That means moving forward with securing the power we need while continuing to advance solutions that address the challenges of growing demand and the pressure on electricity costs.”

So far, including existing and forecast data center demand in PJM’s capacity market has increased capacity costs by $29.4 billion for all customers, according to Monitoring Analytics’ Nov. 12 report that called for the auction to be postponed.

PJM has responded to pressure from states, federal regulators and consumers by proposing a two-part plan to meet data center demand by facilitating bilateral contracts outside its normal capacity auctions, and also holding a backstop procurement for large loads that do not have bilateral contracts. It is also pursuing reforms to its rules for interconnecting large loads and generating supplies. The status of these efforts is pending.

The market monitor’s concerns were amplified by Exelon’s Nov. 3 complaint at FERC that challenged PJM’s three-year forward auction, partly over the uncertainty in forecasting data center load and the resulting billions in potential excess capacity costs, the ratepayer advocates said.

The advocates noted that PJM plans to publish the final “planning parameters” for the upcoming auction, which may remove canceled or delayed data center projects from the auction’s demand curve, on Nov. 19.

However, the market monitor’s recommendation is not simply to update PJM’s load forecast, they added.

“It is to fundamentally change how forecasted data-center demand is treated in the capacity auction so that existing consumers are not required to subsidize speculative or uncommitted data center demand,” the advocates said. “The delay would allow PJM to implement the IMM recommendations, subject to FERC approval and appropriate reliability safeguards.”

A “statement of principles” endorsed in January by PJM and the governors of its 13 states emphasized that large-load forecasts must be “real and verified,” the ratepayer advocates said. “Proceeding with an auction that incorporates both real and speculative data-center demand, despite the [market monitor’s] findings, would undermine that commitment and expose consumers to billions in potentially avoidable costs.”

The letter was sent to PJM by the Office of the Ohio Consumers’ Counsel, the Delaware Public Advocate, the West Virginia Consumer Advocate, the Maryland Office of People’s Counsel, the Pennsylvania Office of Consumer Advocate, the New Jersey Division of Rate Counsel, the Illinois Attorney General’s Office and the Illinois Citizens Utility Board.

#5762. Grid operators need data center ride-through rules that protect reliability

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The first facilities built to Texas’ new standard are about to come online, proving that firm, predictable regulation can protect reliability without slowing development, says ON.energy’s Alan Cooper.

Alan Cooper is CEO of ON.energy, a developer of power infrastructure.

On July 22, a mechanical failure forced a 230-kV transmission line in Northern Virginia offline. Within milliseconds, grid protection systems cleared the fault, as they are designed to do. The event would not have been a particularly noteworthy story, had it stopped there. It’s what happened next, though, that pointed to a growing challenge for grid reliability.

In response to the fault, data centers that had been drawing nearly 4 GW of energy, close to 4% of PJM’s load, collectively disconnected and switched to their on-site backup generation. The move sent voltage and frequency spiking across the system, a technically wonky but threatening result that caused power flickers as far away as Chicago. Thankfully, the prevailing grid conditions were strong enough to absorb the shock of the fault, so the grid held. But if the same event had hit Texas or even Northern Virginia at a time of higher demand, it could have caused a widespread outage. In West Texas, the Electric Reliability Counil of Texas estimates that a loss of as little as 3.2 GW could threaten grid reliability.

The power sector is not unfamiliar with the need for things hooked up to the electricity grid to “ride through” disturbance events. In 2023, the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission told the North American Electricity Reliability Corp. to develop requirements that inverter-based supply resources like wind and solar facilities must follow to ensure they “ride through,” i.e., stay online, during certain disturbances. And this time around, NERC, FERC and several grid regions have been quick to identify a similar need for large loads to adhere to analogous standards.

In an Oct. 14 presentation to ERCOT, Dominion Virginia confirmed that the disturbance fell within the ride-through parameters that have now been finalized or proposed by NERC and two grid operators (ERCOT and PJM Interconnection). They concluded that if those requirements had been in place, the event would have been avoided.

Clear ride-through standards protect the grid, and they protect data centers’ ability to keep connecting to it. The open question for grid operators and regulators isn’t whether to create them, but rather which facilities the rules should cover. Some proposals would exempt projects that aren’t likely to draw grid power for years. Texas took a firmer line and in doing so, gave other operators and regulators an effective blueprint to follow.

The Public Utility Commission of Texas approved ERCOT’s ride through rule and it took effect in August. ERCOT made three choices that are key to making it work.

It drew the applicability line at energization. Facilities that energize roughly 18 months or more after ERCOT’s first market notice must comply with the rule. That exempts projects that are nearly finished, with equipment already ordered or even installed, and it applies to projects that exist only on paper, which can still change their electrical design for a small share of what a retrofit would cost later.

It measured the risk in exempt capacity within a cluster. ERCOT studies ride-through risk in its quarterly stability assessments. Those studies identify the clusters of large load that could trip together and the rule limits the amount of exempted load — i.e., the amount that is not equipped to ride through disturbances within each cluster — to 3,200 MW per single fault. Its latest results flag North Texas and Central Texas as active risk areas, with far West Texas being monitored.

It made the risk transparent and predictable. ERCOT watches each load cluster’s combined consumption in real time. When a cluster’s actual demand reaches about 2,500 MW, a mitigation plan kicks in: each utility and scheduling entity gets a “not to exceed” limit based on its share of recent peak use, and ERCOT keeps the authority to curtail non-compliant load. Because ERCOT publishes the study results and updates them over time, developers can see how much headroom is left long before they hit the limit. This transparency gives them time to decide whether to install ride-through capability, change their plans, or accept the risk of curtailment.

The result is that existing data centers keep running, and projects under construction generally avoid a forced redesign or need to buy different equipment. Other grid users stay protected. The first facilities built to the new standard will connect in the coming months.

In general, regulators and regional grid operators across the country are right to be careful about borrowing from ERCOT. Its isolated grid and energy-only market make much of its experience hard to apply elsewhere.

This isn’t one of those cases. A data center’s response to a voltage dip is the same in Virginia, Ohio or Texas, and so is the physics of thousands of megawatts disconnecting at once. NERC, PJM and other grid operators should adopt the core tenants of the Texas approach:

A single large-scale event would change the conversation overnight. Instead of debating exemption criteria, policymakers would be questioning if data centers can remain connected. Clear standards now are the best way to ensure the industry has a path forward.

The impact of a major event wouldn’t stop at the fence line: a multistate outage would affect the communities where data centers are concentrated and working to become good grid citizens. Proposed exceptions to ride-through requirements leave the door open for exactly the kind of event that consumers and policymakers are concerned about.

None of this protection is theoretical or depends on a technology breakthrough. Equipment to keep data centers connected through grid faults is commercially available today and already running in the field. The first facilities built to Texas’s new standard are about to come online, proving that a firm, predictable regulation can protect reliability without slowing market development. All too often, reliability problems are left lingering until there’s an emergency.

Large load ride through concerns can be solved now. The only thing missing is clear, consistent rules that protect grid reliability while providing certainty to the industry.

#5761. MG U9 EV Ute — It’s A Monster Truck

We visit the MG dealership and get to know the MG U9 EV ute up close and personal.

As I have said many times to CleanTechnica readers, Australia needs more BEV utes. The PHEV utes are taking a bite out of diesel sales, but we need some monster truck battery electrics to push electrification deep into the heart of the bush-bashing Aussie tradie. I think that MG may have achieved that with the launch of the MG U9 EV. Yesterday, Majella and I went to have a look. Even Russell Coight would be impressed and the Mitsubishi Outlander PHEV should be feeling threatened. Take a break and watch the funny video. I doubt it will affect the sales of the Shark though.

So, yesterday, Majella I set out to have an adventure visiting the various car yards near our home. We were actually planning to look at some new electric vans, but ended up in the MG showroom with a very knowledgeable sales rep exploring the MG U9 EV. What a surprise! An early morning google search assured me that there were none in Brisbane available to view. This dealership not only brought one out from the pre-delivery area for us to check out, but also told us they had already sold and delivered their first one and had six more on order.

The MG U9 EV (it also comes in diesel) is joining the thin ranks of BEV utes already available in Australia — the KGM Musso and the Toyota Hilux BEV. So far this year, 269 KGM Mussos have sold in Australia. 174 MG U9 EVs are listed as delivered, possibly mainly to dealers. The cost of the MG U9 EV is approximately AU$80,000, just a few thousand more than the Toyota Hilux BEV. The diesel version of the MG sells for around AU$50,000.

The sales rep told me about his first customer for the MG ute: “He was keen. He already had home solar and a battery and was producing far more electricity than he could use. His solution: trade in the old diesel ute on a U9 EV ‘rolling battery.’ It will complement his Kia EV6. With the 6.6 kWh of the U9 vehicle-to-load [capability], he plans to power his house.” The ecosystem expands. For those with a query, it is not worth selling power back to the grid.

The MG U9 has a 102.2 kWh LFP battery, big enough to give a range of up to 430 km and send this behemoth hurtling down the road — accelerating to 100 km an hour from zero in 5.8 seconds. That’s as fast as my new Tesla! And an advertised top speed of 190 km/hour. Just as well it has disc brakes all round — with the front ones ventilated. And to recharge — 42 minutes from 20–80%.

Tare weight comes in at 2815 kg, towing capacity is 3500 kg, and it has the capacity to carry 685 kg in the tray. Unladen ground clearance is listed as 230 mm, and it has a wading depth of 550 mm. Length is 5500 mm; width 2265 mm; height 1874 mm. Very similar dimensions to the GMC Hummer with half the battery capacity. Hummer: L 5507 mm; W 2201 mm; H 2055 mm.

The MG U9 BEV includes a fold-down rear bulkhead to extend cargo-bed length inside the cabin, plus a 1,200 L tub capacity and a 236 L front trunk (frunk).

Google tells me: “The MG U9 EV Dual-Cab Ute comes equipped from the factory with a four-wheel electronic air suspension system offering over 100 mm of vertical height adjustment (including an Off-Road Lift mode, and automatic lowering by -20 mm at 80 km/h and -40 mm at 120 km/h.”

Of course there is a lot more to know, especially about tech and safety features. I am just whetting your appetite. You can find out more here. Or watch a video review on YouTube here.

At the moment, the dealership is fielding 8 enquiries a week online, but it is expecting some headwinds in mid 2027 when the Australian federal government is planning to review Fringe Benefit Tax benefits for electric vehicles. My comment was that somehow car manufacturers seem to find ways of reducing costs when government support is modified.

While I peppered the sales rep with questions, Majella did her usual photographic exploration and analysis.

She was impressed by the massive frunk, as was I — you could fit a whole body in there! The frunk can be opened from the outside by a physical button.

… and it has a power point for your fridge or coffeemaker!

The ute features doors with physical buttons and handles for ease of access.

The gear lever reminded Majella of the ones we see in the cockpit of a plane.

A variety of charging options for your tech are provided: cigarette lighter, USB charger, and more.

Perhaps MG went overboard with buttons — lots of physical buttons on the dash and on the steering wheel, plus the screen. Majella thought that this was a comfortable wheel size and shape for a long drive.

Behind the back seats in a cubby hole we have the charge cords: a trickle charger and type 2 to type 2.

The back tailgate has a discreet button to push to fold it down so you can load the tray.

An emergency glass breaker and a seat belt cutter is provided.

Although MG appears to have thought of everything, Majella commented that the only thing they don’t supply is a first aid kit.

The air suspension is easily adjusted air suspension from the centre screen. The sales rep confided that the staff were playing with it the day before.

The 360 degree camera extends to the trailer, with an aerial view for easily reversing.

Those looking for a truck to haul stuff, power their tools, and store excess energy would do well to consider the MG U9 EV. It may not be a Hummer, but it’s close. For tradies and bush bashers, the future can now be bright and electric.

David Waterworth is a retired teacher who divides his time between looking after his grandchildren and trying to make sure they have a planet to live on. He is long on Tesla [NASDAQ:TSLA].

#5759. Mission Solar Energy puts supply chain transparency in the spotlight at RE+ 2026

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 18, 2026

With new high-efficiency residential and commercial modules and an integrated supply chain spanning polysilicon to power conversion, Mission Solar Energy is making a case for U.S. manufacturing, domestic content and FEOC-compliant sourcing.

As the solar industry continues to navigate shifting trade policies, domestic content incentives, and supply chain scrutiny, Mission Solar Energy is bringing a message of manufacturing strength and transparency to RE+ 2026. The San Antonio-based manufacturer showcased its latest commercial and residential modules while highlighting the advantages of its relationship with fellow OCI Holdings subsidiaries OCI TerraSus and OCI Power.

The announcement puts the spotlight on a growing priority for solar developers, installers and distributors: knowing exactly where components originate and having the documentation to prove it.

The three companies collectively cover significant portions of the solar value chain. OCI TerraSus produces high-purity, solar-grade polysilicon in Sarawak, Malaysia; Mission Solar designs, engineers, and assembles modules in San Antonio, Texas; and OCI Power manufactures inverters and power conversion systems in South Korea.

According to Mission Solar, all three companies are compliant with Foreign Entity of Concern (FEOC) requirements and are not classified as prohibited foreign entities. The integrated structure offers customers access to a traceable supply chain backed by OCI Holdings, a global clean energy group headquartered in Seoul.

For an industry that’s becoming more and more focused on sourcing eligibility and domestic manufacturing credentials, that positioning could prove commercially important.

On the product front, Mission Solar is highlighting two modules aimed at different market segments.

For commercial and industrial (C&I) applications, the company is featuring its 595W N-Type Bifacial 144HC module. The dual-glass design captures sunlight from both sides, potentially increasing energy production without requiring additional project footprint. Designed and assembled in the United States, the module carries 11.5% domestic content, meets FEOC requirements and supports front-side loads of up to 5,400 Pa.

Residential installers will get a look at the 440W N-Type 108HC rooftop module, which Mission Solar says will be the first of its kind on the market with 12% domestic content. Building on an existing 6% domestic-content version, the module combines N-type cell technology and a multi-busbar design to deliver efficiency of up to 23%. It is also approved on residential financing platforms including LightReach and GoodLeap.

Looking further ahead, Mission Solar plans to preview two next-generation G12R modules, with commercial launches anticipated in late 2027. The company will also display a lower-domestic-content U.S.-produced C&I module option expected in 2027.

Although module performance is still critical to the industry, supply chain credibility is increasingly part of the overall purchasing decision.

As developers evaluate project economics, tax credit eligibility and sourcing risk, manufacturers that can offer transparent documentation alongside competitive technology may have an advantage.

#5757. Google signs 50-MW superhot geothermal PPA with Quaise Energy

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The agreement will support Google’s Oregon data center operations beginning as early as 2030, advancing development of a technology designed to access geothermal resources deeper underground.

Google's agreement with Quaise gives superhot geothermal an important commercial partner as developers seek to move enhanced geothermal systems from demonstration projects to utility-scale deployment.

“Geothermal energy remains largely underutilized. It supplies less than 0.5% of global electricity and is limited to just a handful of states in the U.S.,” Diane Hughes, senior vice president of marketing and communications at Quaise, said in an email.

Unlike conventional geothermal plants, which depend on naturally occurring underground hot-water reservoirs that are geographically constrained and rarely exceed 400 degrees Fahrenheit, enhanced geothermal uses engineered reservoirs to access heat in deeper rock formations.

Quaise is developing millimeter-wave drilling designed to ablate rock without mechanical contact. The company is designing wells to reach depths of up to 65,000 feet, where rock temperatures can exceed 932 degrees Fahrenheit and conventional drill bits cannot withstand the heat, according to Hughes.

“Superhot wells like these can produce up to 10x more power per well than what's currently in play and bring geothermal to places where it isn't viable or economical today," she said.

The agreement comes as technology companies increasingly seek firm power sources to meet data center demand. Google has signed nearly 35 GW of clean energy purchase agreements globally since 2010, including previous investments in next-generation geothermal, according to its most recent environmental report. Google says its three Oregon data center campuses are sourced with 83% carbon-free energy.

Google is also pursuing other emerging technologies, including advanced nuclear power and long-duration energy storage, as part of its efforts to achieve net-zero emissions.

Quaise said in its 2025 year-end update that it had drilled 118 meters through granite at its central Texas test site and is now working toward a depth of 1 kilometer.

#5756. US to install 36% more wind turbines in 2025 than previous year

The United States is expected to add more than 7 GW of wind installations in 2025, a 36% increase over the previous year, and the five-year outlook remains unchanged quarter-over-quarter from previous forecast, according to the “U.S. Wind Energy Monitor” report released by Wood Mackenzie and the American Clean Power Association (ACP).

Amid a challenging market environment, the report shows that the United States is on track to add 46 GW of new wind capacity from 2025 to 2029, with total projected volumes unchanged quarter-on-quarter from previous forecasts. However, timing has shifted, as 2026 and 2027 will deliver significant gains, at 10.7 GW and 12.7 GW, respectively, as more assets advance through the development pipeline.

While Q3 installations came in 23% below forecast at 932 MW, the market has shown progress with 3.8 GW queued for Q4 2025 — representing 52% of the year’s total expected capacity. This back-loaded installation pattern is consistent with typical project-commissioning timelines.

U.S. turbine order intake has rebounded to pre-One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBBA) levels quarter-over-quarter, supported by 2+ GW of firm commitments in Q3, the biggest intake in the region in the last nine months and a 79% quarter-over-quarter increase. However, true visibility remains limited as OEMs increasingly withhold project details and much qualifying “start-of-construction” activity occurs through off-site component manufacturing.

The market will then see a noticeable drop off, as 2029 weakens meaningfully quarter-over-quarter following project cancellations and inactive designations for late-decade capacity, driven by permitting and broader development challenges.

“The U.S. power market is facing mounting strain after a decade of flat demand, with utilities committing to 160 GW of large-load additions. This represents a significant opportunity for wind energy, which benefits from strengthened economic fundamentals and a compelling business case driven by its competitively low LCOE,” said Leila Garcia da Fonseca, director of research at Wood Mackenzie. “However, turbine costs remain elevated due to tariffs and mid-term wind growth will depend on resolving permitting and policy uncertainty.”

Power demand growth through 2029 is expected to average around 3% compared to just 0.7% over the previous decade, with data centers accounting for approximately 59 GW of the 90 GW total peak demand growth. This surge in baseload demand positions wind as a natural fit to meet rising power needs.

The five-year capacity outlook remains unchanged at 39.8 GW of added capacity quarter-over-quarter. The 2025-2027 pipeline is fully committed with all projects having turbine orders in place. More than 60% of the three-year capacity outlook has been commissioned or is under construction.

Activity is led by western states, such as Wyoming, New Mexico and others, that will account for 34% of activity in this time period. Major projects driving the outlook include Pattern’s 3.5-GW SunZia project in New Mexico, which will position the developer as 2026’s top installer, and Invenergy’s 998-MW Towner Energy Center in Colorado, the largest single project expected in 2027.

The market continues expanding geographically, with Arkansas bringing its first utility-scale onshore wind project online through Cordelio’s Crossover Wind.

The repowering market remains strong, as Wood Mackenzie projects that 18 projects will drive 2.5 GW of capacity additions in the next three years.

Wood Mackenzie expects the offshore installation pace to slow in Q4 2025 due to harsh winter weather conditions, pushing remaining capacity into 2026. Despite these near-term adjustments, Vineyard Wind has demonstrated strong execution, connecting 15 turbines in Q3 and delivering 200 GWh through nine months.

“U.S. offshore wind shows diverging momentum,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Projects under construction with COD estimated for 2026 continue to hit key milestones, but post-2027 developments face potential delays amid constrained wind turbine installation vessel capacity, driving delays and contract terminations.”

The offshore sector is also experiencing significant financial pressure.

The report highlights that uncertainty around tariffs is a threat to projections. Wood Mackenzie projects that tariffs will drive up turbine costs in 2026, before moderating in subsequent years. In total, U.S. onshore wind capex is projected to increase by 5% through 2029.

“U.S. wind turbine pricing is experiencing unprecedented uncertainty as conflicting market and regulatory forces interact,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Domestic manufacturing overcapacity relative to permitted project volumes, particularly after 2028, would normally place downward pressure on prices. Despite this, onshore wind costs are expected to continue rising due to tariff exposure on raw material inputs and subcomponents.”

#5755. Texas finalizes ERCOT CEO contract extension, pay raise tied to performance

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

CEO Pablo Vegas could take home more than $4 million next year. The contract extension and pay raise were put on hold in September following public outcry.

The Public Utility Commission of Texas on Thursday filed an order finalizing a roughly 7% base pay raise and potential performance-based bonuses worth millions more for Pablo Vegas, the CEO of the Electric Reliability Council of Texas.

PUCT officials said the contract is “in line with professional peers nationwide.”

The contract was signed in September but put on hold following public outcry, including objections from Texas Lt. Gov. Dan Patrick, who said on social media, “during a time of increasing utility costs, this is not the time to give the CEO of ERCOT a multi-million-dollar pay raise.”

Neither Patrick nor Texas Gov. Greg Abbott responded to a request for comment on the contract’s finalization. Both lost elections in November and will leave office in January.

ERCOT filings with the IRS show Vegas earned a total of about $3.6 million in 2024 and 2025, with a base salary above $1 million for each year.

His new contract could see him earn $6.47 million this year, according to a September presentation to the ERCOT board, though only about $4 million would be paid in 2027 with some potentially due later based on long-term incentives. The new contract runs from 2027 through 2032 and includes base pay of $1.16 million, plus an annual short-term performance incentive of up to 100% of his base salary and an annual long-term incentive up to 180% of his base pay. There is also a $1.38 million “make-whole” payment due in 2027, related to agreements in his previous contract.

The performance incentives are set by the ERCOT board. The compensation agreement “ensures that pay follows performance, where incentive compensation is earned each year only by meeting key grid reliability and performance targets and unpaid incentives are forfeited if the CEO is terminated for cause or resigns without good reason,” PUCT Chairman Thomas Gleeson said in a statement. “This is not a guaranteed payout.”

Vegas has served as president and CEO of the Texas grid operator since August 2022.

#5754. $1199 — Anyland Rev Pro: An Electric Dirt Bike With a Wheelie Assist Built for Beginners

When I was a kid, I loved to ride. Riding a bike, for me, is synonymous with childhood, in fact. We used them to get ourselves places, but we also just used them to entertain ourselves. Inevitably, this led to popping wheelies, building ramps and trying jumps, and doing all sorts of other stuff my Mom just didn’t want to know about, no matter how much I wanted to brag. As an adult, I still love to ride — as the famous saying from Animal Farm goes, “four wheels good, two wheels mo’ bettah!” (I’m paraphrasing, a lot, but you get the point).

So when Anyland hit us up to do a review of their new Anyland Rev Pro, an all-electric dirt bike capable of 34 mph and assisted wheelies, the inner child in me jumped at the chance.

Wheelies look effortless until you try one. Anyone who has learned on a dirt bike knows the usual lesson plan: too little throttle and the front wheel never lifts, too much and you’re flat on your back. Anyland, a startup electric dirt bike brand, created the Rev Pro as an entry-level electric dirt bike with a controller that lifts the front wheel to a preset angle and holds it there, so a new rider can focus on balance instead of juggling throttle and brake.

The system, which Anyland sells as WELIXON and says it co-engineered with controller maker Moxin, offers three angle settings: 25°, 35°, and 45°. Riders start at the lowest angle and move up as their balance improves. According to Anyland, the controller delivers continuous, smooth power to keep the front end at the chosen angle, which is designed to reduce the risk of a “loop out,” where the bike flips backward, one of the most common ways wheelie learners get hurt. Once you’re ready, you can switch the assist off, and the Rev Pro behaves like a regular throttle-controlled electric dirt bike for practicing manual wheelies.

Underneath the trick, the Rev Pro is a lightweight, mid-drive electric dirt bike. Anyland lists:

The speed modes set the first speed caps as low as 12 mph, and matter for the audience Anyland is targeting: teenagers and adults who are new to dirt bikes. Starting in the lowest mode lets a rider build skills before moving up, and gives parents a way to keep a first-timer in a gentler setting.

The Rev Pro arrives in a compact box and includes all the tools you’ll need to set the bike up. My friend and I (two untrained professionals) did it in about 40 minutes:

In short, the ride is really fun. I found the suspension incredibly responsive and comfortable, the acceleration smooth and quick, and the riding position much more comfortable than I anticipated. It’s been raining non-stop where I live, so while I got this bike a few weeks ago in the mail, I haven’t had a chance to get it up onto the trails yet, so stay tuned for that. Testing it on the dirt sidewalks and running over curbs shows me it will likely be a great addition to my weekends.

A wheelie assist doesn’t make any dirt bike risk-free. Wear a helmet, gloves, and protective gear every time, and start in the lowest speed mode at the 25° setting. Practice using the rear brake, supervise younger riders, and stick to private land or designated off-road areas. Check your local rules before riding anywhere else, since many electric dirt bikes aren’t legal on public roads.

Anyland’s Black Friday and Cyber Monday price starts as low as $1,199, and prices have varied, so check the current price before you buy.

For a startup brand in a crowded field of lookalikes, the Rev Pro’s pitch is simple: a beginner-friendly bike built around one clever feature. If you’ve always wanted to learn a wheelie without the bruises, it’s worth a look.

#5753. Market reforms bolster grid, may lower costs this winter: FERC

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Improved coordination in the West will help offset lower hydropower output due to historically low water levels in the Colorado River basin, while New England risks overreliance on oil, FERC said.

Electric market reforms in the West and Northeast may bolster grid reliability and, in some cases, lower costs this winter, according to the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission’s annual winter market and reliability report, released Thursday.

In the West, the California Independent System Operator launched the Extended Day Ahead Market and the Southwest Power Pool expanded into the Western Interconnection earlier this year.

“EDAM and SPP’s expansion should provide ratepayers in much of the West with production cost savings and improved reliability in winter 2026-2027, particularly during cold weather events,” FERC staff said in the report.

This winter, the markets’ improved coordination of generation and reduced need for reserve power supplies will help offset lower power output from the Glen Canyon and Hoover dams due to historically low water levels in the Colorado River basin, staff said.

“On a day-to-day basis, these conditions can create congestion and affect system operators’ ability to respond to voltage support challenges, event response mitigation needs, or any additional grid stressors,” staff said in the report.

About 45% of the continental United States and 95% of the Intermountain West region is in a drought, according to the report.

In the Northeast, ISO New England lowered its capacity performance pay rate, which should lower electricity prices during emergency conditions or capacity performance events this winter, staff said.

Also, the New York Independent System Operator changed its installed capacity market rules to reflect uncertainty about how much power Hydro-Québec will deliver over the 1,250-MW Champlain Hudson Power Express transmission line in the winter, they noted.

The U.S. subsidiary of the Canadian utility Hydro-Québec — the primary reservation holder — has an obligation to serve native load in its Canadian territory, which is a winterpeaking system.

This obligation, along with ongoing reduced runoff to its hydroelectric resources, “has left little or no surplus capacity to export to NYISO in recent years’ peak winter months,” staff said.

The Northeast — especially New England — also faces risks around its reliance on oil-fired power in the winter, according to the report.

On Jan. 25, for example, oil-fired generation reached 7,864 MW in a single hour, supplying about 40% of ISO-NE’s total net generation in that period, staff noted.

However, U.S. and regional distillate fuel supplies are low, according to the report. New England’s supplies were 46% below the prior five-year average as of early October, staff said.

FERC expects natural gas prices will be lower this winter compared to last winter across most of the U.S., in part because of higher storage inventories in some areas and forecasts for warmer weather caused by El Niño, according to the report.

As of Nov. 10, Henry Hub gas prices — a key U.S. benchmark — were trading at $3.19 per million British thermal units for the upcoming winter, 42% below last winter’s settled price of $5.52/MMBtu, staff said.

FERC staff expects power plant gas demand to increase by 0.8 billion cubic feet per day, or about 2%, to 35.7 Bcf/d on average this winter compared to last winter. Power burn is expected to account for 25% of total gas demand this winter, according to the report.

#5752. Sierra Club Submits Comments Urging SEC to Preserve Shareholder Rights & Climate Accountability

WASHINGTON, D.C. — On Thursday, Sierra Club submitted two comment letters opposing the Securities and Exchange Commission’s (SEC) proposal to rescind Rule 14a-8, which allows shareholders to put proposals addressing climate risks and other corporate issues before fellow investors for a vote, and amend Rule 14a-4(c), which governs how companies may exercise discretionary voting authority over shareholder proposals. The Sierra Club submitted both a technical comment and a grassroots comment, reflecting concerns from over 1,100 of its members and supporters.

Together the submissions urge the SEC to withdraw its proposed changes and preserve investors’ ability to raise concerns directly with corporate management and press companies to address material risks, including climate-related risks, that can affect their long-term financial interests.

Jessye Waxman, Campaign Advisor on Sierra Club’s Sustainable Finance Campaign, released the following statement:

“Climate change and other systemic risks have real consequences for companies, investors, and the broader economy. Shareholder proposals give investors an important way to bring those risks to management’s attention, push for meaningful action, and hold companies accountable when they fail to act. The SEC’s proposals would weaken those tools, making it harder for investors to protect their long-term financial interests and leaving corporate management with less accountability for the risks its decisions create. The SEC’s proposals would shield corporate management from meaningful shareholder scrutiny, while undermining the very investor protections the Commission is charged with upholding.”

Rule 14a-8 establishes when public companies must include qualifying shareholder proposals in the materials they send investors ahead of shareholder meetings. The SEC’s proposed repeal would leave decisions about shareholder proposals to state law and individual companies’ governing documents, eliminating a federal framework that investors have used for more than 80 years.

Sierra Club’s technical comments explain that material risks, like climate change, can both have significant financial consequences for companies and create systemic financial risks that extend across investment portfolios and the broader economy. Shareholder proposals give investors a way to bring these concerns to company leadership and fellow shareholders, including by surfacing risks that management may overlook or have limited incentives to address. The letter emphasizes that, for decades, this process has served the broader market by helping investors collectively identify and address financially material risks.

The letter warns that replacing a consistent federal framework with varying state laws and corporate bylaws could make shareholder engagement less predictable and push disputes over proposals into costly litigation. It points to the increase in shareholder lawsuits challenging proposal exclusions during the 2026 proxy season as an indication that disputes do not disappear when the SEC steps back from administering the process; instead, they can shift to more costly and uncertain avenues.

The submission also opposes proposed amendments to Rule 14a-4(c), which would expand company management’s discretionary authority to vote shareholders’ proxies on proposals excluded from company proxy materials, unless shareholders affirmatively opt out. Sierra Club’s letter warns that these changes could allow management to vote on proposals that investors have not had an adequate opportunity to evaluate.

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5751. Six new ways to simplify solar installs: Top Rack Technology brings mounting innovation to RE+ 2026

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 18, 2026

From smarter waterproofing to 360-degree rail adjustment, Top Rack Technology is putting installer efficiency, rooftop flexibility, and cost control at the center of its latest product lineup.

As the solar industry continues to look for ways to reduce installation costs and streamline residential projects, mounting hardware is getting a fresh dose of innovation. Top Rack Technology, Inc. (TRT) is making that case at RE+ 2026, where the solar racking manufacturer is showcasing six new mounting solutions designed to simplify rooftop installations, improve flexibility, and help installers work more efficiently.

Its latest lineup reflects a practical approach to product development: addressing everyday installation challenges while keeping system performance, aesthetics, and affordability in focus.

Six Products, One Common Goal: Better Installation Efficiency

Among the new offerings is TRT’s Full-Coverage Skirt System, which targets an important consideration in residential solar: appearance. By concealing exposed array edges, the system delivers a cleaner, more finished look that can help installations better complement residential properties.

The new TRT-G2 Bracket takes aim at installation simplicity and competitive pricing. Designed around practical field requirements, the bracket can be paired with TRT’s ButyLok waterproofing component, creating an integrated attachment and roof-sealing solution.

Waterproofing is particularly important in rooftop solar, where roof penetrations are still a critical consideration for installers and property owners alike. ButyLok introduces a shoe-like component containing pre-applied, gel-like butyl material. When compressed by the G2 bracket, the material spreads across the intended sealing surface, potentially reducing manual sealant application and simplifying the installation process.

TRT is also introducing SAVRIX, another residential attachment option developed with manufacturing efficiency, installation practicality, and cost competitiveness top of mind.. Meanwhile, the RF-2 Rail Fastener is designed to simplify rail-to-bracket connections.

Top Rack Technology’s FreePivot Bracket features a 360-degree rotatable rail fastener and built-in height adjustment to accommodate varying rooftop conditions and simplify solar panel installations. Credit: Top Rack Technology

But perhaps the most versatile addition is the FreePivot Bracket. Its 360-degree rotatable rail fastener allows installers to accommodate attachment points that aren’t perfectly aligned. Built-in height adjustment helps compensate for uneven roof surfaces, while its all-metal construction supports durability. The one-orientation design and single-socket installation concept are also intended to reduce tool changes and keep crews moving.

Beyond the six new products, TRT is showcasing its established portfolio, including the TRT 01 Rail-less Base System, TRT 02 Rail-Base System, and TRT 03 Tile Hook System.

The company is also highlighting its Online BOM Design Tool, which supports project setup, structural design inputs, module layouts, engineering verification, and automated bill-of-materials generation. Connecting digital planning with physical mounting solutions could offer installers and distributors a more streamlined path from project design to field execution.

Installation hardware doesn’t need to be flashy to make a difference. Improvements in attachment flexibility, waterproofing, assembly time, and aesthetics can translate into meaningful operational advantages.
